### [1] [Hierarchical Belief Modeling for Zero-Shot Opponent Adaptation in Partially Observable Multi-Agent Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12422)
*Kowei Shih,Lu Cheng,Zeyu Wang,Yeyun Xu,Kejian Tong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HORIZON is a hierarchical AI agent for Lux AI Season 3 that combines spatial perception, belief tracking, and opponent-conditioned policies to excel in partially observable environments with randomized dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lux AI Season 3 presents challenges including partial observability, randomized episode dynamics, and a best-of-five match structure requiring both tactical execution and fast adaptation to opponent strategies.

Method: Hierarchical architecture combining symmetry-aware spatial perception, dual memory belief tracking, relic-centric graph attention, information gain driven exploration, and opponent-conditioned policy mixture. Separates short-horizon control from cross-match meta-reasoning, with auxiliary belief and world model objectives. Trained with PPO in JAX simulator.

Result: Consistent gains in match win rate, episode win rate, adaptation gain, and league rating over strong recurrent and feed-forward baselines.

Conclusion: HORIZON successfully addresses the challenges of Lux AI Season 3 through hierarchical reasoning, explicit inference of hidden game parameters and opponent style, and stable learning with auxiliary objectives.

Abstract: Lux AI Season 3 requires agents to act under partial observability, randomized episode level dynamics, and a best of five match structure that rewards both tactical execution and fast adaptation. We present HORIZON, a hierarchical agent that combines symmetry aware spatial perception, dual memory belief tracking, relic centric graph attention, information gain driven exploration, and an opponent conditioned policy mixture. HORIZON separates short horizon control from cross match meta reasoning, while auxiliary belief and world model objectives stabilize learning. Trained with PPO in a large scale JAX simulator, the resulting agent explicitly infers hidden game parameters and opponent style. Experiments show consistent gains in match win rate, episode win rate, adaptation gain, and league rating over strong recurrent and feed forward baselines.

</details>


### [2] [What Drives Recovery in Agentic Text-to-Cypher? LAST-CQ: An LLM Agent Self-Refinement Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12746)
*Ioannis Prokopiou,Athanasios Aidinis,Panagiotis-Christos Kyrmpatsos,Pantelis Vikatos*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agentic text-to-Cypher pipelines gain performance primarily from failure detection and retry routing, not from sophisticated feedback or parallel sampling, with database error strings being as effective as LLM-synthesized feedback.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As agentic pipelines for structured-query generation expand, it's unclear which components drive performance gains, particularly in the loop components like feedback sophistication, correction mechanisms, and sampling strategies.

Method: Used LAST-CQ (a five-agent, training-free, execution-grounded Text-to-Cypher framework) as a testbed, running three counterfactual experiments over 2,471 live-database queries with six LLM backbones across three vendor scale tiers, including removing correction, replacing feedback types, and comparing parallel sampling.

Result: Correction removal reduces performance by 3.1-12.3%; raw database errors perform as well as LLM-synthesized feedback (<0.2% difference); parallel sampling degrades quality by 10-11%; LAST-CQ recovers 91.7% of failed single-pass queries while maintaining efficiency for successful queries; n-gram overlap scoring shows directional biases; LLM judges are optimistic by 9 points.

Conclusion: The key mechanism that works is detecting failure and routing to retry, not feedback sophistication or number of samples, suggesting simpler, more efficient agentic query generation pipelines can achieve similar or better performance.

Abstract: Agentic pipelines for structured-query generation are rapidly expanding, but it is unclear which part of the loop produces the gain. We use LAST-CQ -- a five-agent, training-free, execution-grounded Text-to-Cypher framework -- as an instrumented testbed, running three counterfactuals over 2,471 live-database queries and six backbones spanning three vendor scale tiers. Removing correction is worth between 3.1% aggregate execution-BLEU against the single-pass system and 12.3% against a no-refinement counterfactual (up to 80.7% for the weakest backbone). Replacing schema-grounded, LLM-synthesised feedback with raw database error strings costs almost nothing (20.9% vs. 19.9% naive exact match; <0.2% end-to-end; equivalent within $\pm 0.075$ set-F1 by two one-sided tests). Spending the same call budget on parallel sampling degrades quality by 10-11%. What works is detecting failure and routing it to a retry, not the feedback sophistication or number of samples. LAST-CQ itself recovers 91.7% of queries that fail under single-pass generation, while a query that succeeds first time still costs exactly one LLM call. We also show that n-gram overlap on serialised results is not a bound in either direction: it over-scores against set equivalence on 65.9% of results while under-scoring against judged semantics. Finally, we calibrate our LLM judge against blind human labels and find it optimistic by 9 points.

</details>


### [3] [Occamy-1.0: Open Pareto-frontier 35B Intelligence for Co-work](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11977)
*Wenhui Chen,Shiwen Cheng,Hao Dong,Chenda Duan,Ruixiang Feng,Zhong Guan,Boqiang Guo,Xueyuan Han,Haojie Hao,Liangmeng Huang,Zhelong Huang,Xinke Kong,Hongyu Li,Jiazheng Li,Junbo Li,Qingchuan Li,Yukun Lian,Chang Liu,Tianyu Liu,Zicheng Liu,Shuyi Ouyang,Yijun Pan,Kunyu Shi,Xiaojun Tang,Bingquan Wang,Kesu Wang,Yuchen Wang,Sibo Wei,Sicong Xie,Xiaoying Xing,Yi Xu,Zhijun Xu,Hongwei Xue,Qingcheng Zeng,Di Zhang,Guannan Zhang,Haochen Zhang,Tianlong Zhang,Tianyu Zhao,Tianyu Zhao,Yanjun Zheng,Jialong Zhu,Zijian Zou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Occamy-1.0 is a cost-efficient co-work AI model developed from Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, specialized for practical agent workflows through execution-grounded training while maintaining broad agentic capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Co-work agents perform complex workflows involving information gathering, tool use, coding, and file manipulation across multiple model invocations. The practical value of these agents depends on cost and latency efficiency, not just peak capability. Many everyday work steps emphasize state tracking, coordination, recovery, and follow-through rather than frontier-scale reasoning, creating a need for more efficient models.

Method: The authors developed Occamy-1.0 by further training the post-trained Qwen3.6-35B-A3B checkpoint. They constructed execution-grounded data and environments, captured replayable long-horizon trajectories across multiple harnesses, and used staged post-training to develop and consolidate complementary execution capabilities.

Result: Occamy-1.0 performs consistently among the strongest comparably sized models across a broad suite of co-work benchmarks and remains competitive with substantially larger frontier systems on several tasks. Under the evaluation and pricing protocol, its aggregate performance across four representative benchmarks places it at the low-cost knee of the cost-performance Pareto frontier. The model preserves broad agentic capability in tool calling, coding, and instruction following.

Conclusion: Occamy-1.0 demonstrates that specialized co-work models can achieve high performance efficiently, positioning at the optimal cost-performance trade-off point. The authors release model weights and training data subsets to support research on practical co-work agents and agentic post-training.

Abstract: Co-work agents execute complex workflows that combine information gathering, tool use, coding, and file manipulation across many model invocations. Because cost and latency accumulate over the full episode, their practical value depends not only on peak capability but also on how efficiently that capability is delivered. Yet many steps in everyday work emphasize state tracking, coordination, recovery, and follow-through rather than frontier-scale reasoning. We present Occamy-1.0, a cost-efficient co-work model obtained by further training the post-trained Qwen3.6-35B-A3B checkpoint. We construct execution-grounded data and environments, capture replayable long-horizon trajectories across multiple harnesses, and use staged post-training to develop and consolidate complementary execution capabilities. Across a broad suite of co-work benchmarks, Occamy-1.0 is consistently among the strongest comparably sized models and remains competitive with substantially larger frontier systems on several tasks. Under our stated evaluation and pricing protocol, its aggregate performance across four representative benchmarks places it at the low-cost knee of the observed cost--performance Pareto frontier. Supporting evaluations in tool calling, coding, and instruction following further show that this specialization preserves broad agentic capability. We release the model weights and a subset of the training data to support research on practical co-work agents and agentic post-training.

</details>


### [4] [Harness or Model? Isolating the Harness Effect in Agentic Coding with a Contamination-Controlled Private Suite](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11987)
*Mohsen Arjmandi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper compares vendor-native vs neutral harnesses for LLM coding agents, finding no average advantage for either across models, but revealing cost differences and task-type stratification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to test the assumption that vendor-native harnesses (custom tools, prompts, and control flow) outperform neutral harnesses when paired with their own models. Practitioners often assume vendor-native pairings solve more tasks, but this assumption hasn't been rigorously tested.

Method: The study uses paired same-model contrasts on 256 contamination-controlled tasks (repository and contest tasks). It tests Claude Opus 4.8 under claude-agent-sdk vs deepagents, and GPT-5.5 under openai-codex SDK vs deepagents. 800 planned runs were graded by an isolated oracle. Analysis includes bootstrap confidence intervals, cost calculations from raw per-turn usage, and examination of task-type stratification.

Result: No average advantage for either harness type: -1.25pp for Opus 4.8 and +1.25pp for GPT-5.5, both statistically insignificant. However, task-type stratification revealed opposite effects: native harness trailed by 9.0pp on repository tasks but led by 23.7pp on contest tasks for Opus. Cost analysis showed neutral harness cost 1.3-1.6x more per solved task on Opus and 1.2x more on GPT-5.5.

Conclusion: The assumption that vendor-native harnesses inherently outperform neutral ones is not supported by evidence. Task type matters significantly, with contest tasks favoring native harnesses. Cost differences exist but billing records were incomplete. The paper emphasizes methodological rigor and corrects previous cost calculation errors, releasing tools and analysis code while keeping tasks private.

Abstract: An agentic coding system couples a language model to a harness: the tools, prompts and control flow that turn a chat model into an autonomous software engineer. Vendors ship harnesses tuned to their own models, and practitioners assume the vendor-native pairing solves more tasks. We measure that assumption with paired same-model contrasts on a private, contamination-controlled suite of 256 repository and post-cutoff contest tasks. The same 80 tasks ran under claude-agent-sdk and under deepagents on claude-opus-4-8, and under the openai-codex SDK and deepagents on gpt-5.5, with gemini-3.5-flash and deepseek-v3.2 as side cells. 792 of 800 planned runs were graded by an isolated oracle. Neither contrast resolves an average advantage for either harness: -1.25 pp for Opus 4.8 (48.8% vs 50.0%, task-bootstrap 95% CI [-10.0, +7.5]) and +1.25 pp for GPT-5.5 (55.6% vs 54.4%, CI [-4.4, +6.9]). The Opus average combines opposite strata: the native harness trails by 9.0 pp on the 61 repository tasks and leads by 23.7 pp on the 19 contest tasks (label-permutation p = 0.003). The partition was chosen after seeing the data and needs a designed replication. Correctness and completion also separate: 22 of 81 runs cancelled at the wall-clock ceiling had produced a passing patch. Re-priced from raw per-turn usage at frozen list prices, the neutral harness cost 1.3 to 1.6 times as much per solved task on Opus 4.8 and 1.2 times on GPT-5.5. These are observed-usage estimates. On the Anthropic account 58 runs left no usage record, and allocating that spend to either cell would move the Opus ratio between 0.7 and 2.3, so the billed ordering is unresolved. This revision corrects an August 2026 manuscript whose cost figures rested on a usage-semantics defect in our own telemetry (Section 5.1). We release the orchestrator, grading oracle, reanalysis code and derived aggregates. The tasks stay private.

</details>


### [5] [Reading the Whole Heart: Latent-Attention Masked Autoencoders for Multimodal Cardiac Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12035)
*Andrea Agostini,Simon Böhi,Moritz Vandenhirtz,Samuel Ruiperez-Campillo,Max Krähenmann,Silke Mühlstedt,Irene Cannistraci,Ece Özkan Elsen,Julia E. Vogt,Thomas M. Sutter*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LAMAE is a multimodal masked autoencoder that jointly learns patient representations through latent-attention on a study-view-entity hierarchy, outperforming modality-specific and contrastive baselines on hospital-stay tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current medical foundation models are mostly modality-specific, missing the natural cross-modal integration clinicians use and ignoring structural relationships within each modality. This prevents optimal utilization of complementary diagnostic information.

Method: LAMAE uses a masked autoencoder approach with a shared latent-attention module operating over a study-view-entity hierarchy. It exchanges information directly in latent space during self-supervised pretraining, enabling aggregation of variable observations and handling of missing modalities.

Result: Pretrained on 1.2 million MIMIC-IV hospital stays, LAMAE outperforms modality-specific pretraining and strong contrastive/vision-language baselines across multimodal tasks (in-hospital mortality, ICD-10/DRG coding, length of stay) while remaining competitive on unimodal tasks. Gains persist even with single modality at test time.

Conclusion: Modeling both intra- and inter-modal structure through latent-attention yields more robust, transferable patient representations that capture cross-modal evidence clinicians naturally integrate, leading to better performance across diagnostic tasks.

Abstract: Cardiovascular diagnosis rests on integrating complementary modalities, like ECG, echocardiography, chest radiographs, and clinical variables, each capturing distinct but correlated aspects of cardiac physiology. Yet most medical foundation models remain modality-specific, combining modalities only for finetuning or post-training. This discards the cross-modal evidence clinicians naturally integrate and ignores the structure within each modality. We introduce Latent-Attention Masked Autoencoders (LAMAE), a multimodal, structure-aware masked autoencoder that jointly learns patient-level representations during self-supervised pretraining. Rather than fusing modalities post hoc, LAMAE exchanges information directly in the latent space through a shared latent-attention module operating over a study-view-entity hierarchy, enabling aggregation of variable observations and graceful handling of missing modalities. Pretrained on over 1.2 million MIMIC-IV hospital stays, LAMAE outperforms modality-specific pretraining and strong contrastive and vision-language baselines across multimodal hospital-stay tasks, such as in-hospital mortality, ICD-10 and DRG coding, and length of stay, while remaining competitive on unimodal tasks. These gains persist even when only a single modality is available at test time, showing that modeling both intra- and inter-modal structure yields more robust, transferable representations.

</details>


### [6] [Competence-Gated Pooling of Language Models and Priors for Event Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12101)
*Aditi Tiwari,Aashrith Bandaru,Heng Ji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A competence gate system that selectively integrates language model forecasts with external forecasts based on measured marginal value, improving accuracy over global pooling and external baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In hybrid forecasting, language models are often one of several signals, and systems need to determine whether a model adds useful information beyond existing external forecasts (market, crowd, or statistical). The target isn't standalone model accuracy but relative competence - the model's marginal value beyond available external forecasts.

Method: Under Brier loss, characterize when model disagreement improves external forecasts; derive gain from domain-specific pooling weights. Introduce a competence gate that: 1) estimates domain-level source weights from resolved outcomes, 2) shrinks uncertain estimates toward global weight, and 3) recalibrates the pooled forecast.

Result: Across 2,357 resolved binary questions and five language models, the gate improves main external baseline from 0.0771 to 0.0732 Brier score and significantly outperforms global forecast combinations. Gains remain significant under leakage controls. On ForecastBench market subset, gate largely defers to market. Across four Qwen models, verbal confidence doesn't reliably identify when model outperforms external forecast, while outcome-estimated competence supports better abstention decisions.

Conclusion: Provides practical approach for selective model use based on measured marginal value. Shows that competence estimation from outcomes enables better forecasting decisions than verbal confidence or global pooling.

Abstract: In hybrid forecasting, a language model is often one of several available signals. A system may already have a market, crowd, or statistical forecast and must decide whether the model adds useful information or should be ignored. The relevant target is therefore not standalone model accuracy, but relative competence, defined as the model's marginal value beyond the available external forecast. Under Brier loss, we characterize when model disagreement can improve an external forecast and derive the gain from using domain-specific rather than global pooling weights. We then introduce a competence gate that estimates domain-level source weights from resolved outcomes, shrinks uncertain estimates toward a global weight, and recalibrates the pooled forecast. Across 2,357 resolved binary questions and five language models, the gate improves the main external baseline from 0.0771 to 0.0732 Brier and significantly outperforms global forecast combinations. The gain remains significant under leakage controls and against a leakage-safe time-series prior on the pooled structured set, with separate evidence on FRED. In contrast, the gate gives no significant improvement on the official ForecastBench market subset, where it largely defers to the market. Across four Qwen models, verbal confidence does not reliably identify when the model outperforms the external forecast, while outcome-estimated competence supports better abstention decisions. These results provide a practical approach for selective model use based on measured marginal value.

</details>


### [7] [Language Is an Insufficient Substrate for Quantitative Reasoning, and Consequential Domains Need Large Quantitative Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12105)
*Reuben Vandeventer,David Imrem,David J. Wild*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Language models are insufficient for consequential quantitative decisions due to irreversible information loss in descriptive representations; we need Large Quantitative Models with reproducibility, lineage, and calibrated uncertainty.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper challenges the prevailing assumption that progress in LLMs will naturally lead to progress in consequential quantitative decision-making domains like risk pricing, capital allocation, patient triage, and network intrusion detection.

Method: The authors formalize the fundamental limitation as a property of the representation rather than model capacity, identifying that language models are trained on lossy human descriptions that irreversibly discard quantitative information.

Result: They identify three essential properties for consequential settings that language substrates cannot provide: reproducibility, lineage (traceability from outputs to source records), and calibrated uncertainty, defining a new model class called Large Quantitative Models (LQMs).

Conclusion: Consequential quantitative decision-making requires a distinct class of models (LQMs) that operate on quantitative records directly rather than descriptive language representations, with built-in reproducibility, lineage tracking, and uncertainty calibration.

Abstract: The prevailing assumption in applied machine learning is that progress on consequential quantitative decisions such as pricing risk, allocating capital, triaging patients, or containing a network intrusion will follow from progress in large language models (LLMs). A language model is trained on a representation of the world that was produced by human description; description is a lossy encoding of the quantitative record, and the loss is irreversible: no downstream model, at any scale, can recover from a description what the description did not encode. We formalize this as a property of the representation on which a model is trained rather than of the model capacity, and we identify three further properties that consequential settings demand of a model and that a language substrate cannot supply by construction: reproducibility, lineage from every output back to the source records that produced. it, and calibrated uncertainty. We argue that these properties define a distinct model class, which we call the Large Quantitative Model (LQM).

</details>


### [8] [DU-NO: A Parameter-Efficient Double U-Shaped Neural Operator for Phase-Resolving Wave Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12115)
*Enrique Hernandez Noguera,Md Meftahul Ferdaus,Nathan Cooper,Elias Ioup,Mahdi Abdelguerfi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DU-NO is a novel neural operator architecture that achieves state-of-the-art wave modeling accuracy with 10.8x fewer parameters than leading baselines by combining spectral and convolutional branches only at fine grid levels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Phase-resolving wave models like FUNWAVE-TVD are accurate but computationally expensive for operational forecasting, ensembles, and uncertainty quantification. While neural operators promise similar accuracy at lower cost, existing accurate models are parameter-heavy, making them impractical for many applications.

Method: DU-NO (Double U-shaped Neural Operator) is a multiscale U-shaped spectral operator that attaches lightweight convolutional U-Net branches only at the two shallowest encoder and decoder levels. This placement follows a sampling argument: high-wavenumber content exists only on fine grids, so local pathways go where that content lives, while coarse levels stay purely spectral. A depth-decaying mode schedule keeps parameters low.

Result: DU-NO achieves best autoregressive rollout error among six architectures on FUNWAVE-TVD benchmark, improving on U-FNO by 14.9% with 10.8x fewer parameters (only 3.64M). Frequency-band analysis shows gains across all bands, including high-wavenumber bands where truncated-spectral operators fail. Parameter-matched controls show architectural superiority (best baseline trails by 28.6% at same budget).

Conclusion: DU-NO offers an efficient neural operator architecture for wave modeling that significantly reduces parameter count while improving accuracy, demonstrating architectural superiority over existing approaches. The advantages extend beyond nearshore waves to other PDE domains like Navier-Stokes and shallow-water equations.

Abstract: Phase-resolving wave models such as FUNWAVE-TVD are the accuracy standard for nearshore dynamics, resolving the shoaling, refraction, and breaking of individual waves, but their cost rules them out for the ensembles, uncertainty quantification, and real-time warning that operational forecasting demands. Neural operators promise solver-level accuracy at a fraction of that cost, yet on wave-dominated fields the accurate ones are large: hybrid spectral-convolutional operators such as U-FNO (the strongest baseline in our study after DU-NO) buy their fidelity with tens of millions of parameters. We introduce DU-NO (Double U-shaped Neural Operator), a multiscale U-shaped spectral operator that attaches lightweight convolutional U-Net branches only at its two shallowest encoder and decoder levels. The placement follows a sampling argument: high-wavenumber content exists only on fine grids, so the local, full-band pathways go where that content lives, while the coarse, band-limited levels stay purely spectral. A depth-decaying mode schedule holds the model to 3.64M parameters, an order of magnitude below U-FNO. On our publicly released FUNWAVE-TVD benchmark, DU-NO attains the best autoregressive rollout error of six identically trained architectures, improving on U-FNO by 14.9% with 10.8x fewer parameters, and a frequency-band analysis shows the gain holds across all bands, including the high-wavenumber band where truncated-spectral operators collapse. Parameter-matched controls confirm the gain is architectural: rescaled to the same 3.6M budget, the best baseline still trails DU-NO by 28.6%. The advantage carries beyond nearshore waves: DU-NO matches the strongest baselines on 2D Navier-Stokes and wins clearly on PDEBench shallow-water rollouts. Code, trained models, and evaluation artifacts are available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/duno-code-5A7B/.

</details>


### [9] [When Successful Knowledge Graph Edits Displace Correct Answers: Rank-Level Locality beyond Parameter Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12116)
*Yi-Cheng Lai,Jerry Wang,Hsin-Ling Hsu,Li-Chu Chi,Ya-Wen Teng,Hen-Hsen Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: KGE editing for promoting specific answers often causes rank displacement of other correct answers; the paper introduces rank-displacement auditing and demonstrates trade-offs between correction success and preservation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current knowledge graph embedding editing methods focus on locality tests based only on facts that reuse edited parameters, but these can miss ranking effects where correct answers get displaced from returned lists when promoting a desired answer.

Method: Introduces a rank-displacement audit across three scopes: facts using edited parameters, other correct answers to target query, and correct answers across queries with same relation. Develops dimensional/geometric conditions for updates that improve target while preserving selected scores. Tests various editing mechanisms (direct promotion, strict preservation, support-regularized entity editing, rank-truncated preservation) on FB15k-237 with DistMult and ComplEx.

Result: Direct promotion moves target into top ten but causes damage in 76.8-77% of cases. Strict preservation causes no measured damage but succeeds in only 1.3-1.4%. Support-regularized entity editing gives highest joint success (36.3-37.7%), while rank-truncated preservation reaches 32.8-34.7% and reduces mean displaced answers from ~14 to 1.2. Locality depends on both protected scope and editing mechanism.

Conclusion: KGE editing should report correction success together with incidence and severity of rank displacement. There's a fundamental trade-off between achieving the desired edit and preserving other correct rankings, requiring careful consideration of editing mechanisms and scope of preservation.

Abstract: Editing a knowledge graph embedding (KGE) model to promote a desired answer can displace correct answers from the returned list. Locality tests based only on facts that reuse the edited parameter can miss this ranking effect. We introduce a common rank-displacement audit at three scopes: facts supported by the edited parameter, other correct answers to the target query, and correct answers across queries with the same relation. We also derive dimensional and geometric conditions for an update to improve the target while exactly preserving selected scores. On FB15k-237 with DistMult and ComplEx, direct promotion always moves the target into the top ten, but does so without damage in only 23.0--23.2\% of edits. Strict preservation causes no measured damage, yet succeeds in only 1.3--1.4\%. Support-regularized entity editing gives the highest joint success, 36.3--37.7\%, while rank-truncated preservation reaches 32.8--34.7\% and reduces the mean number of displaced answers from about 14 to 1.2. Experiments across dimensions, scorers, ranking conventions, and a learned editor show that locality depends on both the protected scope and the editing mechanism. KGE editing should therefore report correction success together with the incidence and severity of rank displacement.

</details>


### [10] [Mined from Scientific Literature: Process Schemas for Atomic Layer Deposition and Etching in Materials Science](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12139)
*Sameer Sadruddin,Eleni Poupaki,Alex Watkins,Bora Karasulu,Adriaan J. M. Mackus,Erwin Kessels,Sören Auer,Jennifer D'Souza*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Researchers developed four domain-expert-reviewed JSON Schemas for ALD and ALE experimental and simulation processes to standardize reporting and enable machine-actionable data reuse across literature.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Atomic layer deposition (ALD) and atomic layer etching (ALE) data is reported heterogeneously across experimental and simulation literature in materials science, which hinders comparison between studies and prevents machine-actionable reuse of the data.

Method: The authors present four domain-expert-reviewed JSON Schemas for ALD and ALE experimental and simulation processes. The schemas were curated using schema-miner and grounded in QUDT (Quantities, Units, Dimensions and Types) using schema-miner pro. The schemas structure materials, process conditions, configurations, and measured or predicted results.

Result: The paper compares the scope, structure, and semantic grounding of the developed schemas. The authors demonstrate practical applications by showing how these schemas can be used for schema-guided literature extraction and publication of structured records through ORKG (Open Research Knowledge Graph) templates.

Conclusion: The developed JSON Schemas provide a standardized framework for reporting ALD and ALE processes, enabling better comparison across studies and facilitating machine-actionable data reuse in materials science research.

Abstract: Atomic layer deposition (ALD) and atomic layer etching (ALE) are reported heterogeneously across experimental and simulation literature in materials science, hindering comparison and machine-actionable reuse. We present four domain-expert-reviewed JSON Schemas for ALD and ALE experimental and simulation processes. Curated with schema-miner and grounded in QUDT using schema-miner pro, the schemas structure materials, process conditions, configurations , and measured or predicted results. We compare their scope, structure, and semantic grounding, and demonstrate their use for schema-guided literature extraction and publication of structured records through ORKG templates.

</details>


### [11] [Can LLMs in Draft-Verify-Revise Pipelines Resolve Deictic Ambiguity?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12162)
*Obinna I. Ekekezie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper examines deictic shifts in draft-verify-revise LLM pipelines where context-dependent expressions like "previous" can be interpreted differently by different models, causing referential ambiguity and affecting pipeline accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In draft-verify-revise LLM orchestration patterns, context cascades between stages can cause context-dependent expressions to be resolved differently by different LLMs (deictic shifts), potentially undermining the effectiveness of multi-stage inference pipelines.

Method: Researchers created a synthetic dataset of 10 base examples with three conditions varying which stage LLM resolves expressions correctly. They tested six models from three providers across 21 reasoning effort configurations using e-values for sequential testing, with primary and ablation experiments. The meta-evaluator's rationales for wrong verdicts were separately analyzed.

Result: Balanced accuracy ranged from 0.156 (below chance) to near-perfect. GPT-5.2 improved from 0.156 without reasoning to 0.942 at highest reasoning effort, while Gemini 3 Pro stayed above 0.94 at every level. Gemini 3 Pro at low reasoning effort outperformed GPT-5.2 at xhigh reasoning effort for roughly 5% of the cost per trial. Erroneous meta-evaluators tended to rely on surface cues rather than operational reasoning.

Conclusion: Context engineers implementing draft-verify-revise pipelines should be aware of deictic shifts and make intended referents explicit at each stage to ensure proper resolution of context-dependent expressions across pipeline stages.

Abstract: Draft-verify-revise is a common LLM orchestration pattern for scaling inference-time compute. One LLM drafts, a second critiques the draft and provides feedback, and a third uses that feedback to revise the draft into the final output. As context cascades between stages, LLMs at different stages can resolve a context-dependent expression such as "previous" differently. When that happens, the expression undergoes a deictic shift, a change in what it refers to. This phenomenon was studied with a synthetic dataset of 10 base examples, each rendered in three conditions. Holding the shared components constant, the conditions varied whether the draft stage LLM (the assistant) or the verify stage LLM (the grader) resolved the expression correctly, and how much independent reasoning the revise stage LLM (the meta-evaluator) needed to determine which reading was correct. Six models from three providers were tested across 21 reasoning effort configurations using e-values for sequential testing, in a primary experiment and an ablation experiment that removed error classification labels from the grader's feedback. A separate LLM analyzed the meta-evaluator's stated rationale for each wrong verdict. Balanced accuracy (the unweighted mean of sensitivity and specificity) ranged from 0.156, below chance, to near-perfect. GPT-5.2 rose from 0.156 without reasoning to 0.942 at its highest reasoning effort level, while Gemini 3 Pro stayed above 0.94 at every level. Gemini 3 Pro at low reasoning effort outscored GPT-5.2 at xhigh reasoning effort for roughly 5% of the cost per trial. When the meta-evaluator erred, it tended to rely on surface cues rather than operational reasoning. Context engineers implementing draft-verify-revise pipelines should be wary of deictic shifts and make the intended referent explicit at each stage.

</details>


### [12] [GLARE: Generative Learning via Adversarial Reward Estimation For Social Dynamics Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12165)
*Tenghao Huang,Zhaoxuan Tan,Muhao Chen,Jonathan May,Mengting Wan,Longqi Yang,Pei Zhou,Sihao Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MDFB benchmark for forecasting meeting continuations with utility and human-likeness metrics, plus GLARE method using adversarial imitation learning that outperforms baselines but falls short of human performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need to track complex meeting dynamics (agenda, roles, intentions, disagreements) across long multi-party discussions, requiring methods to forecast plausible continuations rather than just reproducing observed text.

Method: Introduce MDFB benchmark with 2,207 real meetings and 24,794 queries; develop GLARE method using adversarial imitation learning where discriminator ranks observed continuations above model samples, providing KL-regularized policy reward with retraining on current negatives.

Result: GLARE achieves average human-evaluated win rates of 0.66 on utility and 0.70 on human-likeness, outperforming SFT and SPIN baselines while remaining below observed human performance; MDFB also serves as social reasoning benchmark for model comparison.

Conclusion: MDFB provides valuable benchmark for both task-specific learning and output-based evaluation of meeting behavior forecasting, demonstrating GLARE's effectiveness through adversarial imitation learning while highlighting room for improvement compared to humans.

Abstract: Meeting continuation requires tracking the agenda, speaker roles, participant intentions, and disagreement across long multi-party discussions. We introduce the Meeting Dynamic Forecasting Benchmark (MDFB), constructed from 2,207 real-world meetings and 24,794 future-facing queries. Given a transcript prefix and an active question, a model generates a plausible multi-turn continuation in one call. We evaluate utility---progress toward the question---and human-likeness---plausible conversational flow and role consistency---without requiring exact reproduction of the observed future. We further present GLARE, an adaptation of adversarial imitation learning to conditional language generation. A discriminator ranks the observed continuation above samples from the current actor, and its score supplies a KL-regularized policy reward; retraining on current-policy negatives allows the reward landscape to evolve with the actor. GLARE attains average human-evaluated win rates of 0.66 on utility and 0.70 on human-likeness, outperforming SFT and SPIN while remaining below the observed human continuation. We also demonstrate MDFB as a social reasoning arena for comparing general-purpose models, including closed-source systems, through reference-assisted judgments. Together, these studies illustrate the benchmark's use for both task-specific learning and output-based evaluation of meeting behavior.

</details>


### [13] [WinSyn: An Automated Pipeline for Realistic Enterprise Question-Answering Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12171)
*Amey Varhade,Ananya Sutradhar,Ravishankar Krishnaswamy,Navin Goyal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Automated pipeline creates synthetic enterprise email datasets with realistic workplace scenarios to benchmark question-answering agents, revealing current methods still struggle with enterprise complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise question-answering faces unique challenges due to complex, evolving data spread across emails, chats, and documents. Existing benchmarks lack real-world complexity, short-form responses, and unnatural queries, failing to capture enterprise challenges.

Method: Introduced an automated pipeline for generating synthetic email datasets reflecting realistic workplace scenarios, simulating long-running enterprise projects spanning months with up to 25 interacting employees across roles. The data emphasizes ambiguity, distributed information, and natural queries.

Result: Evaluation of standard agentic baselines using frontier models shows aggregate scores below 80% for each dataset, indicating significant room for improvement. The pipeline successfully creates high-complexity evaluation data that reveals current system limitations.

Conclusion: More work is needed for enterprise deployment of question-answering systems, and realistic, high-complexity evaluation data is crucial for developing stronger real-world enterprise deep research systems.

Abstract: Enterprise settings provide a challenging environment for question-answering agents, which often rely on Retrieval-Augmented Generation, Deep Research (DR), and related techniques. Much of this challenge comes from the complexity of enterprise data: information is often spread across evolving and potentially conflict- ing emails, chat messages, documents, and other artifacts. Existing benchmarks typically have limited real-world complexity, short-form responses, and unnatural queries, so they often fail to capture the challenges of enterprise settings. In this work, we introduce an automated pipeline for generating synthetic datasets of emails reflecting realistic workplace scenarios, along with long- and short-form questions and gold answers grounded in the data. Our method simulates long-running enterprise projects spanning several months and involving up to 25 interacting employees across multiple roles. The data emphasizes ambiguity, distributed information, and naturally occurring queries. To validate the pipeline, we evaluate few standard agentic baselines on our datasets using the latest frontier models. We find that aggregate scores averaged over all queries remain below 80% for each dataset, indicating significant room for improvement. These findings suggest that more work remains to be done for enterprise deployment and underscore the importance of realistic, high-complexity evaluation data for developing stronger real-world enterprise DR systems.

</details>


### [14] [Soft Symbol Grounding for Prototypical Concepts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12247)
*Marcos Galván-López,Nijesh Upreti,Hiram Calvo,Carlos Aguilar-Ibáñez,Vaishak Belle*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Soft-PNet is a neuro-symbolic model that reduces reasoning shortcuts by using a Metropolis walk over feasible symbolic solutions guided by prototype distributions, eliminating the need for hand-crafted task-specific losses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neuro-symbolic models trained only on final labels can suffer from reasoning shortcuts - predicting labels correctly while recovering wrong intermediate concepts. Existing prototypical networks require hand-crafted, task-specific differentiable losses that must be redesigned for each task.

Method: Soft-PNet reframes concept grounding as a Metropolis walk over a precomputed cache of feasible symbolic solutions, guided by a prototype distribution built from a single labeled anchor per concept. It trains against a single KL objective between the prototype-weighted cache and the network's concept predictions.

Result: On MNIST-EvenOdd, Visual Sudoku, and Kand-Logic under scarce supervision, Soft-PNet matches loss-engineered prototypical networks at concept and label levels, recovers concepts that soft-grounding baselines miss, with no loss engineering and lower training time.

Conclusion: Soft-PNet provides a task-agnostic approach to neuro-symbolic learning that eliminates the need for hand-crafted losses while effectively reducing reasoning shortcuts and recovering correct intermediate concepts with minimal supervision.

Abstract: Neuro-symbolic models are usually trained with supervision only on final labels, leaving the intermediate concepts unobserved. Since many concept assignments are consistent with a given label, training can predict labels correctly while recovering the wrong concepts, a failure known as a reasoning shortcut. Prototypical networks reduce shortcuts by anchoring each concept to a few labeled examples, but existing methods still couple perception and reasoning through a hand-crafted, task-specific differentiable loss that must be redesigned for every task. We introduce \textbf{Soft-PNet}, which removes this loss: it reframes concept grounding as a Metropolis walk over a precomputed cache of feasible symbolic solutions, guided by a prototype distribution built from a single labeled anchor per concept, and trains against one KL objective between the prototype-weighted cache and the network's concept predictions. The objective is identical across tasks and remains applicable when the solution space cannot be enumerated. On \texttt{MNIST-EvenOdd}, Visual Sudoku, and \texttt{Kand-Logic} under scarce supervision, Soft-PNet matches loss-engineered prototypical networks at the concept and label levels and recovers concepts that soft-grounding baselines miss, with no loss engineering and lower training time.

</details>


### [15] [GTA: Graph Theory Agent and Benchmark for Algorithmic Graph Reasoning with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12265)
*Zixiang Xu,Yanbo Wang,Chenxi Wang,Lang Gao,Zirui Song,Yue Huang,Zhaorun Chen,Xiangliang Zhang,Xiuying Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GT Bench introduces a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating LLMs on graph reasoning tasks across multiple representations, revealing representation sensitivity and proposing GTA - an agent that selects optimal representations and scaffolds reasoning to improve performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of LLMs on graph reasoning have limitations: they use simple tasks on small graphs, focus on code generation rather than direct graph reasoning, or fix a single input format, making it unclear how reliably LLMs can perform multi-step graph algorithms.

Method: Created Graph Theory Bench (GT Bench) covering 24 classical graph problems in 44 task-structure settings with over 100,000 examples across four representations (natural language, structured language, adjacency list, adjacency matrix). Evaluated 8 LLMs on this benchmark and proposed Graph Theory Agent (GTA) which pairs a preference-trained representation selector with plan-and-decompose scaffolding around a frozen executor LLM.

Result: Accuracy is strongly tied to input representation, with the best representation shifting with graph density, size, topology, and model type. This sensitivity persists, though attenuated, in strongest reasoning models. GTA lifts Phi-4 from 53.5% to 69.1% on easy split and from 33.0% to 41.5% on hard split, outperforming eight prompting and agent baselines, and transfers without retraining to other benchmarks.

Conclusion: LLMs' performance on graph reasoning tasks is highly sensitive to input representation, requiring careful selection. The proposed GTA framework effectively addresses this by intelligently selecting representations and scaffolding reasoning, significantly improving performance across various graph problems and demonstrating transferability to other benchmarks.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly asked to reason over structured data such as graphs, yet how reliably they can carry out multi-step graph algorithms in language remains unclear. Existing evaluations tend to use simple tasks on small graphs, to score code generation rather than reasoning over the graph itself, or to fix a single input format. We introduce Graph Theory Bench (GT Bench), a benchmark covering 24 classical graph problems in 44 task-structure settings, with over 100,000 examples across four representations: natural language, structured language, adjacency list, and adjacency matrix. Evaluating eight LLMs on GT Bench shows that accuracy is strongly tied to the input representation, that the best representation shifts with graph density, size, and topology as well as with the model, and that this sensitivity persists, attenuated, in the strongest reasoning models. Building on these observations, we propose the Graph Theory Agent (GTA), which pairs a preference-trained representation selector with plan-and-decompose scaffolding around a frozen executor LLM. GTA lifts Phi-4 from 53.5% to 69.1% on the benchmark's easy split and from 33.0% to 41.5% on its hard split, outperforming eight prompting and agent baselines, and transfers without retraining to GraCoRe and NLGraph. Code for benchmark generation and evaluation: https://github.com/xzx34/GTA. The project homepage is available at https://xzx34.github.io/gta/.

</details>


### [16] [Learning Symbolic Constraint Representations from Examples: A Neuro-Symbolic Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12267)
*Nassim Belmecheri,Arnaud Gotlieb,Nadjib Lazaar,Helge Spieker*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A neuro-symbolic framework for automatic constraint acquisition that reduces user involvement using neural oracle transformers and FastCA engine to recover constraint networks from data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing constraint acquisition approaches require intensive human oracle interactions, making learning costly in terms of time and number of queries. There's a need to reduce user involvement in learning user-defined concepts as constraint networks.

Method: Proposes a neuro-symbolic framework with neural Oracle Transformer models that learn to emulate user responses and generalize conceptual knowledge. These models interact with FastCA, a dedicated constraint acquisition engine that refines oracle responses into sound, consistent, and interpretable constraint networks.

Result: The framework successfully recovers structured symbolic models from data without prior domain knowledge, demonstrating that neuro-symbolic interplay effectively aligns data-driven pattern recognition with symbolic reasoning.

Conclusion: The approach offers a robust method for automating model construction in combinatorial domains by significantly reducing user involvement while maintaining interpretable constraint networks through neuro-symbolic integration.

Abstract: Learning user-defined concepts as constraint networks has been extensively studied in the constraint acquisition (CA) literature. However, existing approaches typically rely on intensive interactions with a human oracle, making the learning process costly in terms of time and number of queries. In this paper, we propose a neuro-symbolic framework for automatic CA that significantly reduces user involvement by introducing neural Oracle Transformer models which learn to emulate user responses and to generalize conceptual knowledge. Trained on previously available examples, the learned oracle interacts with a dedicated CA engine, FastCA, which systematically refines the oracle's responses into a sound, consistent, and interpretable constraint network. This neuro-symbolic interaction enables the recovery of structured symbolic models from data without prior domain knowledge. Our results demonstrate that this neuro-symbolic interplay effectively aligns data-driven pattern recognition with symbolic reasoning, offering a robust approach to automating model construction in combinatorial domains.

</details>


### [17] [T-GADE: Thermodynamical Generative-AI-Driven Evolution of LLM Artifacts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12286)
*Kyoko Ogawa,Naoki Mori*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: T-GADE combines evolutionary computation with LLMs using thermodynamic genetic algorithms to evolve structured artifacts (like code-description pairs), achieving significant improvements in online bin-packing tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Integrating evolutionary computation with large language models requires controlling both population diversity and generative capabilities. There's a need to effectively evolve structured outputs (artifacts) from LLMs, particularly for optimization tasks like online bin-packing.

Method: T-GADE extends thermodynamic genetic algorithms with LLM-based genetic operators and artifact-level diversity evaluation. It uses a free-energy objective with Fermi-type occupancy (excludes repeated genotypes) and Bose-type occupancy (permits them). The method includes exact one-member removal and can recover zero-temperature survival rules from Evolution of Heuristics. Tested on online bin-packing tasks with training and transfer scenarios.

Result: Generational Bose-type T-GADE at T=0.003 reduced median training excess by ~29% (from 1.152% to 0.815%) over 20 runs. Validation selection among top candidates achieved the same median transfer excess (0.496%) as Evolution of Heuristics. Statistical significance shown with Mann-Whitney p=0.042 and Cliff's δ=0.378.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate the utility of thermodynamical selection and validation-based use of retained artifacts for evolving structured LLM outputs. T-GADE successfully combines evolutionary computation principles with LLM capabilities for optimization tasks.

Abstract: Integrating evolutionary computation and large language models (LLMs) requires control of population diversity as well as generative capability. Among LLM outputs, those with explicit structure, such as a description paired with code, are structured artifacts; we use artifact for short. We propose T-GADE, which evolves these artifacts by extending thermodynamical genetic algorithms through LLM-based genetic operators and artifact-level diversity evaluation. A common free-energy objective supports generational and steady-state updates, with Fermi-type occupancy excluding repeated genotypes and Bose-type occupancy permitting them. We establish exact one-member removal and conditions for recovering the zero-temperature survival rule of Evolution of Heuristics (EoH). On the online bin-packing task studied in the EoH paper, excess measures relative bin-count overhead above a volume lower bound. Training excess uses search instances; transfer excess uses instances with another bin capacity. Generational Bose-type T-GADE at $T=0.003$ reduced median training excess by approximately 29%, from 1.152% to 0.815%, over 20 runs per configuration (two-sided Mann-Whitney $p=0.042$, Cliff's $δ=0.378$). Validation selection among its two highest-ranked final candidates reached the same median transfer excess as EoH, 0.496%. These results demonstrate the utility of thermodynamical selection and validation-based use of retained artifacts.

</details>


### [18] [Robust Prototypical Networks for Few-Shot Sensor Fault Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12287)
*Mohammed Ayalew Belay,Amirshayan Haghipour,Pierluigi Salvo Rossi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Multi-Episode Prototypical Networks aggregate prototypes from multiple disjoint support episodes to reduce variance in few-shot fault diagnosis, achieving better one-shot performance without changing encoder architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard prototypical networks can become unstable in very-low-shot regimes because each decision relies on small support sets, leading to unstable class prototypes in industrial fault diagnosis with limited labeled examples.

Method: Proposed Multi-Episode Prototypical Networks (MEPN) aggregate prototypes from multiple disjoint support episodes and use their mean as the final class representative, reducing prototype variance without changing the encoder architecture.

Result: MEPN achieves \SensorOneShotGcpn\% accuracy in per-episode one-shot setting (K=1 shot, aggregated over Nagg=10 support episodes), substantially above single-episode baselines. Under equal 10-sample support budget, MEPN and ProtoNet at K=10 are statistically indistinguishable.

Conclusion: Prototype accumulation is the mechanism for improved performance rather than superior fixed-budget learning, and MEPN effectively addresses prototype instability in very-low-shot industrial fault diagnosis.

Abstract: Industrial fault diagnosis often operates with only a handful of labeled fault examples, making few-shot learning attractive for sensor monitoring. Standard prototypical networks are simple and effective; however, their class prototypes may become unstable in the very-low-shot regime because each decision relies on a small support set. We propose \emph{Multi-Episode Prototypical Networks} (MEPN), which aggregate prototypes from multiple disjoint support episodes and use their mean as the final class representative, reducing prototype variance without changing the encoder architecture. We evaluate MEPN on the DeFACTO sensor dataset using five-way fault classification with synthetic bias, drift, spike, and noise faults injected into real industrial measurements. Over 100 independent runs, MEPN reaches \textbf{\SensorOneShotGcpn\%} in the per-episode one-shot setting ($K\!=\!1$ shot, aggregated over $N_{\text{agg}}\!=\!10$ support episodes), substantially above single-episode baselines. Under an equal 10-sample support budget, MEPN and ProtoNet at $K\!=\!10$ are statistically indistinguishable, confirming prototype accumulation as the mechanism rather than superior fixed-budget learning.

</details>


### [19] [Hybrid Physics-AI Framework of Body Center of Mass Dynamics from Wrist-Worn Sensors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12304)
*Shuhao Que,Valentina Breschi,Ying Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes hybrid AI models (HKM-NN) combining simplified kinematic modeling with neural networks to estimate whole-body center of mass acceleration from wrist-worn IMU data, achieving improved accuracy and robustness over pure kinematic models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wrist-worn IMUs are popular for health monitoring but don't capture whole-body dynamics well. The body's center of mass (COM) is the physiological gold standard for movement assessment, but current wrist IMUs alone can't measure it directly.

Method: 1) Developed a simplified kinematic model (KM) mapping wrist IMU to COM acceleration using reductive assumptions; 2) Created three hybrid AI models (HKM-NN): serial learning (ser-) and two simultaneous learning approaches (sim1-, sim2-) combining the KM with neural networks; 3) Trained and tested on a custom dataset of 10 healthy volunteers performing six gait activities and sit-to-stand movements with ground-truth COM measurements.

Result: The KM model achieved 6.7-12.5% error for gait activities and 5.6% for sit-to-stand. HKM-NN models significantly improved performance to 5.3-9.3% error for gait and best error of 3.9% for sit-to-stand. HKM-NN models showed better robustness to Gaussian noise, while KM was more robust to salt-and-pepper noise.

Conclusion: Combining biomechanical structure (grey-box models) with data-driven learning (black-box models) provides superior performance and robustness for wearable sensing applications, especially under imperfect and noisy measurement conditions.

Abstract: Wrist-worn IMU has been widely used for daily-life health monitoring. Yet, it does not fully represent whole-body dynamics, for which the body center of mass (COM) is considered the physiological reference standard. Therefore, this work proposes a simplified kinematic model (KM), which is designed to map the wrist IMU to the COM acceleration. It is built upon several reductive assumptions that enable the solvability of the dynamic equations based on wrist IMU measurements alone. This work further proposes three types of hybrid AI modeling methods, namely human kinematic model-based neural network (HKM-NN) models, to leverage the power of both grey-box and black-box modeling. The HKM-NN methods include serial learning (ser-) and two approaches of simultaneous learning (sim1- and sim2-). The proposed models are trained and tested using our dataset, which includes wrist IMU measurements and ground-truth COM measurements from 10 healthy volunteers during six gait activities and sit-to-stand (SS) transitional movement. The results demonstrate the feasibility of estimating COM acceleration from wrist IMU measurements. Our KM model yields satisfactory results, with an error ranging from 6.7% to 12.5% for gait activities and 5.6% for the SS. In comparison with the KM model, our HKM-NN models significantly enhance the performance, achieving 5.3% to 9.3% errors for gait activities, and the best error of 3.9% for the SS. In addition, the HKM-NN models demonstrate distinct robustness characteristics under noisy test conditions, with sim1-/sim2- generally maintaining greater robustness under Gaussian perturbations, while the KM model exhibits comparatively strong robustness under salt-and-pepper noise. These findings highlight the importance of combining biomechanical structure with data-driven learning for wearable sensing applications operating under imperfect and noisy measurement conditions.

</details>


### [20] [Do Influence-Derived Data Perturbations Enable Machine Unlearning? A Controlled Study of Three Plausible Roles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12313)
*Chenkai Wu,Chrispine Kambimbi,Qinyang Zeng,Jun Yan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Deep Perturbation Learning (DPL) fails to satisfy claimed machine unlearning roles when properly evaluated with corrected implementation and matched baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous work has positioned Deep Perturbation Learning (DPL) for machine unlearning in three roles: direct deletion signal, utility-preserving regularizer, and warm start for adversarial unlearning. Evidence for weaker roles has been used to support stronger claims, so systematic testing is needed.

Method: The study tests each DPL role separately under matched protocols with exact-seed retraining baselines. It audits the public implementation, corrects identified issues (image direction computation on augmented tensors but applied to raw images, and label perturbation below float32 resolution), then evaluates DPL on CIFAR-10/ResNet-18 with three paired seeds and a one-seed Tiny ImageNet check.

Result: After correcting the implementation, DPL fails the direct-deletion criterion on CIFAR-10 in all three paired seeds. Its utility effects are inconsistent across seeds, and when counting direction-computation time, it underperforms simple warm-start baselines. The Tiny ImageNet check also does not favor DPL as a regularizer or warm start.

Conclusion: DPL does not satisfy its claimed machine unlearning roles when properly evaluated with corrected code and matched baselines, though results are limited to random instance deletion and don't rule out influence-based methods in other deletion regimes. The paper releases evaluation protocols and audit checklists for future perturbation-based deletion claims.

Abstract: We evaluate Deep Perturbation Learning (DPL), which perturbs training images and labels along influence-derived directions, in three roles in which prior work has positioned it for machine unlearning: a direct deletion signal (the strongest claim), a utility-preserving regularizer, and a warm start for adversarial unlearning. Evidence for the weaker roles has been used to support the stronger one, so we test each role separately under a matched protocol with exact-seed retraining baselines. An audit of the public implementation identifies two correctness issues: image directions are computed on augmented, normalized tensors but applied to raw images, and the label perturbation falls below float32 resolution, leaving labels unchanged. After correcting the image-perturbation pipeline, DPL fails the direct-deletion criterion on CIFAR-10/ResNet-18 in all three paired seeds. Its utility effects are inconsistent in sign across seeds, and once direction-computation time is counted it underperforms simple warm-start baselines. A one-seed Tiny ImageNet check likewise does not favor DPL as a regularizer or warm start; preprocessing inconsistencies in the released code make the direct comparison there inconclusive. These results cover random instance deletion only and do not rule out influence-based methods in other deletion regimes. We release a role-matched evaluation protocol and an audit checklist for perturbation-based deletion claims.

</details>


### [21] [AIM: A Privacy-Aware Interoperable Memory Framework for Multi-Agent Multi-User LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12320)
*Zachary Johnson,Nigel Boachie Kumankumah,Somya Chatterjee,Tejas Sathyamurthi,Min Chen,Xinyi Alice Li,Xiao Wang,Emily Morgan Gelchie,Jessica Lin,Sadid A. Hasan,Sulaiman Vesal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AIM is a privacy-aware memory framework for multi-agent, multi-user LLM systems that dynamically classifies information as private or public with access controls, plus MUMBench, the first public multi-user memory evaluation dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional LLMs are limited to individual user sessions and can't learn evolving user preferences. Existing agentic memory systems operate at individual-user level, restricting sharing of beneficial public knowledge across users to improve responses and coordination.

Method: Introduced AIM (Agentic Interoperable Memory) - a unified memory framework that dynamically classifies information as private (owner-only) or public (all users) with index-level access controls. Also created MUMBench dataset with multi-user interactions across four domains to evaluate memory operations (retrieval, creation, update, deletion).

Result: On MUMBench across three runs: 96.0% visibility classification accuracy, 58.8% strict operation accuracy, and 70.5% state-aware operation accuracy. Successfully demonstrated ability to manage both private and shared memories in multi-user environments.

Conclusion: AIM provides an effective solution for persistent, privacy-aware memory management in multi-agent, multi-user LLM systems, enabling both personalized experiences through private memories and improved coordination through shared public knowledge, with MUMBench serving as a valuable evaluation benchmark.

Abstract: Traditional large language models (LLMs) are scoped to individual user sessions, limiting their knowledge to a single conversation and preventing them from learning user preferences that evolve over time. Existing agentic memory systems address this limitation but generally operate at the individual-user level, restricting the public knowledge that could be shared across users to improve downstream responses. We introduce AIM (Agentic Interoperable Memory), a unified, privacy-aware memory framework that enables multi-agent, multi-user LLM systems to persistently manage private and shared memory. AIM dynamically classifies information as private, scoped to one user and inaccessible to others, or public, accessible to all users. It enforces index-level access controls so that private memories are retrievable only by their owner, protecting sensitive data while allowing beneficial shared knowledge to improve coordination and consistency. We also introduce MUMBench (Multi-User Memory Benchmark), a dataset of multi-user interactions containing private and shareable information across four domains. To our knowledge, MUMBench is the first public dataset designed to evaluate multiple memory operations, including retrieval, creation, update, and deletion, in a multi-user environment. Across three independent runs on MUMBench, AIM achieves 96.0% visibility classification accuracy, 58.8% strict operation accuracy, and 70.5% state-aware operation accuracy.

</details>


### [22] [Affective Agent: On-Device Personalized Intervention Reasoning for Wearable Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12322)
*Reina Mun,Zishen Wan,Vijay Janapa Reddi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Affective Agent is a three-layer on-device architecture for personalized intervention reasoning on wearables, combining compact language models with physiological data and user history to make intervention decisions without cloud dependency or per-user retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current affective computing systems can infer user states from wearables, but on-device reasoning about whether, when, and how to intervene remains challenging. There's a need for personalized intervention systems that can operate directly on wearable hardware without cloud dependencies or extensive retraining.

Method: A three-layer reference architecture (perception, personalization, and reasoning) using a compact sub-billion-parameter language model combined with physiological evidence, context, and user history. The system adapts to individuals through host-managed structured memory evolution rather than weight updates, enabling personalization without per-user retraining.

Result: The system was instantiated for indoor environmental quality control and evaluated on simulator-generated longitudinal scenarios. Results showed that memory-driven personalization and two-pass structured reasoning improve intervention decisions within this synthetic evaluation framework.

Conclusion: Affective Agent demonstrates a path toward closed-loop, personalized intervention on wearable-class hardware by moving the decision layer on-device, advancing from state inference to intelligent intervention without cloud dependency or extensive computational overhead.

Abstract: Affective computing has advanced wearable state inference, but on-device reasoning about whether, when, and how to intervene remains challenging. We present Affective Agent, a three-layer reference architecture for personalized intervention reasoning under uncertainty on wearable-class hardware. It combines a compact sub-billion-parameter language model with physiological evidence, context, and user history to decide whether, when, and how to intervene, without cloud dependency or per-user retraining. The architecture is organized into three interacting layers (perception, personalization, and reasoning), adapting to individual users through host-managed structured memory evolution rather than per-user weight updates. We instantiate Affective Agent in indoor environmental quality control and evaluate it on held-out, simulator-generated longitudinal scenarios spanning physiological variation, context, signal quality, and intervention history. Results show that memory-driven personalization and two-pass structured reasoning improve intervention decisions within this synthetic evaluation. By moving the decision layer on-device, this work demonstrates a path from wearable state inference toward closed-loop, personalized intervention on wearable-class hardware.

</details>


### [23] [LoRA-RC: Reservoir Computing with Low-Rank Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12327)
*Wenbin Wan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LoRA-RC adapts reservoir computing systems online using low-rank corrections with stability guarantees, significantly improving prediction accuracy during system drifts while maintaining stability properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional reservoir computing uses a fixed recurrent layer that degrades when the underlying system drifts, while readout-only adaptation is insufficient and unconstrained reservoir adaptation can destroy the stability properties that make RC reliable.

Method: Proposes LoRA-RC which adapts the recurrent matrix through low-rank corrections driven by streaming prediction errors. The base reservoir and adaptation bases are fixed offline, while a small core matrix is adapted online with projection onto a spectral-norm ball and low-pass filtering at each step to ensure stability.

Result: On a Lorenz system with abrupt parameter drift, LoRA-RC reduces post-drift prediction error by 56% vs fixed RC and 51% vs readout-only adaptation. Ablation studies show removing the projection inflates error by more than 40x.

Conclusion: LoRA-RC successfully addresses the system drift problem in reservoir computing by providing online adaptation with guaranteed stability through spectral-norm projections, maintaining both echo-state and incremental stability properties while significantly improving prediction performance.

Abstract: Reservoir computing (RC) trains only a linear readout over a fixed recurrent layer, making it fast and data-efficient for online prediction. However, a static reservoir degrades under system drift, readout-only adaptation is then insufficient, and unconstrained reservoir adaptation can destroy the echo-state and incremental stability properties that make RC reliable. This paper proposes LoRA-RC, which adapts the recurrent matrix through a low-rank correction driven by streaming prediction errors. The base reservoir and adaptation bases are fixed offline; a small core matrix is adapted online, projected onto a spectral-norm ball, and low-pass filtered at each step. The projection guarantees that every applied recurrent matrix remains within a certified contraction set, and an incremental input-to-state stability bound is established for the reservoir along each online adaptation path, with path-independent rate and gain. On a Lorenz system with an abrupt parameter drift, LoRA-RC cuts post-drift prediction error by 56% versus a fixed RC and 51% versus readout-only adaptation; ablations over 20 seeds show that removing the projection inflates this error by more than a factor of 40.

</details>


### [24] [Toward Robust Personalized Alignment for LLMs: Mitigating Persona Drift in Multi-Turn Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12373)
*Youyuan Zhang,Siyuan Li,Fangming Liu,Jing Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CORE is a new method for personalized language models that separates local evidence from persistent persona-state revision, using uncertainty-aware belief revision to selectively update user preferences while maintaining robustness against transient or ambiguous observations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Persona drift remains a major challenge for personalized language models because user profiles naturally evolve over long interactions rather than staying fixed. Models need to revise persistent persona states when preferences genuinely change, but avoid updating based on transient, ambiguous, or unresolved observations.

Method: CORE separates turn-local evidence from persistent persona-state revision and selectively updates grounded user preferences through uncertainty-aware belief revision. The authors also introduce PERSIST, a held-out post-anchor benchmark for testing persona-state robustness under sequential interaction stress covering ambiguity, conflict, and controlled social influence.

Result: Across ALOE, PersonaChat, and the new PERSIST benchmark, CORE improves personalized alignment and robustness, with complementary gains in normalized closed-slot state fidelity. Human evaluation and mechanistic controls further demonstrate that explicit update control provides benefits beyond just stronger generation or persistent memory alone.

Conclusion: The CORE framework effectively addresses persona drift by providing explicit control over persona-state updates through uncertainty-aware belief revision, enabling models to distinguish between genuine preference changes and transient observations while maintaining robust persona representations across long-term interactions.

Abstract: Persona drift remains a central challenge for personalized language models, as user profiles evolve over long interactions rather than remain permanently fixed. Models must therefore revise persistent persona states when preferences genuinely change, while avoiding updates driven by transient, ambiguous, or unresolved observations. We propose CORE, which separates turn-local evidence from persistent persona-state revision and selectively updates grounded user preferences through uncertainty-aware belief revision. We also introduce PERSIST, a held-out post-anchor benchmark for persona-state robustness under sequential interaction stress, covering ambiguity, conflict, and controlled social influence. Across ALOE, PersonaChat, and PERSIST, CORE improves personalized alignment and robustness, with complementary gains in normalized closed-slot state fidelity. Human evaluation and mechanistic controls further support explicit update control beyond stronger generation or persistent memory alone.

</details>


### [25] [BlueLM-GUI Technical Report: A Real-Device-Centric Flywheel for Self-Improving Mobile GUI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12394)
*Tong Ye,Kunyang Han,Guozhi Wang,Longqiang Luo,Zhifeng Ding,Yongxiang Zhang,Xiaolei Shen,Yuxuan Zhang,Zhuping Zhang,Tao Xu,Yue Pan,Yucheng Zhao,Yupei Hu,Yuanjiang Ouyang,Danfeng Shen,Runqi Lin,Hongda Cai,Zhaoxiong Wang,Mengjia Yan,Yingjie Zhong,Chen Zhou,Zeyu Zhang,Xuwen Zhu,Penggang Shi,Mingcheng Luo,Ziyang Wu,Min Jin,Mingfu Shen,Zairong Xu,Fan Zhang,Hao Wang,Liang Liu,Zhulin Xie,Lijun Yao,Xiao Liang,Liangmin Wen,Liqiang Feng,Feilong Wu,Min Hu,Min Chen,Guanjing Xiong,Xiaohu Ruan,Xiaoxin Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BlueLM-GUI is a 35B mobile GUI agent that addresses industrial deployment gaps through real-device-centric training, salvaging all training samples, and adaptive benchmarking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mobile GUI agents face three persistent gaps in industrial deployment: distribution mismatch between sandbox training and production, underutilization of expensive real-device failures, and saturated fixed benchmarks that lose effectiveness for guiding iteration.

Method: Three principles: 1) Every Sample Matters - dual-track pipeline with Heterogeneous Triple-System Consensus evaluation and Error Correction & Derivation Module to salvage every trajectory; 2) Every Rollout Is Real - three-stage recipe (continual pre-training, supervised fine-tuning, agentic reinforcement learning) on hundreds of real phones; 3) Every Query Evolves - quota-driven benchmark methodology with three orthogonal axes for precise attribution and systematic upgrades.

Result: Achieves 87.4 on MobileGUI-VBench (surpassing best closed-source model by 5.1 points) and 84.9 on AndroidWorld (best among open-source models, competitive with closed-source models).

Conclusion: Grounding model training and iterative improvement in real devices and the three Every principles yields strong, robust, and transferable mobile GUI capability for industrial deployment.

Abstract: Mobile GUI agents are shifting from multi-module frameworks to native models trained end-to-end, yet industrial deployment faces three persistent gaps. Sandbox training produces a distribution mismatch with production environments; expensive real-device failures remain underutilized; and fixed benchmarks saturate, losing the power to guide iteration. We present BlueLM-GUI, a 35B-A3B mobile GUI agent built as a real-device-centric flywheel that closes these gaps through three principles. Every Sample Matters: a dual-track pipeline with Heterogeneous Triple-System Consensus evaluation and an Error Correction \& Derivation Module salvages every trajectory into usable supervision. Every Rollout Is Real: a three-stage recipe---continual pre-training, supervised fine-tuning, and agentic reinforcement learning on hundreds of real phones---grounds every rollout in real production environments, so the capability the model learns transfers directly to deployment. Every Query Evolves: a quota-driven benchmark methodology with three orthogonal axes enables precise attribution and allows the benchmark to be systematically upgraded as the model improves. BlueLM-GUI achieves 87.4 on MobileGUI-VBench, surpassing the best closed-source model by 5.1 points, and 84.9 on AndroidWorld, the best result among open-source models and competitive with closed-source models.
