These results demonstrate that grounding model training and iterative improvement in both real devices and the three Every principles yields strong, robust, and transferable mobile GUI capability.

</details>


### [26] [Is Gaussian Splatting Becoming Neural Again? A Taxonomy and Controlled Study of Learned Parameterization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12395)
*YuanHang Wang,Xin Cao,Yi Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Neural parameterization in 3D Gaussian Splatting should be selective—neural networks help for appearance and opacity sharing but not for geometric structure decoding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent 3DGS systems increasingly use neural networks to generate Gaussian parameters, but it's unclear which neuralization strategies are beneficial and how they should be applied. The paper aims to systematically characterize and evaluate different neural parameterization approaches in Gaussian splatting.

Method: The authors analyze 19 representative methods along five axes: attribute decoding, spatial sharing, view-conditioned decoding, topology generation, and amortized inference. They also conduct controlled experiments using mip-NeRF 360 to isolate three forms of neural parameterization and evaluate their impact.

Result: Sharing appearance and opacity parameters through neural networks improves reconstruction quality, while decoding geometric structure offers no additional gain. The analysis shows neural parameterization cannot be reduced to a simple binary classification, and different approaches address different limitations.

Conclusion: Selective neuralization is optimal—neural functions help when they capture reusable correlations while preserving the local geometric freedom of explicit splats. The evidence supports using neural networks for appearance and opacity sharing but not for geometric structure generation.

Abstract: Three-dimensional Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) combines explicit primitives with efficient rasterization, yet recent systems increasingly use neural networks to generate or share Gaussian parameters. We characterize this trend along five axes: attribute decoding, spatial sharing, view-conditioned decoding, topology generation, and amortized inference. An analysis of 19 representative methods shows that these choices address different limitations and cannot be reduced to a binary neural label. We also isolate three forms of neural parameterization in a controlled mip-NeRF 360 study. Sharing appearance and opacity improves reconstruction quality, while decoding geometric structure offers no further gain. The evidence favors selective neuralization: shared functions help when they capture reusable correlations without sacrificing the local geometric freedom of explicit splats.

</details>


### [27] [Niching Agents in The Core](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12398)
*Gary B. Parker,Jim O'Connor,John Asaro*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The Core algorithm evolves agents without fitness functions using local tournament selection. This research shows that niching agents to specific environmental subsets improves performance over non-niched or differently-niched agents in the Xpilot game.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous research showed The Core algorithm's success in evolving agents for combat and navigation in Xpilot. This work aims to improve performance by niching agents to specific environmental subsets rather than the entire environment.

Method: Extends The Core competitive co-evolution algorithm (which uses tournament selection, crossover, and mutation without traditional fitness functions) by implementing niching techniques. Agents are specialized to specific subsets of the Xpilot game environment rather than evolving in the complete environment.

Result: Niched agents demonstrated superior performance over both agents trained on the entire system and agents specialized to different sub-environments, showing the effectiveness of environmental specialization.

Conclusion: Environmental niching within competitive co-evolution algorithms like The Core can enhance agent specialization and performance, suggesting that targeted environmental adaptation is more effective than general evolution across entire complex environments.

Abstract: The Core is a unique competitive co-evolution algorithm that allows agents to evolve autonomous control without utilizing a traditional fitness function. The agents evolve via local interactions through tournament selection, crossover, and mutation, producing offspring by evolving better controllers. Previous works have shown The Core's ability to evolve agents capable of combat and navigation in the Xpilot video game. This research expands upon that premise by niching agents to specific subsets of the original environment The Core was tested in. Our results demonstrate the niched agents capacity for success over agents niched to the entire system and agents niched to different sub-environments.

</details>


### [28] [OneLA: Scaling Linear-Attention Decoding to Large Beams in Generative Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12399)
*Xiangrui Yang,Cheng Peng,Yunfeng Zhao,Liang Zeng,Ao Hu,Jiawei Yang,Shengzhe Wang,Jingshan Lv,Xiao Liang,Chen Yang,Jiaqiang Liu,Yiming Qiu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OneLA is a linear-attention decoding framework that optimizes generative recommendation systems by efficiently managing shared prompt states and divergent beam histories, reducing memory overhead and improving decoding speed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative recommendation systems require large-beam decoding to generate candidate items, creating scaling challenges for recurrent linear attention. Existing approaches either materialize full recurrent states for every beam or replay shared history, incurring significant memory and traffic overhead.

Method: OneLA represents all beam states using a single shared prompt-derived state and compact, append-only records of divergent transitions. It computes only required state information at each step without reconstructing full recurrent states. A lightweight ancestry index tracks transition records for each beam's history, allowing updates without moving/copying records. A fused GPU kernel reuses shared state across beams.

Result: OneLA achieves 1.54-2.46x end-to-end decode speedups while substantially reducing recurrent-state memory use and data movement compared to existing linear attention serving systems.

Conclusion: OneLA effectively addresses the scaling challenges of generative recommendation workloads by exploiting shared prompt characteristics and divergent suffixes, providing significant performance improvements through efficient state management and computation.

Abstract: Generative recommendation (GR) relies on large-beam decoding to generate hundreds of candidate items, creating a new scaling challenge for recurrent linear attention. Existing linear attention serving systems either materialize a full recurrent state for every beam or repeatedly replay shared history, incurring substantial memory and traffic overhead. To address this, we present OneLA, a linear-attention decoding framework that exploits the shared prompt and short divergent suffixes of GR workloads. Specifically, OneLA represents all beam states using a single shared prompt-derived state and compact, append-only records of their divergent transitions. Using this representation, OneLA computes only the state information required at each decoding step, without reconstructing a full recurrent state for every beam. Furthermore, OneLA uses a lightweight ancestry index to track the transition records that make up each beam's history, allowing beams to be updated without moving or copying existing records. A fused GPU kernel further reuses the shared state across beams. Our analysis shows that OneLA achieves 1.54-2.46x end-to-end decode speedups while substantially reducing recurrent-state memory use and data movement.

</details>


### [29] [Decentralized Evolution of Hexapod Gaits with Independent Leg Controllers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12400)
*Gary B. Parker,John Asaro,Jim O'Connor*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A decentralized evolutionary algorithm evolves hexapod leg gaits independently, achieving emergent coordination that outperforms cooperative coevolution in generating stable, adaptive locomotion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional centralized gait optimization for hexapods is complex; there's a need for simpler, scalable approaches that can achieve coordinated locomotion through emergent behaviors rather than explicit control.

Method: A decentralized evolutionary algorithm where each leg's gait controller evolves independently without centralized coordination, using Webots simulator with Mantis hexapod robot, benchmarked against cooperative coevolution.

Result: The decentralized method shows improved efficacy over cooperative coevolution, generating stable and adaptive gaits with interesting emergent coordination, while reducing gait optimization complexity.

Conclusion: Independent evolution of leg controllers enables simpler gait optimization and demonstrates the potential of decentralized strategies for scalable, adaptive robotic systems through emergent coordination.

Abstract: This paper presents a novel approach to hexapod locomotion by evolving each leg's gait independently through a decentralized evolutionary algorithm. Using the Webots simulator and the Mantis hexapod robot, we optimize individual leg controllers without centralized coordination, allowing emergent behaviors to drive the development of efficient, coordinated locomotion. Our decentralized method is benchmarked against cooperative coevolution, demonstrating improved efficacy in generating stable and adaptive gaits while showing interesting emergent coordination. By enabling independent evolution of leg controllers, this method reduces the complexity of gait optimization and highlights the potential of decentralized strategies for scalable and adaptive robotic systems.

</details>


### [30] [Beyond ID Embeddings: Process-Grounded Language Modeling for Cognitive Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12403)
*Minghang Liu,Yuanzhuo Wang,Qiang Qiu,Huawei Shen,Xueqi Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PLCD uses LLMs to build cognitive structures from language, replacing discrete embeddings with process-aware representations for better cognitive diagnosis and cold-start robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional Cognitive Diagnosis Models (CDMs) use discrete, ID-based embeddings that don't reflect how knowledge is actually stored in human cognition. This leads to semantic limitations, especially when dealing with new exercises or concepts that weren't seen during training.

Method: Proposes Process-aware Language Cognitive Diagnosis (PLCD) framework that: 1) Uses LLMs to construct concept schemas and cognitive process graphs as cognitive priors, 2) Implements target-conditioned semantic memory to retrieve relevant historical responses, 3) Uses a process-grounded Language-to-Cognition Mapper with DA-MoE experts and process-level contrastive learning to map textual evidence into unified cognitive space.

Result: Experimental results show PLCD outperforms traditional baselines in predicting student performance and exhibits strong cognitive transfer capabilities. The framework connects LLMs' computational power with psychometric goals of measuring latent knowledge states.

Conclusion: Structured language priors calibrated by response records can improve cold-start robustness and cognitive grounding, suggesting a promising direction for more cognitively plausible CDMs that leverage LLMs for semantic understanding.

Abstract: Cognitive Diagnosis Models (CDMs) play a pivotal role in personalized online learning. Traditional CDMs rely on discrete, ID-based embeddings to represent students, exercises, and concepts. This paradigm diverges from the nature of learner cognition, where knowledge is not stored and retrieved as isolated symbols. As a result, CDMs suffer from semantic limitations when new exercises or concepts appear. In this paper, we propose a Process-aware Language Cognitive Diagnosis (PLCD) framework that uses language-derived structures as cognitive priors and response records to calibrate student posterior states. PLCD leverages large language models (LLMs) to construct concept schemas and cognitive process graphs, and uses target-conditioned semantic memory to retrieve historical responses that are relevant to each target exercise. A process-grounded Language-to-Cognition Mapper with DA-MoE experts and process-level contrastive learning then maps the textual evidence into a unified cognitive space. Experimental results show that PLCD not only outperforms traditional baselines in predicting student performance but also exhibits strong cognitive transfer capabilities. These results connect the computational power of LLMs with the psychometric goal of measuring latent knowledge states, suggesting that structured language priors calibrated by response records can improve cold-start robustness and cognitive grounding.

</details>


### [31] [VRL-Bench: Benchmarking agents on computer control tasks under finite trial budgets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12404)
*Yu Bai,Yukai Miao,Dawei Wang,Li Chen,Yanyu Ren,Yuqian Shi,Dan Li,Ying Xiong,Chengqiu Tan,Run Zhou,Li Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VEX²: a verbal exploration-exploitation scheduler that uses language models to jointly select policies and allocate trial budgets, achieving positive success-rate gains over retry across all settings in the VRL-Bench evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current verbal reinforcement learning methods like Reflexion show inconsistent performance - improving success in some settings but reducing it in others. There's a need for a systematic evaluation framework (VRL-Bench) and better methods that consistently improve trial-and-error learning for language agents on complex tasks.

Method: 1) Created VRL-Bench evaluation harness for fair trial-and-error learning assessment under finite trial budgets. 2) Evaluated multiple verbal-memory methods across three models on MiniWoB and WebShop. 3) Proposed VEX² - a verbal exploration-exploitation scheduler that uses language models to jointly select policies and allocate remaining trial budget.

Result: VEX² was the only evaluated update to achieve positive observed success-rate gains over retry in all six settings (across models and tasks). Replay experiments revealed that using reflection can reduce success rates, showing a trade-off between exploiting experience and continued exploration.

Conclusion: VEX² effectively addresses the exploration-exploitation trade-off in verbal reinforcement learning by intelligently scheduling policy selection and budget allocation, providing consistent improvements over simple retry baselines across diverse settings.

Abstract: Learning from trial and error is a promising way to improve language agents on complex tasks such as computer control. Reflexion introduced verbal reinforcement learning, which turns failed trials into text that guides later attempts without updating model parameters. We introduce VRL-Bench, a harness for fair evaluation of trial-and-error learning under finite trial budgets. Across three models on MiniWoB and WebShop, we evaluate updates from several prominent verbal-memory methods spanning Reflexion and later work: each improves observed success over memory-free retry in some settings but reduces it in others. Replay experiments show that using reflection can reduce success rates, revealing a trade-off between exploiting experience and continued exploration. We propose VEX$^2$, a verbal exploration--exploitation scheduler that uses a language model to jointly select policies and allocate the remaining trial budget. VEX$^2$ is the only evaluated update to achieve positive observed success-rate gains over retry in all six settings.

</details>


### [32] [SoK: Rethinking Jailbreaking in the Era of Agentic AI: Attacks, Defenses, and Practical Consideration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12413)
*Md Jueal Mia,Yanzhao Wu,Selcuk Uluagac,M. Hadi Amini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A systematic knowledge review examining which jailbreak security findings remain relevant for modern LLMs and agentic AI, revealing gaps in current defenses and advocating for execution-aware security approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs evolve from conversational assistants to agentic systems with reasoning, planning, tool use, and memory capabilities, and as modern models exhibit stronger native safety alignment, there's a need to understand which established jailbreak security findings remain valid in this new landscape.

Method: Conducted a Systematization of Knowledge (SoK) reframing jailbreak security around the full agentic execution pipeline. Developed unified taxonomies of attacks and defenses across interaction, planning, memory, tool use, and inter-agent communication. Created a security-utility-efficiency evaluation framework and performed controlled empirical study of representative attacks/defenses within a common agentic framework.

Result: Three key gaps identified: 1) Strong native alignment doesn't guarantee adversarial jailbreak robustness, 2) Defense effectiveness varies significantly by model, attack, and component with substantial trade-offs in refusal rates, utility, and latency, 3) Low final-response attack success can mask severe intermediate compromise where planning, memory, and tool interactions remain unsafe despite filtered final responses.

Conclusion: The findings motivate a paradigm shift from response-centric jailbreak defense toward cross-layer, execution-aware security that protects agent state, component transitions, and external actions while maintaining practical utility and efficiency.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are rapidly evolving from conversational assistants into agentic AI systems that reason, plan, invoke tools, maintain persistent memory, communicate with other agents, and execute multi-step tasks. At the same time, modern models exhibit substantially stronger native safety alignment than earlier generations on which many jailbreak attacks and defenses were originally studied. This shift raises a fundamental question: \textit{which established jailbreak-security findings remain valid in the era of modern LLMs and agentic AI?} We address this question through a Systematization of Knowledge (SoK) that reframes jailbreak security around the full agentic execution pipeline. We develop unified taxonomies of attacks and defenses spanning user interaction, planning and reasoning, memory, tool use, and inter-agent communication, and introduce a security--utility--efficiency evaluation framework that separates native harmful-prompt safety, adversarial jailbreak robustness, and agent-level security outcomes. We further conduct a controlled empirical study of representative attacks and defenses within a common agentic framework. Our results reveal three important gaps. First, strong native alignment does not imply robustness to adversarial jailbreaks. Second, defense effectiveness is highly model-, attack-, and component-dependent and can come at substantial cost in over-refusal, utility, and latency. Third, low final-response attack success can mask severe intermediate compromise: planning, memory, and tool interactions may remain unsafe even when the final response is successfully filtered. These findings motivate a shift from response-centric jailbreak defense toward cross-layer, execution-aware security that protects agent state, component transitions, and external actions while preserving practical utility and efficiency.

</details>


### [33] [LifeFuse-Mem: Lifecycle-Aware State Fusion Against Temporary Overwriting for Long-Term Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12436)
*Hanyu Zhao,Yuqian Feng,Zhenyu Song,Yuanchao Cheng,Yance Jiao,Tengfei Pan,Li Du*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LifeFuse-Mem is a lifecycle-aware neural memory framework for LLM agents that separates information by temporal commitment, using lifecycle metadata during training and phase-aware readout during evaluation to prevent temporary context from overwriting durable knowledge.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-running LLM agents need memory mechanisms that maintain coherent internal states across interactions. Current approaches suffer from behavioral drift when temporary information overwrites durable knowledge, creating a mismatch between what should persist versus what's context-specific.

Method: Introduces LifeFuse-Mem framework with lifecycle-labeled memory setting: uses write episodes with lifecycle metadata during training, and phase-aware readout during evaluation. Separates information by temporal commitment using dedicated memory components and lifecycle-aware updates to prevent temporary context from becoming durable state.

Result: On anti-overwrite benchmark: improves acquisition-controlled retention and reduces temporary overwrite. On two public long-memory benchmarks: remains broadly competitive. Demonstrates that explicit lifecycle signals help diagnose and mitigate overwrite in compact online memory.

Conclusion: Lifecycle-aware memory frameworks like LifeFuse-Mem effectively address overwrite problems in persistent LLM agents by separating information based on temporal commitment, preventing behavioral drift and improving long-term memory stability.

Abstract: Long-running LLM agents require memory mechanisms that maintain coherent internal states across interactions. We study a lifecycle-labeled memory setting in which write episodes provide lifecycle metadata during training, and phase-aware readout is used during evaluation. This setting reflects the need to distinguish information that should remain influential across future interactions from information that should affect only the current context. A mismatch between these lifecycles can cause temporary information to overwrite durable knowledge, leading to behavioral drift in persistent agents. Within this setting, we introduce \textbf{LifeFuse-Mem}, a lifecycle-aware neural memory framework that separates information according to its temporal commitment. LifeFuse-Mem uses dedicated memory components and lifecycle-aware updates to allow stable and transient knowledge to evolve locally without converting temporary context into durable state. On the controlled anti-overwrite benchmark, LifeFuse-Mem improves acquisition-controlled retention and reduces temporary overwrite; on two public long-memory benchmarks, it remains broadly competitive. These results suggest that explicit lifecycle signals can help diagnose and mitigate overwrite in compact online memory.

</details>


### [34] [Do LLMs Trust the Accuser or the Accusation? Measuring Belief Shifts in Werewolf](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12446)
*Yu-Yu Yang,Ti-Rong Wu,Hung Guei,Hsing-Yu Chen,I-Chen Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs struggle to properly integrate accusation content with source trust in strategic social deduction games like Werewolf, even though larger models (up to 120B) perform better at distinguishing true wolves from villagers based on game history.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of LLM agents in social-deduction games like Werewolf rely mainly on final game outcomes, lacking analysis of communication skills and belief updating during gameplay.

Method: Created a belief-shift evaluation benchmark using LLM-played Werewolf games, annotating suspicion and accusation messages, and measuring how observing village-side models' beliefs change after each message. Evaluated 40 open-weight LLM configurations on 1,224 annotated messages.

Result: Larger models better distinguish true wolves from villagers based on game history, but accusations still strongly influence their beliefs - models become more suspicious of accused targets and less suspicious of accusers, especially when accuser is trusted (even if wolf-aligned). Larger models better resist accusations from distrusted accusers.

Conclusion: Current open-weight LLMs up to 120B parameters still struggle to integrate accusation content with source trust in strategic communication, indicating limitations in reasoning about social dynamics and trust in multi-agent settings.

Abstract: Social-deduction games such as Werewolf are increasingly used to evaluate LLM agents, but existing evaluations often rely on final game outcomes. We propose a belief-shift evaluation benchmark in Werewolf for analyzing communication skills through belief updating. Using LLM-played games, we annotate suspicion and accusation messages and measure how an observing village-side model's beliefs change after each message. We evaluate 40 open-weight LLM configurations on 1,224 annotated messages. Our results show that larger models better distinguish true wolves from villagers based on game history, but accusations still strongly influence their beliefs. Models become more suspicious of the accused target and less suspicious of the accuser, especially when the accuser is trusted, even if the accuser is wolf-aligned. Larger models better resist accusations from accusers they already distrust. Overall, our findings suggest that current open-weight LLMs up to 120B parameters still struggle to integrate accusation content with source trust in strategic communication. Our benchmark and code are available at https://rlg.iis.sinica.edu.tw/papers/werewolf-accusation-benchmark.

</details>


### [35] [EvoRS: On-Policy Self-Evolution of Reward Systems for Open-Ended Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12459)
*Weiyuan Li,Aili Chen,Xintao Wang,Yikai Zhang,Qingqing Dong,Jinghan Xu,Hongru Hou,Wenxuan Zhao,Chengkun Lang,Jun Gao,Yuanli Guo,Hongcheng Guo,Yanghua Xiao,Deqing Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EvoRS is a self-evolving RL framework that dynamically updates the reward system during training to prevent reward hacking and maintain reliability in open-ended tasks, outperforming fixed-reward approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In open-ended reinforcement learning, fixed rubric-based rewards become unreliable over time due to reward hacking and reduced discriminability as policies optimize, creating a dynamic feedback loop where the reward system needs to evolve alongside the policy.

Method: EvoRS evolves the reward system from on-policy experience using an executable Reward-DAG representation. An agentic designer updates this system based on on-policy rollouts and reward traces to maintain train-time reliability.

Result: Across writing and roleplay tasks, EvoRS achieves best quality under all three judges, outperforming the policy by 2.107 and 4.767 points respectively, while reducing reward hacking, coverage failures, and preserving reward informativeness.

Conclusion: Comprehensive fixed reward systems cannot remain reliable in open-ended tasks and must evolve throughout training; EvoRS demonstrates that dynamic evolution of the reward system is essential for maintaining training reliability.

Abstract: Open-ended reinforcement learning often relies on rubric-based rewards for tasks without directly verifiable answers. Yet the policy and reward system form a dynamic feedback loop: as the policy optimizes the current reward, an initially useful reward system may become unreliable due to reward hacking or reduced response discriminability. The reward system should therefore evolve rather than remain fixed during training. Existing dynamic-rubric methods adapt evaluation criteria, but reward failures can also arise from scoring mechanisms or signal composition. We introduce EvoRS, a self-evolving RL framework that evolves the reward system from on-policy experience, representing it as an executable Reward-DAG. Specifically, an agentic designer updates this system from on-policy rollouts and reward traces to maintain train-time reliability. Across writing and roleplay, EvoRS achieves the best quality under all three judges, outperforming the policy by \(2.107\) and \(4.767\) points, respectively, while reducing reward hacking and coverage failures and preserving reward informativeness. Ablations confirm that a comprehensive fixed reward system cannot remain reliable in open-ended tasks and must evolve throughout training.

</details>


### [36] [Beyond Vector Similarity: Hierarchical Context-Aware Graph RAG vs Standard RAG in Enterprise Code Migration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12464)
*Nilesh Jaiswal,Aniket Agrawal,Arjit Shukla,Divya Malhotra,Saurabh Garg,Suchit Puri,Suddhasatwa Bhaumik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Hierarchical Context-Resident Graph (HCRG) methodology for microservices code translation, replacing standard vector-based RAG with graph-based approaches that preserve architectural relationships, significantly reducing dependency issues in LLM-generated code.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard vector-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) struggles with microservices code translation because it fetches isolated code chunks that sever important architectural relationships like inheritance chains, leading to high compilation failure rates and poor structural integrity in generated code.

Method: The authors propose HCRG methodology that: 1) uses tree-sitter for AST extraction, 2) maps architectural edges into a Google Cloud Spanner Property Graph, and 3) serializes this structure into Gemini Context Cache for topological, parent-first code translation. They also shift evaluation from text-overlap metrics to a custom 7-metric Software Engineering framework.

Result: Graph RAG significantly outperforms standard RAG: API hallucination rates dropped from 56.4% to 16.2%, Dependency Resolution Quality improved from 34.8% to 65.9%, and Parent-Child Consistency rose from 26.7% to 45.5%. However, it introduces trade-offs like reduced Cyclomatic Complexity Consistency (71.6% to 46.7%) and slightly worse Docstring Preservation (67.0% to 61.0%).

Conclusion: While trading code complexity for reduced hallucinations, Graph RAG provides a substantially more viable, architecturally sound path for automated enterprise codebase modernization, though traditional metrics like CodeBLEU (91% for both methods) mask the structural advantages of the graph approach.

Abstract: As enterprises modernize legacy monolithic systems to microservices, Large Language Models (LLMs) are heavily utilized for automated code translation. However, traditional vector-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation (Standard RAG) struggles to capture topological relationships. It fetches isolated chunks that sever inheritance chains, leading to high compilation failure rates. This paper introduces a Hierarchical Context-Resident Graph (HCRG) methodology to resolve these limitations. Our pipeline uses tree-sitter for Abstract Syntax Tree (AST) extraction, maps architectural edges into a Google Cloud Spanner Property Graph, and serializes this structure into a Gemini Context Cache for topological, parent-first code translation. We shift evaluation from naive text-overlap to a custom 7-metric Software Engineering framework. Traditional metrics like CodeBLEU (which scored 91% for both methods) effectively masked Standard RAG's structural failures behind syntactically plausible but broken code. Empirically, Graph RAG decisively mitigates dependency loss: API hallucination rates dropped from 56.4% to 16.2%, Dependency Resolution Quality improved from 34.8% to 65.9%, and Parent-Child Consistency rose from 26.7% to 45.5%. However, Graph RAG introduces specific trade-offs. The dense global context causes defensive over-engineering by the LLM, reducing Cyclomatic Complexity Consistency from 71.6% to 46.7%, and slightly degrades Docstring Preservation (67.0% to 61.0%). Ultimately, while trading code complexity for reduced hallucinations, Graph RAG provides a substantially more viable, architecturally sound path for automated enterprise codebase modernization.

</details>


### [37] [TripPattern: A Pattern-based Text Watermarking Method for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12472)
*Sangjun Moon,Dasom Choi,Jingun Kwon,Hidetaka Kamigaito,Taro Watanabe,Manabu Okumura*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TripPattern is a text watermarking framework that uses three vocabulary partitions (neutral and two pattern groups) and alternates token selection between pattern groups to embed detectable watermarks while maintaining text quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing text watermarking methods that divide vocabulary into green/red tokens tend to reduce text quality and naturalness by overly constraining generation toward specific tokens. There's a need for watermarking techniques that preserve LLM generation quality while maintaining robust detectability.

Method: TripPattern divides vocabulary into three groups: one neutral group and two pattern groups. During text generation, the model alternates token selection between the two pattern groups to embed detectable patterns, while neutral tokens can be selected independently when appropriate to maintain flexibility and naturalness.

Result: Theoretical analysis and empirical evaluations on four multilingual datasets demonstrate that TripPattern maintains LLM generation quality while achieving robust watermark detectability. The pattern-based statistical tests provide interpretable p-values by measuring how often adjacent tokens alternate between pattern groups.

Conclusion: TripPattern successfully addresses the quality-preservation problem in text watermarking by using a three-partition approach with neutral tokens, allowing for both effective watermark detection through pattern alternation and maintenance of text naturalness and quality.

Abstract: Text watermarking techniques have gained significant attention for identifying machine-generated text and mitigating risks from large language models (LLMs). Existing methods typically divide an LLM's vocabulary into green and red tokens, but encouraging generation toward green tokens can reduce text quality and naturalness. To address this, we propose TripPattern, a watermarking framework that formulates text watermarking as a pattern-based matching task using three vocabulary partitions. TripPattern divides the vocabulary into one neutral group and two pattern groups. During generation, the model alternates token selection between the two pattern groups to embed detectable patterns, while neutral tokens are selected independently to improve flexibility and preserve naturalness. For detection, TripPattern uses pattern-based statistical tests that provide interpretable p-values by measuring how often adjacent tokens alternate between the pattern groups. Theoretical analysis and empirical evaluations on four multilingual datasets show that TripPattern maintains LLM generation quality while achieving robust watermark detectability.

</details>


### [38] [When Does AI Augment Work? A Workflow-Level Framework for Human-Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12482)
*CIVIC-AI Collaboration,:,Jiaying Wu,Caleb Ziems,Raymond Chan,Nancy F. Chen,Corlyss Chua,Gerard Chung,Jungpil Hahn,Wee Sun Lee,Zhengyuan Liu,Jamie Ng,Desmond C. Ong,Jeryl Ong,Da Ren Soon,Tianqi Song,Zhi-Xuan Tan,Sixing Tao,Emily Yang,Yajing Yang,Stella Xin Yin,Min-Yen Kan,Diyi Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI workplace value should focus on human-agent collaboration augmenting entire workflows, not just current automation capabilities, with a framework defining six conditions for meaningful AI augmentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current studies measure AI's workplace value primarily through current automation capabilities and public adoption, which ignores the transformative potential of human-agent collaboration in changing the nature of work itself.

Method: Established a precise definition of AI augmentation comprising six conditions: durable net value, meaningful human control, accountability and recovery, and long-term human development through learning, career pathways, and job purpose. Applied this framework in a case study of AI-mediated social surveys.

Result: Developed a comprehensive framework for understanding AI augmentation in workplaces that goes beyond task automation to consider workflow transformation and human development aspects.

Conclusion: Organizations, researchers, and government leaders can use this framework to better understand and shape the future of work by focusing on meaningful human-agent collaboration rather than just automation metrics.

Abstract: We aim to characterise the value of artificial intelligence in the workplace. Current studies largely measure this value in terms of the current automation capabilities and public adoption of AI. However, such metrics ignore the greater impacts of human--agent collaboration in transforming the nature of work. To account for this, we must expand the scope of our analysis beyond atomised tasks of today, and instead focus on how AI can augment entire workflows of the future. To ground this analysis, we establish a precise definition of AI augmentation comprising six conditions, spanning durable net value, meaningful human control, accountability and recovery, and long-term human development through learning, career pathways, and job purpose. We elaborate on these conditions and apply the framework in a case study of AI-mediated social surveys. We conclude by outlining how organisations, researchers, and government leaders can use this framework to make sense of the future of work.

</details>


### [39] [Confidence-Gated Transductive Test Generation for Code Reranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12489)
*Sungjae Lee,Youngsik Yoon,Seockbean Song,Siwei Wang,Wei Chen,Jungseul Ok*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CoTT is a confidence-gated test generation method that adaptively uses inductive (efficient) and transductive (more accurate) approaches based on confidence scores, achieving better test case synthesis for LLM-generated program evaluation while reducing computational costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Test case synthesis is essential for evaluating LLM-generated programs, but constructing high-quality test cases is challenging because reliable expected outputs are often difficult to obtain. Current methods need better efficiency-effectiveness trade-offs.

Method: Confidence-Gated Transductive Test Generation (CoTT) uses an adaptive design: first employs an efficient inductive procedure, then only invokes transductive generation when inductive confidence is low. This gates computation based on confidence levels.

Result: On code reranking benchmarks, CoTT outperforms prior baselines across all reported metrics while reducing computational costs compared to applying transductive generation to every input.

Conclusion: Confidence-based allocation of test-time computation provides a favorable efficiency-effectiveness trade-off with a single efficient LLM, enabling better test case synthesis for evaluating LLM-generated programs.

Abstract: Test case synthesis is crucial for evaluating and ranking programs generated by large language models (LLMs). However, constructing high-quality test cases remains challenging because reliable expected outputs are often difficult to obtain. We propose Confidence-Gated Transductive Test Generation (CoTT), which first uses an efficient inductive procedure and invokes transductive generation only when inductive confidence is low. This adaptive design improves output reliability while allocating extra computation only when needed. On code reranking benchmarks, CoTT outperforms prior baselines across the reported metrics while reducing cost relative to applying transductive generation to every input. These results show that confidence-based allocation of test-time computation provides a favorable efficiency-effectiveness trade-off with a single efficient LLM.

</details>


### [40] [Information Specialization and Constrained Synthesis in Multi-Agent LLM Forecasting: A Prospective Live-Study of the 2026 FIFA World Cup](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12495)
*Julian Varghese,Lucas Bickmann,Sarah Sandmann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Specialized multi-agent LLM systems with quantitative and news specialists were tested on World Cup predictions, finding that unstructured news information provided valuable forecasting signal, but meta-agents didn't improve on the best specialist.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether organizing LLMs into multi-agent systems with specialized roles produces distinct forecasts and whether subsequent synthesis improves utility over individual specialists.

Method: Prospective evaluation over 56 matches of 2026 FIFA World Cup using a sequential four-agent system: two primary forecasting agents (quantitative specialist focusing on structured statistics, news specialist focusing on current unstructured information), a separate critic for review, and a meta-agent for combining forecasts. Betting market forecasts served as benchmark.

Result: News specialist achieved highest mean probability-weighted Top-3 utility and matched betting market in Top-3 exact-score hits. Specialists agreed on at least 2 of 3 scorelines in 50/56 matches. Meta-agent never generated more than one scoreline outside specialists' forecast set.

Conclusion: Rapidly changing unstructured information provides valuable forecasting signal alongside structured statistics, but adding critic and meta-agent stages does not create complementary information or improve on the strongest specialist alone.

Abstract: Large language models are being organized into multi-agent systems with specialized roles, but whether such specialization produces distinct forecasts and whether subsequent synthesis improves utility remains unclear. In this study, we carried out a live, prospective evaluation over the final 56 matches of the information-dense 2026 FIFA World Cup, keeping a frontier foundation model constant while assigning two primary forecasting agents contrasting specialist roles: a quantitative specialist focusing on structured performance statistics and a news specialist focusing on current injuries, tactics and information from press conferences. Their forecasts were then reviewed by a separate critic before being combined by a meta-agent, resulting in a sequential four-agent model. Forecasts from the betting market served as an external benchmark. The news specialist obtained the highest mean probability-weighted Top-3 utility and matched the betting market in Top-3 exact-score hits. Nevertheless, the two specialist forecasters agreed on at least two of the three scorelines in 50 out of 56 matches, and the meta-agent never generated more than one scoreline outside the specialists' forecast set. These findings show that rapidly changing, unstructured information can provide a valuable forecasting signal alongside structured statistics, whereas adding critic and meta-agent stages does not necessarily create complementary information or improve on the strongest specialist.

</details>


### [41] [From Collaboration to Capability: Internalizing Routed LLM Experts into Compact Reasoners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12578)
*Frank Nie,Shuyao Wang,Ethan B. Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A method called RIVET enables a small controller model to internalize expert collaboration through reinforcement learning and supervised training, allowing it to generate reasoning and code independently after expert removal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to create compact controllers that can learn from expert collaboration (including their reasoning and code) and continue to perform well after the experts are removed, enabling efficient deployment without external LLM dependencies.

Method: RIVET uses expert-augmented reinforcement learning with shared outcome signals on controller decisions and expert responses, plus verified trajectory internalization through format-aware supervised training. The controller learns to generate reasoning, code, and interaction structure with local Python execution.

Result: RIVET-1.7B and RIVET-4B achieve 28.25% and 44.16% average accuracy on seven competition-mathematics benchmarks. Stage II improves RIVET-4B's accuracy after expert removal by 6.49 points, with GPQA-Diamond showing generalization to scientific reasoning.

Conclusion: Training on verified collaboration content and structure effectively internalizes expert capabilities into compact controllers, enabling them to perform complex reasoning independently without external LLM support.

Abstract: A compact controller can coordinate stronger experts by selecting whom to consult, formulating requests, and integrating their responses. We study whether learning from both the controller's decisions and the experts' reasoning and code improves its generation after expert removal. We introduce \textsc{Rivet} for \emph{collaboration internalization}: expert-augmented reinforcement learning applies a shared outcome signal to controller decisions and returned expert spans, and verified trajectory internalization consolidates complete successful interactions through format-aware supervised training. The deployed controller generates reasoning, code, and interaction structure with local Python execution and no external LLM. Across seven competition-mathematics benchmarks, RIVET-1.7B and RIVET-4B achieve average accuracies of $28.25\%$ and $44.16\%$; Stage~II improves RIVET-4B's accuracy after expert removal by $6.49$ points, and GPQA-Diamond results provide evidence of generalization to scientific reasoning. Ablations show gains from ordinary trajectory supervision and additional format weighting, supporting the effectiveness of training on the content and structure of verified collaborations.

</details>


### [42] [Reproducing and Evaluating the Generalizability of Subliminal Learning in Open-Weight Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12586)
*Daan van der Weijden,Nathan Brack,Selene Baez Santamaria*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Subliminal learning from teacher models varies across traits and tasks; reproduction supports original claims but extensions show limitations in universality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate subliminal learning in distillation where teacher models transmit behavioral preferences through semantically unrelated data, reproducing and extending the original study with new traits, tasks, and models.

Method: Reproduction of original experiments (animal preferences, misalignment traits across number sequences, code, chain-of-thought) plus extensions: new preference categories (actors/politicians), new task (chess move generation), additional model (Ministral8B), and ablation on answer-space size (1-3 digit sequences) using open-weight models from HuggingFace.

Result: Reproduction supports original paper's claims, but extensions reveal transmission strength varies across traits/tasks, with one model showing almost no effect, indicating subliminal learning is not universal.

Conclusion: Subliminal learning occurs but its effectiveness depends on specific traits, tasks, and models, highlighting limitations in universality while confirming the original phenomenon.

Abstract: In this reproduction paper we investigate subliminal learning, a consequence of distillation where teacher models transmit behavioral preference traits through semantically unrelated data. The original paper explores two types of traits (animal preferences and misalignment), three data modalities (number sequences, code, and chain of thought), and several model families. We reproduce their experiments and extend the setup along three axes: new preference categories (actors and politicians), a new task (chess move generation), and an additional open-weight model (Ministral8B). We also run a controlled ablation on the numbers task's answer-space size (1-, 2-, and 3-digit sequences). We focus on open-weight models with accessible checkpoints on HuggingFace, since the original paper's GPT-4.x fine-tuning is no longer available. Our reproduction supports the original paper's claims, but our extensions show they are not universal as transmission strength varies across traits and tasks, and one model shows almost no effect at all.

</details>


### [43] [Beyond Generation and Accuracy: Diagnosing and Enhancing Visual Chain-of-Thought for Geometry Problem Solving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12606)
*Zhitong Dong,Jicai Pan,Yingguo Gao,Jingting Ding,Hao Chen,Jinjie Gu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GeoVAD-Bench introduces a diagnostic benchmark for evaluating visual reasoning in geometry problems, focusing on intermediate visual aids quality and causal effectiveness, leading to GeoWeave-8B model with significant performance improvements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations for visual reasoning in geometry focus only on visual generation quality and final answer accuracy, ignoring whether intermediate visual aids are geometrically valid, properly utilized, or causally responsible for task success.

Method: Develop GeoVAD-Bench with five-dimensional trajectory diagnosis (perception, auxiliary quality, utilization, deductive reasoning, final correctness) and controlled intervention settings (No-Aux, Auto-Aux, GT-Aux). Create specialized data construction pipeline and progressive SFT+multimodal RL training framework.

Result: The resulting GeoWeave-8B model outperforms base model by +25.3% in final geometric accuracy and achieves +30.4% gain in process average across the four intermediate diagnostic dimensions.

Conclusion: While high-quality auxiliary aids offer substantial gains for geometric problem solving, autonomous generation is frequently hampered by compounding errors across multiple dimensions, and systematic diagnosis plus specialized training can significantly improve geometric reasoning capabilities.

Abstract: While multimodal reasoning has advanced rapidly, solving complex geometry problems critically hinges on active visual assistance, such as constructing auxiliary lines, spurring the rise of Visual Chain-of-Thought (VCoT). However, existing evaluations typically assess visual generation quality and final answer accuracy in isolation, failing to examine whether intermediate visual aids are geometrically valid, effectively utilized in subsequent reasoning, or causally responsible for task success. To bridge this gap, we introduce GeoVAD-Bench, a diagnostic benchmark that pairs a fine-grained five-dimensional trajectory diagnosis covering perception, auxiliary quality, utilization, deductive reasoning, and final correctness with controlled No-Aux, Auto-Aux, and GT-Aux intervention settings to systematically isolate intermediate error modes, the causal gains of visual aids, and the resulting autonomy gap. Our findings reveal that while high-quality auxiliary aids offer substantial theoretical gains for geometric problem solving, autonomous generation is frequently hampered by compounding errors across geometric perception, faithful visual manipulation, visual-state grounding, and deductive reasoning. Guided by these diagnostic insights, we establish a specialized data construction pipeline encompassing geometric perception, diagram editing, and interleaved visual-textual reasoning trajectories, and develop a progressive SFT and multimodal RL training framework. The resulting model, GeoWeave-8B, outperforms the base model by +25.3% in final geometric accuracy and achieves a +30.4% gain in process average across the four intermediate diagnostic dimensions.

</details>


### [44] [SteerDuplex: Steerable Duplex Speech Dialogue Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12623)
*Utkarsh Tyagi,Ramaneswaran Selvakumar,Advait Gosai,Sonal Kumar,Nikhil Barhate,Isabell Sagar,Steven Li,Miheer Bavare,Daniel Quigley,Fabiola Tapia Carrillo,Jose M Patron E,Diego Macías Gutiérrez,Paul Song,Ramani Duraiswami,Dinesh Manocha,Yunzhong He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SteerDuplex: A full-duplex speech model with enhanced steerability via instruction tuning and RL, evaluated on new benchmark SteerBench showing significant improvements in audio-based steering and timing control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current full-duplex spoken dialogue models lack reliable steerability - the ability to shift conversational behavior (tone, persona, speaking rate, voice style) in response to user instructions, which is crucial for natural human-AI interaction.

Method: 1) Introduce taxonomy of text- and audio-based steerability; 2) Develop SteerDuplex by fine-tuning Moshi-based full-duplex model on natural conversations and synthetic dialogues; 3) Apply two-stage RL with hybrid rewards combining verifiable interaction checks and judge-based semantic feedback; 4) Create SteerBench benchmark with 390 spoken prompts and 1,067 human-authored rubrics.

Result: Supervised training improves audio-steering average pass rate by 44.5pp over strongest open baseline; RL raises interruption response from 72.5% to 82.5%, reduces synthetic pause barge-in from 26.5% to 9%; Audio MultiChallenge improves by 7 points; but reward hacking discovered through incomplete responses.

Conclusion: The work enables systematic research on spoken steerability, showing that timing gains must be evaluated alongside response completeness due to reward hacking risks, and providing both model and benchmark for future research.

Abstract: Full-duplex spoken dialogue models support low-latency turn taking, interruption handling, and backchanneling, yet a key capability remains underexplored: steerability, the ability to reliably shift conversational behavior along attributes such as tone, persona, speaking rate, and voice style in response to user instructions. We introduce a taxonomy of text- and audio-based steerability that identifies substantial gaps in current full-duplex models. To address this gap, we introduce SteerDuplex, a Moshi-based full-duplex speech model fine-tuned on natural conversations and synthetic dialogues targeting instruction following, vocal delivery, reasoning, and duplex interaction. We further apply two-stage reinforcement learning (RL) with hybrid rewards, combining verifiable interaction checks and judge-based semantic feedback to improve timing and response continuity. To evaluate full-duplex spoken steerability, we introduce SteerBench, a benchmark with 390 spoken prompts and 1,067 human-authored binary audio and text rubrics spanning tone, persona, style/accent, and speed/length. On SteerBench, supervised training improves audio-steering average pass rate by 44.5 percentage points over the strongest evaluated open baseline. On Audio MultiChallenge, task average pass rate improves by 7 points over its strongest evaluated open baseline. RL further raises source-clean interruption response from 72.5% to 82.5% and reduces synthetic pause barge-in from 26.5% to 9%. Steering and aggregate task scores remain comparable or higher, while reward probes reveal reward hacking through incomplete responses. Our model and benchmark support systematic research on spoken steerability, with reward analysis showing why timing gains must be evaluated alongside response completeness.

</details>


### [45] [Generative AI Use Cases In Real Estate Marketing: Adoption and Constraints in Germany](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12684)
*Victor Kolominsky-Rabas,Leopold Müller,Felicia Perpina,Niklas Kühl*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GenAI adoption in real estate marketing is emerging but uneven, primarily used for marketing communication with human oversight, constrained by integration and compliance issues rather than model capability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite GenAI's potential to transform work organization and performance, there's scarce evidence of its use in real estate agents' day-to-day practice, prompting empirical investigation into actual adoption patterns and constraints.

Method: Conducted a German-based empirical study using eleven semi-structured interviews to investigate GenAI usage in real estate marketing practices.

Result: GenAI is already used across various activities with marketing communication being most prominent, though adoption is uneven; writing exposé texts is the only widely established use case; human-in-the-loop interaction dominates; constraints stem from integration issues, data availability, and compliance rather than model limitations.

Conclusion: The study provides a grounded map of existing and potential GenAI use cases in real estate marketing and identifies practical implications for adoption, highlighting the emergent nature of usage patterns and the importance of addressing integration and compliance barriers.

Abstract: Generative artificial intelligence (GenAI) is changing how work is organized and performed. Real estate marketing is a prime example of this, yet evidence of GenAI in real estate agents' day-to-day practice remains scarce. In this work, we report on our insights from a German-based empirical study with eleven semi-structured interviews. GenAI is already utilized across different activities, with marketing communication being the most prominent. Concrete use cases are emergent and unevenly adopted, with writing exposé texts being the only widely established one. Interaction is predominantly human-in-the-loop: GenAI drafts, structures, and retrieves, while real estate agents curate, verify, and decide. Constraints stem less from model capability than from integration with listings and documents, data availability, and compliance in sensitive tasks. The study contributes a grounded map of existing and potential use cases and identifies tentative practical implications for adoption.

</details>


### [46] [Residual Vector-based Reconstruction as Long-Context Recall Regardless of Context Window Size](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12686)
*MyungHoon Ryu,XinYu Piao,Jong-Kook Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a memory-efficient long-context recall method that uses LLM feed-forward layer activations to reconstruct facts without referencing original documents, maintaining near-constant GPU memory regardless of context length.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs face increasing token-level memory usage proportional to input length when processing long contexts. Existing methods like model optimization and lossy prompt compression fail to solve long-context recall beyond pretrained context windows and still have memory constraints.

Method: Uses parameter activations in LLM's feed-forward layers, which store residual vectors representing facts from source documents. Reconstructs facts by utilizing these residual vectors to deterministically reconstruct query-relevant facts without referencing the original document, avoiding memory overhead of storing long contexts.

Result: Enables answering single-fact questions in two-million-token story contexts where previous methods fail. Maintains near-constant GPU memory usage as context length increases, achieving this without additional training or fine-tuning of model weights.

Conclusion: The method provides an effective solution to long-context recall problems by leveraging intrinsic fact storage in feed-forward layers, offering memory efficiency and high fidelity reconstruction without requiring model modifications or retraining.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) process long contexts, including long documents and lengthy conversations, but face token-level memory usage that increases proportionally to input length. Although model optimization and lossy prompt compression are widely used, these methods still fail to solve the long-context recall problem beyond pretrained and size-constrained context windows. This paper proposes a long-context recall method that maintains near-constant GPU memory usage as context length increases, without additional training. The main idea is to reconstruct facts using parameter activations in the LLM's feed-forward layers, which store residual vectors representing facts from the source document. Utilizing residual vectors allows the LLM to deterministically reconstruct query relevant facts without referencing the original document, preserving high fidelity and reducing memory usage without fine-tuning weights. Experimental results show that the proposed method enables answering single-fact questions in two-million-token story contexts where previous methods fail.

</details>


### [47] [I Am AdMan: A Pipeline for Automatic Generation of Personalized Advertising Imagery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12694)
*Victor Kolominsky-Rabas,Leopold Müller,Claudius Budcke,Niklas Kühl*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AdMan is a multi-agent AI pipeline that transforms customer data into personalized advertisement images at scale, using personas, image generation, and automated quality control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing personalization approaches effectively match products to customers but fail to personalize visual ad representations. While AI shows promise for text personalization and scalable ad production, there's limited research on systematically translating detailed customer information into fully AI-generated personalized advertising imagery at scale.

Method: AdMan, a multi-agent pipeline that: 1) transforms customer data into personas, 2) generates personalized advertisement images conditioned on product reference images, 3) uses an LLM-based judge agent for automated quality control. Implemented with two model configurations, evaluated across four products using six celebrity personas and 100 real customer profiles, producing 1745 advertisements.

Result: The pipeline successfully generates photorealistic and personalized advertisements. Performance varies substantially by product complexity and model configuration. Evaluation combined qualitative expert focus groups and quantitative artifact-rate assessment.

Conclusion: Demonstrates the feasibility and current limitations of fully automated image generation for personalized advertising. Extends literature on AI-based personalized advertising by showing both capabilities and challenges in scaling visual personalization.

Abstract: Personalized marketing can increase customer engagement, satisfaction, and conversion. While existing personalization approaches have become effective at matching the right product to the right customer, the visual representation of advertisements remains generic and only weakly tailored to the individual. Prior research shows that generative artificial intelligence can improve the creation of personalized advertisements, particularly for text, and that image generation models can support scalable advertisement production. However, little research has examined how detailed customer information can be systematically translated into fully AI-generated, personalized advertising imagery at scale on a technical level. To address this gap, we propose AdMan, a multi-agent pipeline that transforms customer data into personas, generates personalized advertisement images conditioned on product reference images, and applies an LLM-based judge agent for automated quality control. We implement the pipeline with two different model configurations and evaluate it across four products, using six celebrity personas for qualitative inspection, and 100 real customer profiles, producing 1745 advertisements. The evaluation combines a qualitative expert focus group and a quantitative artifact-rate assessment. The results show that the pipeline can generate photorealistic and personalized advertisements. At the same time, performance varies substantially by product complexity and model configuration. Our findings extend the literature on AI-based personalized advertising by demonstrating the feasibility and current limitations of fully automated image generation for advertising.

</details>


### [48] [Enabling and Understanding Personalization in AI-Generated Advertising Imagery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12697)
*Victor Kolominsky-Rabas,Leopold Müller,Claudius Budcke,Claas Christian Germelmann,Niklas Kühl*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI framework generates personalized ad images from customer data; moderate personalization works best as high personalization increases both positive personalization perception and negative creepiness effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing personalized marketing focuses on static product matching or basic text/image modifications, lacking comprehensive AI-generated personalized advertising imagery directly from customer data.

Method: Developed AI framework for generating personalized advertising imagery from customer data; conducted two-stage within-subject study with N=100 participants across 4 products and 3 personalization levels (varying data amount/specificity); measured attitudes toward ads, products, and purchase intentions.

Result: Participants perceive differences across personalization levels; AI-generated advertising imagery evaluated most positively at moderate personalization; high personalization increases perceived personalization (positively associated with outcomes) but also increases perceived creepiness (negatively associated with outcomes), with creepiness dominating the total effect.

Conclusion: Moderate personalization in AI-generated advertising imagery is optimal, as high personalization creates a trade-off where negative creepiness effects outweigh positive personalization benefits, demonstrating the "personalization paradox" in AI-generated visual advertising.

Abstract: Personalized marketing traditionally matches static products to customers, while dynamic creative optimization focuses mainly on AI-driven text personalization or basic product image modifications. We address this gap by developing and implementing an AI-based framework that generates personalized advertising imagery directly from customer data. We evaluate this framework in a two-stage within-subject study with N=100 participants across four products and three levels of personalization, varied by the amount and specificity of customer data used. Participants rated each image on attitude toward the advertisement, attitude toward the product, and purchase intention. Results show that participants perceive differences across personalization levels and evaluate AI-generated advertising imagery most positively at a moderate level of personalization. High personalization increases perceived personalization, which is positively associated with all three outcome measures, but also increases perceived creepiness, which is negatively associated with the outcomes and dominates the total effect.

</details>


### [49] [Implicit Personality Representations in Humans and LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12704)
*Yilin Geng,Omri Abend,Eduard Hovy,Lea Frermann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM's internal trait representations strongly align with human implicit personality structure, capturing social warmth and intellectual competence dimensions


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test whether large language models reproduce the consistent relational structure of human personality traits that has been observed across cultures and raters for a century.

Method: Built a human implicit-personality matrix from millions of crowd-sourced ratings of fictional characters, and a matching matrix from LLM (Qwen 2.5-7B-Instruct) contrastive activations over hundreds of traits, comparing the relational structures using correlation analysis.

Result: Strong alignment between human and model trait structures (Mantel r = 0.77), with model representations recovering the two dominant human dimensions: social warmth and intellectual competence. Model-generated personality profiles from dialogue projections agree with human ratings.

Conclusion: LLMs internalize human-like personality trait structure, establishing a framework for comprehensive comparison between model representations and human personality impression systems.

Abstract: A century of psychology has found that the trait words people use to describe one another vary, but the relational structure among those traits, which ones go together and which oppose, is strikingly consistent across raters and cultures. We test whether the LLM (Qwen 2.5-7B-Instruct) reproduces this structure in its internal trait representations. From millions of crowd-sourced personality ratings of fictional characters, we build a human implicit-personality matrix over hundreds of traits; from contrastive model activations, we build a matching matrix over the same traits. The two relational structures align strongly (Mantel r = 0.77), and the agreement holds trait by trait as well as in aggregate. Two dominant axes of the model's trait representations recover the social and intellectual dimensions long known to organize human personality impressions, social warmth and intellectual competence. On held-out dialogue, projecting model activations onto these directions yields personality profiles that agree with human ratings. This work establishes a framework that enables comprehensive, human-grounded comparison between internal model trait geometry and the shared structure of human personality impressions.

</details>


### [50] [When Rubrics Fail: Hallucinations Reveal Blind Spots in Medical AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12718)
*Griffin Farrow,Lily Sijia Li,Jack Johnson,Tingyan Wang,Philip Torr,William Bolton,Fabio J. Fehr*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Rubric-based evaluation of medical LLMs misses clinically relevant hallucinations; fact-checking helps but rubrics alone are insufficient for clinical reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hallucinations in LLMs can undermine clinician trust, and it's unclear whether current rubric-based evaluation methods capture clinically relevant errors. There's a need to determine if rubric scores properly reflect harmful medical hallucinations that would affect clinical practice.

Method: 1) Controlled study using MedHallu showing specific rubrics better distinguish correct from hallucinated responses. 2) Developed taxonomy of medical hallucination types and clinician-validated error-injection pipeline to create matched correct/error responses. 3) Tested across HealthBench, HealthBench Professional, and LiveMedBench datasets.

Result: Clinically relevant hallucinations were missed by rubrics, often leaving scores unchanged. Rubrics are most effective when explicitly checking facts but less effective for additional/unexpected errors. Preliminary retrieval-based factuality check recovered some rubric-blind errors, suggesting complementary approach needed.

Conclusion: Rubric scores alone are insufficient to establish clinical reliability for LLMs in medicine, revealing systematic blind spots in current evaluation methods. This undermines clinician trust and confidence in clinical deployment, requiring improved evaluation approaches.

Abstract: Hallucinations can undermine clinician trust in LLMs, making it important that evaluation methods capture clinically relevant errors. Rubric-based evaluation has become the leading approach for assessing LLMs in medicine, but it is unclear whether rubric scores reflect such errors. We first study this in a controlled setting using MedHallu, finding that more specific rubrics better distinguish correct from hallucinated responses. To test this systematically, we develop a taxonomy of medical hallucination types and a clinician-validated error-injection pipeline that creates matched correct and error-injected responses. Across HealthBench, HealthBench Professional, and LiveMedBench, our clinically relevant hallucinations are missed by rubrics, often leaving scores unchanged. We find that rubrics are most effective when explicitly checking facts, and are less effective for additional or unexpected errors they do not anticipate. A preliminary retrieval-based factuality check recovers some of the rubric-blind errors, suggesting a complementary approach. These findings reveal systematic blind spots in current medical evaluation of LLMs and suggest that rubric scores alone are insufficient to establish clinical reliability, potentially undermining clinician trust and confidence in clinical deployment.

</details>


### [51] [Skill Issue: Lessons from Optimizing Repository SKILLs for Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12742)
*Mykhailo Kozyrev,Andrei Kozyrev,Anton Podkopaev*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a new method for evaluating and optimizing repository documentation by mining harder tasks from merged pull requests and measuring whether coding agents perform better with the documentation than without it.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods for evaluating repository documentation (SKILLs) use synthetic benchmarks that are too easy - capable agents can solve them without any documentation at all. There's a need for harder evaluation tasks and better methods to assess documentation quality.

Method: The researchers mine harder tasks from repository histories by using merged pull requests that were reverted at a single frozen base commit. They then score candidate documents by measuring whether the same coding agent performs better with the documentation than without it. They test this approach on three Kotlin repositories.

Result: GEPA (the new method) raises documentation scores by 4.9 percentage points on average, while SkillOpt (a prior method) leaves scores essentially unchanged (0.1pp above seed). The GEPA gain matches what prior work reports with the same optimizer. Interestingly, the documents were qualitatively better than the scores suggest - a maintainer found valuable project-specific knowledge in them.

Conclusion: The proposed method provides more meaningful evaluation of repository documentation quality by using real, harder tasks from project history. While the quantitative improvements match prior work, the true value of documentation may be better assessed qualitatively, as the documents contained knowledge only obtainable through project experience. The method also highlights the need for larger datasets to separate signal from agent variance.

Abstract: Coding agents increasingly read repository knowledge from SKILLs --- plain \texttt{.md} files versioned alongside the code. Recent work synthesizes these files automatically, by optimizing the document against a benchmark. A bare repository comes with no benchmark, and the synthetic tasks prior work builds are small enough that a capable agent saturates them with no document at all. We mine harder tasks --- merged pull requests of the repository, reverted at a single frozen base commit; and score a candidate document by whether the same agent does better with it than without it. On three Kotlin repositories, the documents GEPA finds raise this score by $4.9$pp on average, and the ones SkillOpt finds leave it where it started, $0.1$pp above the seed. The GEPA gain matches what prior work reports with the same optimizer, and at the dataset size a single repository supplies it cannot be separated from the agent's run-to-run variance; settling that would take more tasks than one repository's history yields. The documents themselves read better than the score: a maintainer of one repository found in them knowledge one only gets by working in the project.

</details>


### [52] [Assisted Spatial Cognition Through Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12747)
*H. Riaz,J. B. Fernandez,I. Mills,D. Hickey,F. Cleary,M. I. Ali*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A novel end-to-end framework combining LLMs, VLMs, and digital twin technologies to provide spatially cognitive navigation support for visually impaired and neuro-divergent individuals using real-time 3D scene reconstruction from mobile phone videos.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the navigation challenges faced by over 43 million visually impaired and neuro-divergent individuals who lack comprehensive 3D scene understanding in existing assistive technologies, which rely on constrained route-based strategies that hinder user autonomy.

Method: An end-to-end framework that captures video via mobile phone cameras, uses SLAM3R for real-time dense 3D point cloud generation from monocular RGB sequences, applies custom post-processing for accurate multi-viewpoint alignment, enhances SpatialLM for structured 3D representation, and employs locally deployed LLMs for 3D context interpretation, scene descriptions, and distance measurements.

Result: The system demonstrated consistent accuracy in 3D scene interpretation and object localization across diverse video scenarios with various perspectives, looped walking views, and multiple environments.

Conclusion: The framework shows potential as a transformative assistive navigation solution that effectively combines advanced visual perception with spatial reasoning, offering comprehensive 3D understanding to enhance autonomy for visually impaired and neuro-divergent users.

Abstract: Multimodal AI, powered by Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision-Language Models (VLMs), is transforming assistive technologies by enabling simultaneous processing of visual and textual data. This advancement holds significant promise for over 43 million visually impaired and neuro-divergent individuals worldwide who face persistent challenges in navigating indoor and outdoor environments due to limited spatial awareness and insufficient environmental cues. Existing navigation aids often lack comprehensive 3D scene understanding, relying on constrained route-based strategies that hinder user autonomy. In this paper, we introduce a novel end-to-end framework that integrates LLMs, VLMs and digital twin technologies to deliver a spatially cognitive navigation support for visually impaired and neuro-divergent users. Our system captures video input via standard mobile phone cameras, and employs SLAM3R to generate dense 3D point clouds from monocular RGB sequences in real-time. Our custom post-processing algorithm ensures accurate point cloud alignment across multiple viewpoints without requiring predefined reference points. This enhances the capabilities of SpatialLM to produce structured 3D representations, including architectural elements and oriented object bounding boxes. The enriched spatial data is then processed by a locally deployed LLM, which interprets 3D contexts to generate detailed scene descriptions and precise distance measurements between users and surrounding objects. We evaluated our approach across diverse video scenarios featuring various perspectives, looped walking views and captured in multiple environments. The evaluation results demonstrate consistent accuracy in 3D scene interpretation and object localisation, underscoring the potential of our system as a transformative assistive navigation solution that combines advanced visual perception with spatial reasoning

</details>


### [53] [SCQ: Stabilizing Conservative Q-Learning with Sigmoid-Bounded Entropy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12749)
