*Xiefeng Wu,Shu Zhang,Zhaojie Chu,Mingyu Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SCQ introduces a sigmoid-bounded entropy formulation to prevent negative log-probabilities in offline-to-online RL, stabilizing policy optimization while maintaining performance across simulated and real robot tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing offline-to-online RL methods suffer from value estimation instability. While current approaches use pessimistic regularization and architectural normalization, a key overlooked issue is that the standard log-entropy term can become negative, which destabilizes policy updates during training.

Method: SCQ replaces the problematic log-entropy term with a sigmoid-bounded formulation that stays strictly positive. It retains conservative Q regularization and return-based lower-bound calibration while ensuring entropy contributions are always positive, stabilizing policy optimization without sacrificing exploration.

Result: SCQ matches or exceeds baseline performance on D4RL benchmarks under single-demonstration and standard dataset settings. It shows more stable training dynamics across state-based and visual benchmarks, and successfully transfers to four real-robot platforms (manipulation, wheeled, quadruped, and humanoid systems).

Conclusion: The key insight is that positivity of the entropy contribution, rather than a specific score shape alone, drives much of the improvement. A direct clipping intervention removing negative log-probability contributions confirms this. SCQ effectively addresses a fundamental instability source in offline-to-online RL while maintaining strong performance across diverse domains.

Abstract: Offline-to-online reinforcement learning reduces interaction cost for real-world robot learning but suffers from persistent value estimation instability. Existing methods address this through pessimistic regularization, lower-bound calibration, and architectural normalization, but an overlooked source of instability lies in the entropy formulation: the standard log-entropy term can become negative, destabilizing policy updates. We introduce SCQ (Sigmoid-Bounded Conservative Q-Learning), which replaces this term with a sigmoid-bounded formulation that stays strictly positive. SCQ retains conservative Q regularization and return-based lower-bound calibration, stabilizing policy optimization without sacrificing exploration. We evaluate SCQ on D4RL (Minari) benchmarks under both single-demonstration and standard dataset settings, as well as on simulation and real-world visual tasks. SCQ matches or exceeds baseline performance while exhibiting more stable training dynamics across state-based and visual benchmarks, and transfers to four real-robot platforms including manipulation, wheeled, quadruped, and humanoid systems. A direct clipping intervention that removes negative log-probability contributions, together with gradient-matched positive-score controls, indicates that positivity rather than a particular score shape alone drives much of the improvement. Project website: https://scq-rl.github.io.

</details>


### [54] [Unified Agentic Video Editing Across Levels of Complexity and Creativity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12769)
*Surabhi S. Nath,Kim Ferres,Milan Petrović,Lion Schulz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Automated video editing agents for three editorial tasks: scene previews, video summaries, and cinematic trailers, with evaluation of outputs and discussion on automation implications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Video editing is a complex creative process requiring planning and decision-making under multiple constraints, making it a challenging area for automation where creative agency is important.

Method: Developed agentic tooling methods for automated video editing across three distinct editorial tasks that vary in goals, complexity, and creativity levels.

Result: Evaluated the outputs of the automated editing systems for scene previews, video summaries, and cinematic trailers, examining their performance on these varied editorial tasks.

Conclusion: Discussed implications for automation and agency in creative video production, exploring the balance between automated assistance and human creative control.

Abstract: Editing is a core component of video production, requiring creative planning and decisions under multiple constraints. Here, we report methods for agentic tooling for automated video editing across three tasks varying in editorial goal, complexity and creativity, namely scene previews, video summaries and cinematic trailers. We evaluate the outputs and discuss implications for automation and agency.

</details>


### [55] [MPT: Missing Prototype Tracking via Barycentric Reconstruction in Vehicular Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12771)
*Hanju Jang,Gyeongmin Han,Sungmin Lee,Kichang Lee,Chunghan Lee,JeongGil Ko*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MPT is a cross-vehicle federated learning framework that maintains rare-class recognition when vehicles holding most samples of a class depart, by reconstructing the class prototype from privacy-preserving statistics without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In cross-vehicle FL, vehicles may depart before training converges while taking their local data, causing rare classes to become even rarer when departing vehicles hold most samples of those classes. This leads to silent degradation of rare-class recognition as the shared backbone evolves, with no practical recovery due to privacy constraints preventing centralized data access.

Method: MPT reconstructs rare-class prototypes at every FL round using privacy-preserving class-level statistics via three components: 1) barycentric decomposition to track drift shared with remaining-class prototypes, 2) covariance-based residual prediction to estimate out-of-span drift, and 3) adaptive calibration to weigh remaining rare-class samples according to their reliability.

Result: MPT outperforms all calibration and drift-compensation baselines on three vehicle classification tasks with four backbones, achieving 0.516 F1 score on nuImages dataset with only 1% of rare-class samples remaining, without requiring raw data, per-sample features, or retraining.

Conclusion: MPT effectively addresses the challenge of maintaining rare-class recognition in cross-vehicle FL when vehicles depart, demonstrating that rare-class prototypes can be reconstructed from privacy-preserving statistics to prevent silent degradation without violating privacy constraints.

Abstract: Cross-vehicle federated learning enables vehicles to collaboratively improve perception models while keeping locally collected driving data private. However, vehicle participation is transient, and a vehicle may depart before training converges while permanently taking its local data. When this departing vehicle holds most samples of a target class, the class becomes rare in the remaining FL network, and its recognition can silently degrade as the shared backbone continues to evolve. Recovering the class is difficult since the few remaining samples provide a noisy prototype estimate, while FL privacy constraints prevent centralized access to raw data or per-sample features. This paper presents MPT, a cross-vehicle FL framework that maintains rare-class recognition by reconstructing its prototype at every round from privacy-preserving class-level statistics. MPT combines a barycentric decomposition that tracks drift shared with remaining-class prototypes, a covariance-based residual prediction that estimates out-of-span drift, and an adaptive calibration that weighs the remaining rare-class samples according to their reliability. We evaluate MPT on three vehicle classification tasks and four backbones against representative calibration and drift-compensation baselines. MPT outperforms all baselines in rare class F1, reaching 0.516 on the nuImages dataset with only 1\% of rare-class samples remaining, without raw data, per-sample features, or retraining.

</details>


### [56] [Interpreting the predictions of neural network classification based on a Taylor Coefficient Analysis (TCA)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12801)
*Markus Klute,Artur Monsch,Lars Sowa,Roger Wolf*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Taylor Coefficient Analysis (TCA) is a method using Taylor expansion of neural network predictions to understand how input features influence classification outputs, with validation on simple tasks and application to particle physics at CERN LHC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to develop a systematic approach to interpret neural network decisions in event classification tasks, particularly for high-energy physics experiments like those at CERN LHC, where understanding what input features drive predictions is crucial but challenging.

Method: The paper introduces Taylor Coefficient Analysis (TCA), which uses Taylor expansion of neural network predictions (ŷ) with respect to input features (X). The method creates a taxonomy and paradigm for characterizing feature influence, validated on simple example tasks before applying to complex CERN LHC classification problems.

Result: TCA successfully reveals which properties of input features lead to specific prediction values in neural networks. The method builds intuition through simple examples and shows practical applicability to CERN LHC data analysis. The study suggests that for typical LHC experiment complexity, second-order Taylor expansion is usually sufficient to capture the most relevant feature properties.

Conclusion: Taylor Coefficient Analysis provides a powerful, systematic framework for neural network introspection in classification tasks, particularly valuable for particle physics applications where understanding feature influence is essential. The method's effectiveness is demonstrated across different complexity levels, with second-order expansion being adequate for typical CERN LHC experiment requirements.

Abstract: We introduce a rigid and comprehensive taxonomy and paradigm for characterizing the influence of the input feature space $X$ on the predictions $\hat{y}$ of a neural network (NN) used for event classification, based on a Taylor expansion of $\hat{y}$ in $X$. The complete process of introspection we refer to as Taylor Coefficient Analysis (TCA). Based on two simplistic example tasks, which can be easily understood and bencmarked, we illustrate the power of the TCA when it comes to revealing, what properties of $X$ have led to what value of $\hat{y}$, of a given NN model, building up intuition for the method. A more complex application is meant to represent $X$ of a typical classification task at a CERN LHC experiment. Based on this application, we play through the different levels of introspection that the TCA offers and discuss a number of practical aspects for a TCA application typical for the analysis of CERN LHC data. We conclude with a study to support the assumption that those properties of $X$ most relevant for tasks of the complexity typical for a CERN LHC experiment, are usually caught by a TCA up to the second order.

</details>


### [57] [K-Bench: A Benchmark for LLM Unlearning in Agentic Deployments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12808)
*Guangsheng Yu,Yanna Jiang,Qin Wang,Baihe Ma,Xu Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: K-Bench introduces a new benchmark for evaluating LLM unlearning in agentic deployments, showing that current model-level benchmarks (TOFU, MUSE) fail to detect secret leakage across all agent channels (CoT, tool calls, observations, summaries), with published unlearning methods being ineffective when secrets are in model weights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current unlearning benchmarks like TOFU and MUSE only certify forgetting based on the model's final answer, but this model-level certificate doesn't transfer to real-world agent deployments where secrets can leak through multiple channels beyond just the final response.

Method: Introduces K-Bench, a benchmark that inspects all six channels a ReAct agent exposes (chain-of-thought, tool calls, tool observations, elicited summary). The benchmark tests secrets placed in exactly one of three sources (model weights, prompt, retrieval store) and computes K-Score separately for each source, requiring both forgetting and agent usability.

Result: When secrets are in prompts/retrieval stores, TOFU/MUSE report no leakage but deployed agents still leak 22-86% of queries. When secrets are in weights, no published method demonstrably removes them - only input-corruption intervention achieves selective forgetting. Top methods vary across base models, and refusal-tuning resists extraction without verified knowledge removal.

Conclusion: Model-level unlearning certificates don't guarantee agent-level safety - secrets can leak through multiple agent channels even when final answers appear safe. Current unlearning methods are largely ineffective for agent deployments, highlighting the need for deployment-aware evaluation frameworks like K-Bench.

Abstract: Unlearning benchmarks such as TOFU and MUSE certify forgetting by reading the model's final answer, where a model that refuses to answer already counts as having forgotten. We show that this model-level certificate does not transfer once the model is deployed as an agent. We introduce K-Bench, a benchmark that scores LLM unlearning under agentic deployment. K-Bench inspects all six channels a ReAct agent exposes, including its chain-of-thought (CoT), tool calls and tool observations, and elicited summary. A query counts as leaked if the secret appears in any of them. Each experiment places the secret in exactly one of the agent's three sources (the weights, the prompt, or the retrieval store). The K-Score is computed separately for each source and credits forgetting only when the agent remains usable. Clearing the answer channel does not make the secret unrecoverable. On structured retrieval, the secret stays verbatim in the tool-observation channel and the aggregate leak rate is unchanged. When the secret lives in the prompt or the retrieval store, TOFU and MUSE report no leakage, while the deployed agent still leaks it on 22--86\% of queries. When the secret is in the weights, none of the twenty evaluated published methods demonstrably removes it, and only an input-corruption intervention reaches selective forgetting under the evaluated observer. The top-ranked method changes across base models. A refusal-tuning method resists the evaluated extraction without verified knowledge removal.

</details>


### [58] [Scaling Clinical Judgment to Evaluate Medical AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12822)
*Thomas A. Buckley,Zahir Kanjee,Peter G. Brodeur,Byron Crowe,Anthony M. Pettinato,Aashna P. Shah,Adrian D. Haimovich,Liam G. McCoy,Daniel Restrepo,Ethan Goh,Jonathan H. Chen,Laura Zwaan,Katherine E. Goodman,Daniel J. Morgan,Raja-Elie E. Abdulnour,Adam Rodman,Arjun K. Manrai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PrecepTron is an LLM fine-tuned to perform physician-level evaluation of clinical reasoning, enabling scalable and reproducible assessment of medical AI without extensive human grading.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Blinded physician evaluation is the gold standard for assessing clinical reasoning in LLMs but is difficult to scale. Existing studies rely on small physician panels, limiting scientific investigation and reproducibility. There's a need for more rigorous and scalable methods to study clinical reasoning in AI models.

Method: Developed PrecepTron, an LLM fine-tuned using low-rank adaptation (LoRA) of a 32-billion-parameter model trained on a small number of physician examples. Created GRAND-ROUNDS, a large-scale physician-annotated benchmark with 9,217 scores by 11 physicians across seven studies. Used PrecepTron to evaluate LLM responses and reproduce findings from existing studies.

Result: Frontier LLMs in typical "LLM-as-a-judge" approaches often disagree with physicians and each other. PrecepTron fine-tuned on few cases achieves physician-level consistent scoring across tasks. Successfully reproduced headline findings from five influential studies in top medical journals without new human grading. Enabled new research questions about LLM reasoning in medicine that would be infeasible with human grading alone, including measuring diagnostic accuracy when clinical cases are provided piecemeal.

Conclusion: PrecepTron and GRAND-ROUNDS provide a foundation for reproducible, large-scale study of how LLMs reason in medicine, addressing scalability limitations of traditional physician evaluation while maintaining scientific rigor.

Abstract: Blinded physician evaluation has been considered by many to be the gold standard for assessing clinical reasoning in large language models (LLMs). This is difficult to scale; thus, prior studies typically rely on small physician panels, often from a single institution or specialty, which both limits the scientific questions investigated and makes it unclear whether findings would be reproduced with a different set of evaluators. To more rigorously and scalably study clinical reasoning in AI models, here we introduce PrecepTron, an LLM fine-tuned for physician-level evaluation of open-ended responses. PrecepTron was trained using low-rank adaptation (LoRA) of a 32-billion-parameter model on a small number of physician examples. We also release GRAND-ROUNDS, a new large-scale physician-annotated benchmark of 9,217 scores by 11 physicians across seven studies. We show that frontier LLMs in typical "LLM-as-a-judge" approaches often disagree with physicians and with each other, but fine-tuning PrecepTron on a small number of cases enables physician-level consistent scoring across tasks. We use PrecepTron to reproduce headline findings from five influential studies assessing LLMs for clinical care in JAMA, Science, and Nature Medicine without new human grading. Using PrecepTron, we then pose new questions about how LLMs reason in medicine that would have been infeasible with human grading alone, including measuring the diagnostic accuracy of frontier LLMs when clinical cases are provided piecemeal, even token by token. Together, PrecepTron and GRAND-ROUNDS provide a foundation for reproducible, large-scale study of how LLMs reason in medicine. All code, data, and labels are made freely available for researchers.

</details>


### [59] [MedRoundsQA: A Persona and Difficulty Aware Evaluation for Multi-Turn Medical Consultations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12851)
*Youssef Mohamed,Ahmed Heakl,Qinrong Cui,Junhong Liang,Rafiq Ali,Bdour Babillie,Nazira Dunbayeva,Lang Gao,Omar Hussein,Ahmed Nada,Ahmed Mohamed Magdy Mohamed,Jinghui Liu,Salman Khan,Imran Razzak,Yuxia Wang,Xiuying Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MedRoundsQA is a multi-turn diagnostic benchmark that converts standardized clinical cases into controlled doctor-patient dialogues with varying patient personas, revealing significant performance drops from single-turn to multi-turn settings and highlighting equity risks in medical AI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing medical benchmarks are dominated by single-turn, multiple-choice clinical cases that poorly reflect real consultations where clinicians elicit evidence interactively and patient communication varies widely. This gap fails to capture the dynamic, multi-turn nature of real medical consultations.

Method: The researchers created MedRoundsQA by converting 1,387 board-exam cases across 17 specialties into structured 24-slot clinical records. These records were then instantiated as controlled doctor-patient dual-agent dialogues under varying patient personas while keeping clinical content fixed. Cases were classified by difficulty using model-based uncertainty.

Result: Evaluation of fifteen LLM doctor agents showed: (i) Moving from single-turn to multi-turn consultations causes large degradations of 13-39 points; (ii) More turns improves question relevance but diagnostic accuracy exhibits diminishing returns, plateauing after 6-12 turns; (iii) Patient persona differences can shift diagnosis accuracy by 7-8 points (lowest to highest education), revealing equity risks.

Conclusion: Multi-turn benchmarks reveal critical limitations in medical AI that single-turn benchmarks miss, particularly significant performance drops in interactive settings and equity risks from patient persona variations. This highlights the need for more realistic evaluation frameworks that capture the dynamic nature of clinical consultations.

Abstract: Medical benchmarks are dominated by single-turn, multiple-choice clinical cases that poorly reflect real consultations. Practically, clinicians elicit evidence interactively and patient communication varies widely. We introduce MedRoundsQA, a multi-turn diagnostic benchmark derived from 1,387 board-exam cases across 17 specialties. Each case is converted into a structured 24-slot clinical record, and then instantiated as controlled doctor-patient dual-agent dialogues under varying patient personas, with the underlying clinical content held fixed. We further classify cases by difficulty using model-based uncertainty to enable easy-to-hard analysis. Evaluations of fifteen LLM doctor agents show that (i) moving from a single-turn diagnosis on the standardized records to multi-turn consultations causes large degradations of roughly 13-39 points; (ii) more turns reliably improves question relevance, but diagnostic accuracy exhibits diminishing returns and typically plateaus after 6-12 turns; and (iii) patient persona differences can shift diagnosis accuracy by about 7-8 points (lowest to highest education), highlighting equity risks that single-turn benchmarks miss.

</details>


### [60] [Tracing and Coordinating Cross-Layer Influence for Multimodal Model Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12897)
*Pengyang Zhou,Xiaobin Tu,Zhengxi Liu,Rongkun Xue,Haochen Li,Miancan Liu,Ziyuan Chen,Yinggui Wang,Jinkui Ren,Xiantao Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TAC-Merge is a multimodal model merging method that traces and coordinates cross-layer influence between visual and language expert updates, enabling better consolidation of complementary capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard unimodal model merging methods combine expert updates layer-wise, but they overlook how expert updates propagate across layers and interact in multimodal settings. Visual and language updates affect each other when combined, creating coupling effects that existing methods don't address.

Method: TAC-Merge introduces two modules: (1) Multimodal Influence Mapping (MIM) constructs graphs of update effects using Ricci curvature and expert predictions to define a shared fusion objective, and (2) Coupled Merge Control (CMC) models interactions among coefficient adjustments and jointly optimizes regional weights to synthesize one shared model.

Result: Experiments across diverse multimodal tasks show TAC-Merge effectively consolidates complementary expert capabilities and supports generalization to unseen tasks.

Conclusion: Tracing and coordinating cross-layer influence is crucial for effective multimodal model merging, and TAC-Merge provides a systematic approach to address the coupling effects between visual and language expert updates.

Abstract: Multimodal model merging aims to consolidate task experts into a single model that retains their complementary capabilities. Most unimodal model merging methods combine expert updates within individual layers, and multimodal approaches largely follow this design. However, an expert update changes the representations passed to subsequent layers, allowing its influence to propagate across depth and affect how visual and textual information interact. When visual and language updates are combined, later updates act on inputs already modified by earlier ones, coupling their effects. This poses two challenges: (1) how to characterize the multimodal influence of individual expert updates across depth, and (2) how to jointly combine expert updates based on their multimodal influence. To address these challenges, we propose TAC-Merge for tracing and coordinating cross-layer influence in multimodal model merging. It contains two modules, i.e., multimodal influence mapping (MIM) and coupled merge control (CMC). MIM constructs graphs of update effects and uses Ricci curvature together with expert predictions to define a shared fusion objective. CMC models interactions among coefficient adjustments and jointly optimizes regional weights to synthesize one shared model. Experiments across diverse multimodal tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of TAC-Merge in consolidating complementary expert capabilities and supporting generalization to unseen tasks.

</details>


### [61] [EduFair-Bench: Evaluating Pedagogical Fairness of LLM Tutors Across Student Demographics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12949)
*Jiaxu Zhao,Bahar Radmehr,Fares Fawzi,Tanya Nazaretsky,Tanja Käser*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EduFair-Bench is a new benchmark for evaluating the pedagogical fairness of LLM tutors across student demographics, showing that model capability doesn't guarantee fairness and revealing systematic biases in tutoring quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are increasingly deployed as educational tutors, there's uncertainty about whether they support all students equally well. The paper aims to audit whether tutoring quality varies systematically with student demographics like gender, immigration background, first language, and socioeconomic status.

Method: The authors introduce EduFair-Bench, which pairs a multi-domain question bank (math, physics, chemistry) with controlled simulations. A fixed LLM student interacts with tutors across nine demographic levels spanning four dimensions. Tutoring quality is scored on five turn-level metrics and four conversation-level dimensions using an LLM judge validated against human annotations. Bias is measured via statistical tests, and ablations study demographic cues and conflicting information effects.

Result: The smallest LLM was the most consistent, while four more capable tutors exhibited wide demographic gaps with no clear capability-to-fairness ordering. Pedagogy-specific RL training redistributed rather than removed bias. Language- and immigration-related cues produced larger gaps than gender- and SES-related cues.

Conclusion: Model capability and demographic fairness are largely orthogonal in LLM tutoring, highlighting the need for specialized fairness auditing in educational applications. Current advanced models show systematic biases despite their capabilities, requiring targeted interventions for equitable AI education.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as tutors, but it is unclear whether they support all students equally well. We introduce \textbf{EduFair-Bench}, a benchmark for auditing the pedagogical fairness of LLM tutors---whether tutoring quality varies systematically with student demographics. EduFair-Bench pairs a multi-domain question bank (mathematics, physics, chemistry) with a controlled simulation in which a fixed LLM student interacts with each tutor across nine demographic levels spanning four dimensions: gender, immigration background, first language, and socioeconomic status (SES). Tutoring quality is scored on five turn-level pedagogical metrics and four conversation-level dimensions, using an LLM judge validated against three-annotator consensus on 180 tutor turns. Bias is measured via paired Wilcoxon signed-rank tests and bootstrap effect-size confidence intervals. Two ablations (demographic cues conveyed through names; conflicting demographic information between tutor and student) disentangle tutor-driven from student-driven bias. Across five tutors, we find that model capability and demographic fairness are largely orthogonal: the smallest model is the most consistent while the four more capable tutors all exhibit wide demographic gaps with no clear capability-to-fairness ordering, pedagogy-specific RL training redistributes rather than removes bias, and language- and immigration-related cues produce larger gaps than gender- and SES-related cues.

</details>


### [62] [How Good Are Frontier Models at Physics? Expert Re-Grading Reveals Broken Evaluations and Near-Saturation of Leading Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13009)
*Ali Ansari,Haoran Sun,Andy Zeyi Liu,Mark Jabbour,Yongshan Ding,Steven Girvin,Yu He,Sohrab Ismail-Beigi,Aleksander Kubica,Owen D. Miller,Corey O'Hern,Vidvuds Ozolins,David Poland,A. Douglas Stone,Frank C. van den Bosch,Logan Wright,Navid Akbari,Santanu Antu,Kangle Cai,Andrew Calabrese-Day,Mateo Cárdenes Wuttig,Meng Cheng,Barry T. Chiang,Ali Ghorashi,Shouzhen Gu,Haoyang Huang,Zhibo Kang,Lukas Kienesberger,Hantian Liu,Charles Lomba,Zhongling Lu,Wenchao Ma,Rohin E. McIntosh,Evan McKinney,Ivan Rojkov,Xulei Sun,Yarone Meir Tokayer,Naveen Balaji Umasankar,Mira Varma,Leda Wang,Qimin Wang,Tyler Wang,Haoyu Wei,Jinming Yang,Jinchen Zhao,Sherlock Tingrui Zhao,Qinyuan Zheng,Jay S. Zou,Lucas Baker,Arman Cohan,John Sous*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Frontier language models actually perform much better on physics problems than current benchmarks suggest - expert auditing reveals most 'errors' are actually benchmark issues, not model reasoning failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Low reported scores on physics benchmarks suggest models struggle with advanced physics, but this conflicts with domain experts' positive experiences using models in their actual work.

Method: Expert auditing of six physics benchmarks: experts review problem statements, reference solutions, and model responses to distinguish genuine model errors from benchmarking issues (grader errors, incorrect solutions, ambiguous questions). Experts then correct erroneous solutions and repair/exclude flawed questions.

Result: After expert correction, GPT-5.6-Sol's performance dramatically improves: mean@4 rises from 47.3% to 78.7% on HLE-Physics and from 61.0% to 87.2% on CMT-Benchmark. Corrected pass@4 reaches 94.4% on retained CritPt challenges. Similar substantial improvements seen on UGPhysics, PRISM-Physics, and PHYBench.

Conclusion: Current physics benchmarks substantially understate frontier models' ability to solve well-posed physics problems, highlighting the need for more demanding, expert-validated evaluations.

Abstract: Low reported scores on leading physics benchmarks, including those featured in the Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index (2026), suggest that frontier language models still struggle with advanced physics, a demanding test of their scientific reasoning and quantitative problem-solving abilities. Yet this impression does not always align with domain experts' experiences using these models in their work. We revisit these reported findings by evaluating frontier models on six widely used physics benchmarks and auditing them with experts, focusing on text-only problems with verifiable final answers. For each subfield of physics, faculty and graduate researchers with relevant expertise carefully review problem statements, reference solutions, and model responses to distinguish genuine model errors from grader errors, incorrect reference solutions, and ambiguous or underspecified questions. Most audited cases initially evaluated as incorrect reflect these benchmarking issues rather than errors in the models' physics reasoning. We then ask experts to address these benchmarking issues by correcting erroneous reference solutions and repairing or excluding flawed questions. We find that GPT-5.6-Sol's measured mean@4 rises from 47.3% to 78.7% on HLE-Physics and from 61.0% to 87.2% on CMT-Benchmark, while its corrected pass@4 reaches 94.4% on the 54 retained CritPt challenges. Corrected scores are computed on the retained evaluation subsets following expert review. Scores on the audited subsets of UGPhysics, PRISM-Physics, and PHYBench also rise substantially after correction. These findings suggest that current benchmarks substantially understate frontier models' ability to solve well-posed physics problems. Near-saturation on these closed-ended tasks highlights the need for more demanding, expert-validated evaluations.

</details>


### [63] [Diffusion Models and Concept Formation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13047)
*Zekun Wang,Karthik Singaravadivelan,Christopher J. MacLellan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper establishes a formal correspondence between Cobweb (a classic cognitive model of concept formation) and diffusion models, showing that both implicitly perform similar hierarchical categorization with an emergent basic level.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to bridge cognitive science and machine learning by showing that diffusion models, though designed for image synthesis, actually perform similar hierarchical concept organization as Cobweb - a classic cognitive model of how humans organize knowledge into taxonomies with basic levels of abstraction.

Method: The authors analyze diffusion models through four key parallels with Cobweb: 1) both are hierarchical density models, 2) both are hierarchical-Bayesian with Gaussian prototypes, 3) both treat categorization as score-following uncertainty reduction, 4) both have emergent basic levels. They test the correspondence on MNIST and Fashion-MNIST by recovering the diffusion hierarchy through mode-finding and comparing basic levels.

Result: The paper demonstrates that diffusion models implicitly build a continuous, interpolable hierarchy analogous to Cobweb's discrete tree, with both models showing emergent basic levels at intermediate noise levels where class identity commitment occurs in diffusion models.

Conclusion: This reframes diffusion models as cognitive models of concept formation and offers Cobweb a continuous, scalable instantiation, bridging cognitive science and machine learning paradigms.

Abstract: Humans organize knowledge into a taxonomy of concepts with nested levels of abstraction and a \emph{basic level} at which people recognize and name objects with the least cognitive effort. Cobweb is a classic cognitive account of this ability, an incremental learner that builds a probabilistic concept hierarchy by maximizing category utility. We argue that diffusion models, although designed for image synthesis, implicitly perform the same computation. The noisy marginals of a diffusion model are Gaussian smoothings of the data distribution, and the modes of these marginals form a hierarchy that corresponds to a Cobweb tree of probabilistic prototypes in four respects. Both are hierarchical density models, both are hierarchical-Bayesian models with Gaussian prototypes, both treat categorization as score-following that reduces uncertainty, and in both a basic level emerges. We locate this basic level for a diffusion model at an intermediate noise level, where recent analyses show that the reverse process commits to the class identity of a sample. The two models differ mainly in how they represent and learn the taxonomy. Cobweb learns a discrete tree incrementally, whereas a diffusion model encodes a continuous, interpolable hierarchy in a single learned score field fit to the data distribution. We test the correspondence on MNIST and Fashion-MNIST by recovering the diffusion hierarchy through mode-finding and comparing the basic levels of the two models. This reframes diffusion as a cognitive model of concept formation and offers Cobweb a continuous, scalable instantiation.

</details>


### [64] [Anchoring Clinical Events in Time: UID-Preserving Multimodal Reconstruction and Source-Grounded Adjudication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13062)
*Sayantan Kumar,Nicolas Grimaldi,Jack Cummins,Jeremy C. Weiss*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a UID-preserving framework for clinical timeline reconstruction from EHR data, with multimodal revision improving temporal agreement while maintaining event recovery, plus a GAVEL LLM judge for timeline evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical timelines are crucial for treatment-window analysis and leakage-free modeling, but discharge summaries often obscure chronology and structured EHR tables only partially capture patient courses, creating a gap in comprehensive timeline reconstruction.

Method: Proposed a UID-preserving framework that links narrative event occurrences to source spans and retains identity through text-only estimation, structured-evidence retrieval, timestamped source-row grounding, and joint revision. Also introduced GAVEL, an LLM judge for comparing UID-aligned timelines against narrative and structured records.

Result: GLM 5.2 multimodal revision improved temporal agreement without reducing event recovery and performed competitively with clinician annotations. UIDs primarily preserve event retention while source-row linkage supports temporal placement. The framework achieved 43% increased event recovery and demonstrated competitive performance with clinician annotations.

Conclusion: The UID-preserving framework with multimodal revision and GAVEL judge enables better clinical timeline reconstruction with occurrence-level provenance, offering a systematic approach that bridges narrative and structured EHR data for comprehensive patient course modeling.

Abstract: Clinical timelines support treatment-window analysis and leakage-free modeling, but discharge summaries often obscure chronology and structured EHR tables describe only part of the patient course. We present a UID-preserving framework that links each narrative event occurrence to its source span and retains that identity through text-only estimation, structured-evidence retrieval, timestamped source-row grounding, and joint revision. We also present GAVEL, an LLM judge that compares two UID-aligned timelines against the narrative and structured record, to augment prior matching and temporal assessments. Across six open-weight models and 40 mixed-critical-care summaries, the GLM 5.2 multimodal revision, as compared to its text-only variant, improved temporal agreement without reducing event recovery and performed competitively with clinician annotations, while other model revisions showed smaller gains and lower overall performance. Ablations showed that UIDs primarily preserve event retention, whereas source-row linkage supports temporal placement. Blinded human review upheld most GAVEL findings, and controlled adjudication favored multimodal over text-only GLM 5.2 but did not for DeepSeek V3.2. In developing the UID and judge pipeline, we are able to demonstrate 43\% increased event recovery, a framework competitive with clinician annotations, and a system with occurrence-level provenance for both reconstruction and evaluation.

</details>


### [65] [Autonomous Research for Open-Ended Problems: A Case Study on Telecom Ticket Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13073)
*Junghyun Min,Huseyin Uzunalioglu,Mohamed Trabelsi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Autonomous AI research systems can achieve 90% of state-of-the-art performance on open-ended telecom ticket retrieval tasks with modest costs ($200) in significantly shorter time (10 weeks vs 10 months), but require human collaboration due to limitations in intuition and creativity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite progress in LLM-based autonomous ML research systems, current frameworks are limited to narrow problem spaces (like language modeling or biomedical benchmarks) and struggle with open-ended, industry-grade ML problems. The paper aims to explore how autonomous research can be adapted to solve such complex, real-world problems.

Method: The study uses a case study approach focusing on telecom ticket retrieval - an open-ended task with significant degrees of freedom in representation, architecture, and training data generation. The research evaluates both commercial and open-source autonomous agents working on this problem, with minimal human supervision.

Result: Autonomous research achieved 90% of state-of-the-art performance (0.34 vs 0.38 Recall@1) in telecom ticket retrieval. It excelled at narrow hyperparameter optimization but showed limitations in human-like intuition and creativity. The process was much faster (10 weeks vs 10 months of human work) and cost-effective (up to $200 per campaign).

Conclusion: While autonomous research shows promise for accelerating ML research, particularly in optimization tasks, it cannot fully replace human researchers due to lack of intuition and creativity. The optimal approach involves collaboration between human researchers and autonomous frameworks for best results in ML research.

Abstract: Recent breakthroughs in LLM-based systems and their abilities in problem solving and coding have allowed progress in the AI for Science paradigm, potentially replacing human roles in machine learning (ML) research. However, while several frameworks of fully autonomous end-to-end ML research have been proposed, successful implementations of them are often limited to problems with narrow search spaces, like language modeling or biomedical ML benchmarks. In this paper, we explore how autonomous research can be adapted to solve open-ended, industry-grade ML problems, by considering a case study: telecom ticket retrieval, an open-ended task with degrees of freedom in representation, architecture, and training data generation. We discover that autonomous research for open-ended problems with commercial and open-source agents shows both promise and limitations: while autonomous research can excel in narrow hyperparameter optimization, it lacks human-like intuition and creativity and requires operational overhead. Even with minimal human supervision, autonomous research can reach $90\%$ of state-of-the-art performance (0.34 vs. 0.38 Recall@1) in a much shorter time period (10 weeks vs. 10 months of human work) at a modest cost (up to \$200 per Cursor campaign). Our empirical evidence recommends that human researchers and autonomous research frameworks work together for best results in ML research.

</details>


### [66] [Embodied-BenchForge: A Closed-Loop Agentic Workflow for Embodied Benchmark Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13082)
*Baoyang Jiang,Fengchun Zhang,Leyuan Wang,Haotian Li,Yida Wang,Zhe Ji,Jinshan Lai,Xi Ren,Danyang Li,Zheng Yang,Jianwei Hu,Qiang Ma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Embodied-BenchForge: An agentic framework that automates embodied benchmark construction through closed-loop synthesis with verification and repair, creating diverse executable benchmarks for evaluating embodied AI systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agentic benchmark construction approaches are limited - they cover isolated stages, remain specialized to predefined environments/tasks, and lack verification of intermediate artifacts, allowing defects to propagate into final benchmarks.

Method: Closed-Loop Benchmark Synthesis integrating forward artifact synthesis (Skill-Orchestrated Artifact Synthesis with typed reusable skills and dependency graphs) with backward verification and repair (Requirement-Guided Verification and Repair with artifact-specific contracts and provenance-triggered local re-execution/upstream rollback).

Result: Constructed six benchmarks covering diverse embodied scenarios in Offline EQA Track and one interactive benchmark with 220 executable tasks in Interactive Embodied Track; evaluations show benchmarks effectively distinguish model capabilities in observation-based understanding and closed-loop execution.

Conclusion: Embodied-BenchForge enables automated, high-quality embodied benchmark construction with verification and repair, supporting diverse scenarios while ensuring benchmark quality through localized recovery and cross-benchmark skill reusability.

Abstract: Agentic systems offer a promising way to automate embodied benchmark construction, but existing approaches typically cover isolated stages or remain specialized to predefined environments and task families. More importantly, multi-step construction produces dependent intermediate artifacts that are often passed downstream without artifact-specific verification, allowing local defects to propagate into the final benchmark. We present Embodied-BenchForge, an agentic framework that transforms user-specified evaluation intents into complete embodied benchmark artifacts. It formulates construction as Closed-Loop Benchmark Synthesis, integrating forward artifact synthesis with backward verification and repair. Skill-Orchestrated Artifact Synthesis composes typed and reusable skills into executable workflows, while an artifact dependency graph records intermediate outputs and their dependencies. Requirement-Guided Verification and Repair applies artifact-specific contracts throughout construction and uses provenance to trigger local re-execution or upstream rollback when verification fails. Embodied-BenchForge constructs six benchmarks covering diverse embodied scenarios in the Offline EQA Track, together with one interactive benchmark containing 220 executable tasks in the Interactive Embodied Track. Evaluations of representative MLLMs and embodied agents show that the benchmarks distinguish model capabilities in both observation-based understanding and closed-loop execution. Quality assessment and ablations validate benchmark quality and the effectiveness of verification and repair, while repair and skill-reuse analyses demonstrate efficient localized recovery and cross-benchmark reusability.

</details>


### [67] [CMA-OT: Hierarchical Expert Supervision for Dance-to-Music Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13118)
*Jinting Wang,Chenxing Li,Dong Yu,Li Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CMA-OT is a novel dance-to-music generation framework that uses curriculum-guided multi-scale representation alignment with scale-aware optimal transport to bridge the semantic gap between sparse dance cues and dense music composition requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing dance-to-music generation methods suffer from semantic mismatch between sparse dance cues (rhythm/style) and dense music requirements (structure/instrumentation/dynamics), resulting in limited musicality and structural coherence due to poor representation learning from only supervising final audio output.

Method: Proposes CMA-OT with three key components: 1) Leverages external music expert for hierarchical supervision of generator's latent features, 2) Curriculum-guided multi-scale learning strategy for progressive knowledge transfer, and 3) Scale-aware optimal transport alignment mechanism for fine-grained temporal alignment despite structural variations.

Result: Extensive experiments on two datasets demonstrate state-of-the-art performance in rhythmic synchronization, perceptual quality, and overall music generation.

Conclusion: The CMA-OT framework successfully addresses the semantic gap in dance-to-music generation through hierarchical supervision and multi-scale alignment, resulting in superior musical quality and structural coherence compared to existing methods.

Abstract: Dance-to-music (D2M) generation aims to synthesize music that is rhythmically and stylistically aligned with dance videos. A key challenge arises from the semantic mismatch between sparse dance cues, such as rhythm and style, and the dense information required for music composition, including structure, instrumentation, and expressive dynamics. Existing methods typically rely on these sparse cues and supervise only the final audio output, resulting in poorly learned music representations and generated music with limited musicality and structural coherence. To address these issues, we propose Curriculum-guided Multi-scale representation Alignment with scale-aware Optimal Transport (CMA-OT), a novel paradigm that leverages an external music expert to provide hierarchical supervision for the generator's latent features, bridging the semantic gap and enhancing representation learning. To effectively incorporate hierarchical supervision, we introduce a curriculum-guided multi-scale learning strategy that progressively transfers musical knowledge from the expert to the music generator, enabling stable and effective representation learning. Moreover, to accommodate the semantic and structural variations across different expert scales and achieve fine-grained alignment under temporal mismatch, we propose a scale-aware optimal transport alignment mechanism, which models soft correspondences between hierarchical expert representations and the generator's latent features. Extensive experiments on two datasets demonstrate that CMA-OT achieves state-of-the-art performance in rhythmic synchronization, perceptual quality, and overall music generation.

</details>


### [68] [A Hybrid LSTM-XGBoost Framework for Multi-Horizon Stock Return Prediction Across Diversified Equity Portfolios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13125)
*Seif ElDein Mostafa,Yahia Ahmed,Farah Datwish,Marwa Solayman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Hybrid LSTM+XGBoost model for multi-horizon stock return prediction achieves significantly lower RMSE than standalone LSTM and matches/surpasses XGBoost baseline, with directional accuracy improving over longer horizons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Equity return prediction is challenging due to non-stationary, nonlinear, and low signal-to-noise ratio nature of financial time series, requiring more sophisticated approaches than traditional methods.

Method: Two-stage hybrid architecture: LSTM (2 stacked layers, 64 units) processes 60-day windows of 5 market features to create temporal embeddings; these embeddings are concatenated with 14 technical indicators and fed into XGBoost regressor tuned via 3-fold CV grid search; trained on multi-stock pooled corpus with chronological splits and per-stock normalization.

Result: Hybrid model achieves test RMSE of 0.0949 on 30-day horizon (1/3 of standalone LSTM RMSE), matches/surpasses XGBoost-only baseline; directional accuracy increases with horizon length (97.6% at 365 days), though partly tracks high base rate of positive long-horizon returns; short-horizon above-base-rate gap is more informative signal.

Conclusion: The hybrid LSTM+XGBoost architecture effectively combines sequential learning with gradient boosting for improved multi-horizon stock return prediction, with particularly strong performance on short horizons; proposed composite investment scoring framework can support portfolio decision-making.

Abstract: Accurate prediction of equity returns remains a major challenge in computational finance due to the non-stationary, nonlinear, and low signal-to-noise ratio nature of financial time series. This paper proposes a hybrid two-stage architecture that combines a long short-term memory (LSTM) network with an XGBoost gradient-boosted regressor for multi-horizon stock return prediction across a diversified panel of 14 U.S. equities spanning six industry sectors. The LSTM component, comprising two stacked layers with 64 hidden units, processes 60-day sliding windows of five sequential market features to produce 64-dimensional temporal embeddings that encode learned sequential market dynamics. These embeddings are concatenated with 14 hand-crafted technical indicators to form a 78-dimensional hybrid feature vector, which is subsequently passed to an XGBoost regressor tuned via 3-fold cross-validation grid search. The framework is trained on a multi-stock pooled corpus using strict chronological splits and per-stock MinMaxScaling to prevent look-ahead bias, and evaluated across four prediction horizons of 30, 90, 252, and 365 trading days. Experimental results demonstrate that the hybrid model achieves a test RMSE of 0.0949 on the 30-day horizon, roughly one-third that of the standalone LSTM baseline, while marginally matching or surpassing the XGBoost-Only baseline across the majority of stocks. Directional accuracy rises with horizon length, reaching 97.6% at 365 days; we show, however, that this largely tracks the high base rate of positive long-horizon returns in the sample, and we therefore benchmark directional accuracy against a naive always-positive predictor and treat the above-base-rate gap at short horizons as the more informative signal. A composite investment scoring framework derived from multi-horizon predictions is further proposed to support portfolio ranking and decision support.

</details>


### [69] [Rethinking Heterogeneous System Disaggregation for Subquadratic Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13134)
*Arya Tschand,Yaosheng Fu,Vikram Sharma Mailthody,Nicolai Oswald,Po-An Tsai,Ritchie Zhao,Oreste Villa,Vijay Janapa Reddi,Karu Sankaralingam*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SQD is a fine-grained heterogeneous disaggregation scheme that splits LLM decode by quadratic vs subquadratic attention layers rather than by operator type, achieving significant throughput and energy efficiency gains on emerging heterogeneous systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing systems use dense attention-centric disaggregation decisions, but frontier language models increasingly use subquadratic attention to reduce memory and compute requirements while maintaining accuracy. The unique arithmetic intensity and memory footprint characteristics of subquadratic attention LLMs create opportunities for more efficient disaggregation schemes.

Method: SQD (SubQuadratic Disaggregation) introduces a fine-grained heterogeneous disaggregation scheme that splits decode based on quadratic vs subquadratic attention layers rather than operator type. For sparse attention LLMs, it disaggregates decode into top-k selection (which indexes through full KV cache) and top-k attention plus FFN (with static memory footprint). For linear and sliding-window attention LLMs, it disaggregates into dense attention layers and subquadratic attention layers plus FFN.

Result: On an adjusted 8xB200 heterogeneous system proxy, SQD achieved average tokens/J improvements of 53% on GLM 5.2, 31% on Nemotron 3 Ultra, and 56% on Gemma 4 31B over GPU-only baselines. In an analytical model of Rubin plus LPX system with fixed power budgets, it showed 1.2x to 1.5x tighter achievable latencies and up to 3.6x higher throughput over attention-FFN disaggregation baselines.

Conclusion: Disaggregating inference around the unique characteristics of subquadratic attention LLMs can achieve significant performance and efficiency gains. SQD demonstrates that attention-type-based disaggregation is more effective than operator-type-based disaggregation, providing architectural insights for next-generation heterogeneous systems serving subquadratic attention models.

Abstract: Frontier language models are more aggressively using subquadratic attention to reduce the memory footprint and compute requirements during inference while still delivering frontier accuracy. While existing systems make dense attention-centric disaggregated serving decisions, we show that disaggregating inference around the unique arithmetic intensity and memory footprint of subquadratic attention LLMs can achieve significant throughput and energy efficiency gains on emerging DRAM-based and SRAM-only heterogeneous systems.
  We introduce SQD (SubQuadratic Disaggregation), a fine-grained heterogeneous disaggregation scheme that splits decode by quadratic and subquadratic attention rather than by operator type, and that applies across subquadratic attention variants. For sparse attention LLMs, we disaggregate decode into top-k selection, which must index through the full KV, and top-k attention plus FFN, which have static memory footprints. For linear and sliding-window attention LLMs, we disaggregate decode into dense attention layers and subquadratic attention layers plus FFN. In an adjusted 8xB200 heterogeneous system proxy, we observe average tokens/J improvements of 53% on GLM 5.2, 31% on Nemotron 3 Ultra, and 56% on Gemma 4 31B over the strongest GPU-only baselines. In an analytical model of a Rubin plus LPX system with fixed power budgets, we observe 1.2x to 1.5x tighter achievable latencies and up to 3.6x higher throughput over the best baseline of attention-FFN disaggregation. Our experiments also reveal architectural insights on chip and interconnect provisioning for next-generation heterogeneous systems serving subquadratic attention.

</details>


### [70] [Evidence-Aligned Local Composition of Discrete Experts for Sequence Restoration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05801)
*Mohammad Panahazari,Usman A. Khan,Shuchin Aeron*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Paper introduces evidence-aligned local composition, a method to restore corrupted documents by inferring position-wise weights over domain experts without training or region labels, using denoising losses and smoothing to handle mixed compositions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When documents are corrupted and only frozen domain experts are available, restoration requires deciding both what is missing and which expert to trust at each position, without region labels or trained routers at test time. Documents often contain compositions from different domains (e.g., README files with prose, code, and configuration).

Method: Evidence-aligned local composition infers soft, position-wise weighting over experts from marginal evidence of corrupted observation under a given corruption model. It estimates evidence from experts' own denoising losses and smooths weights across positions. The method works without region labels or trained routers.

Result: Across categorical simulator, byte-level experts, and experts fine-tuned from a 1.3B discrete flow-matching model, inferred weights track true regions at 0.85 field accuracy on naturally mixed scientific documents and 0.98 on constructed mixtures with lexically disjoint regions. Restoration improves over single global weight when experts are distinct and reduces to it when they converge.

Conclusion: Evidence-aligned local composition effectively handles document restoration from mixed domains using frozen experts without supervision, adaptively weighing experts based on local evidence and tracking expert separation.

Abstract: A document modeled as a discrete sequence of tokens can be thought of as being generated from a composition of texts from different domains; a README file, for example, moves between prose, code, and configuration. When such a document is corrupted and only frozen domain experts are available, restoring it requires deciding both what is missing and which expert to trust at each position, at test time and without region labels or a trained router. We introduce evidence-aligned local composition, which infers a soft, position-wise weighting over the experts from the marginal evidence of the corrupted observation under a given corruption model, estimating the evidence from the experts' own denoising losses and smoothing the weights across positions. Because the weighting is soft, it recovers a mixture when the true composition is mixed and concentrates on one expert when that suffices. Across a categorical simulator, byte-level experts, and experts fine-tuned from a $1.3$B discrete flow-matching model, the inferred weights track the true regions at $0.85$ field accuracy on naturally mixed scientific documents, and at $0.98$ on constructed mixtures whose regions are lexically disjoint. Restoration improves over a single global weight when the experts are genuinely distinct and reduces to it when they converge, tracking a measure of expert separation.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [71] [Look Before You Leap: Pre-Action Verification for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11957)
*Asaad Althoubi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLM agents can fail silently through plausible but incorrect actions; static verification before execution catches errors with selective refusal, achieving high recall at low false-positive rates for both shell commands and code edits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents execute actions like shell commands and code edits, but wrong actions can fail silently (producing plausible but incorrect results without errors), creating a critical oversight gap that needs addressing.

Method: Uses deterministic static verification before action execution, with oracle-exact checks for shell commands (syntax and binary validation) and different approaches for code edits (content-anchored formats vs location-anchored formats). Implements refuse-when-unsure policy to convert silent failures into recoverable errors.

Result: For shell commands: 95.8% invalid command detection with 10.0% false positives (syntax/binary checks: 0 false positives, half of errors caught). For code edits: line-number edits corrupt 99.1% of files under one-line shift; function-name edits wrong 12.7% of time; overall system achieves 0.958 recall at 7.0% false positives and only 0.01% silent misapplications.

Conclusion: Static verification before execution is an effective, underused form of agent oversight that can detect silent failures with high accuracy; the refuse-when-unsure policy successfully converts dangerous silent failures into recoverable errors at manageable cost.

Abstract: An LLM agent acts on the world by emitting actions: shell commands to run, edits to apply. A wrong action does not always fail loudly; it can fail silently, producing a plausible but incorrect effect that raises no error. We argue that a cheap deterministic check, run before an action takes effect, is an effective and underused form of agent oversight, and we study it across two action modalities in one framework. The idea is to fix an action's correct effect by construction, before any executor runs, so that silent failure is measured directly and the verifier may abstain rather than guess. For shell commands, a static verifier over 9930 commands and 482 tools catches 95.8% of invalid commands at a 10.0% false-positive rate. Its syntax and binary checks are oracle-exact, giving zero false positives while catching half of all errors; the flag check is bounded only by help-text coverage and accounts for every false positive. For code edits, a benchmark of 640 edits over 224 files isolating the apply step exposes a sharp split. Content-anchored formats such as search/replace and diff fail cleanly, whereas location-anchored formats fail silently: line numbers corrupt 99.1% of files under a one-line shift, and function-name edits hit the wrong function 12.7% of the time. In both settings a refuse-when-unsure policy turns silent failures into recoverable ones at a tunable cost in applicability: selective grounding reaches 0.958 recall at 7.0% false positives, and an anchor-and-verify applier records one silent misapplication in 8320 trials (0.01%). We release both benchmarks, the verifiers, and the guards.

</details>


### [72] [Fundamental Dynamical Units for Physics-Informed Structural Inference from Perturbation Time-Series in Networked Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11934)
*Nima Nouri*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces Fundamental Dynamical Units (FDUs) as signed three-node interaction patterns to tackle structural inference in networked systems, enabling physics-informed learning with intervention design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Three major obstacles in recovering interaction structure from perturbation time-series data: combinatorial complexity of interaction architectures, ambiguity of causal attribution under limited interventions, and state-dependent dynamics confounding structural inference. These are structural problems requiring structural solutions.

Method: Proposes Fundamental Dynamical Units (FDUs) as signed three-node interaction patterns that serve as composable primitives, converting the interaction hypothesis space into a finite, constructive representation. Uses FDU-regularized structural inference within a physics-informed neural ODE that transforms structural hypotheses into verifiable dynamical predictions.

Result: The framework enables joint recovery of interaction structure and perturbation-resolved trajectories. Validated on synthetic benchmarks with known ground truth, it supports structural commitment through FDU primitives, motif-prescribed intervention design, and physics-informed learning.

Conclusion: The FDU-based approach provides a principled basis for mechanistically interpretable inference in networked dynamical systems by addressing fundamental structural obstacles through a reductionist representation that links interaction structure to intervention design requirements.

Abstract: In networked dynamical systems, the parameter of primary mechanistic interest is signed interaction structure. Recovering this structure from perturbation time-series data is a fundamental identification problem, compounded by three coupled obstacles: the combinatorial complexity of interaction architectures, ambiguity of causal attribution under limited interventions, and state-dependent dynamics that confound structural inference. Each obstacle is structural in origin and calls for a structural solution. We address these challenges by adopting a reductionist approach, introducing Fundamental Dynamical Units (FDUs): signed three-node interaction patterns as composable primitives that convert the interaction hypothesis space into a finite, constructive, and tractable representation. We show that local interaction structure determines the perturbation conditions required to disentangle direct from relayed influence, making intervention design a structural consequence of the FDU representation. We embed FDU-regularized structural inference within a physics-informed neural ordinary differential equation (ODE) whose governing-equation constraint transforms structural hypotheses into verifiable dynamical predictions, enabling joint recovery of interaction structure and perturbation-resolved trajectories. Validated on synthetic benchmarks with known ground truth, the framework supports structural commitment, expressed through FDU primitives, motif-prescribed intervention design, and physics-informed learning, as a principled basis for mechanistically interpretable inference in networked dynamical systems.

</details>


### [73] [Physics-Informed Conformal Prediction: Embedding PDE Consistency into Distribution-Free Uncertainty Quantification for Neural Operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11935)
*Michael Chin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A framework called Physics-Informed Conformal Prediction (PI-CP) combines physics residuals with conformal prediction to provide distribution-free uncertainty estimates for neural operators solving PDEs, with demonstrated superior performance over existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While neural operators like FNO achieve high accuracy in approximating PDE solutions, they lack rigorous uncertainty quantification methods with provable coverage guarantees, which is crucial for reliable applications in scientific computing.

Method: The authors propose Physics-Informed Conformal Prediction (PI-CP) which embeds PDE residuals into the nonconformity score of split conformal prediction, creating prediction intervals with distribution-free coverage guarantees. They also identify and solve FNO's approximation barrier for Dirichlet boundary conditions using coordinate channels.

Result: PI-CP achieves consistent 89-91% coverage across six physics scenarios (heat conduction, structural mechanics, Darcy flow, Navier-Stokes) while competing methods (MC Dropout, Deep Ensembles) show unstable 82-100% coverage. FNO with coordinate channels reduces error by up to 63x and outperforms CNN and DeepONet by 10-12x.

Conclusion: PI-CP provides a principled, physics-aware uncertainty quantification framework for neural operators with provable coverage guarantees, while addressing architectural limitations of FNO through coordinate channels, enabling more reliable deployment of neural operators in scientific applications.

Abstract: Neural operators such as the Fourier Neural Operator (FNO) achieve remarkable accuracy in approximating solutions to partial differential equations (PDEs). However, providing rigorous uncertainty estimates remains an open challenge. We propose Physics-Informed Conformal Prediction (PI-CP), a framework that embeds PDE residuals into the nonconformity score of split conformal prediction, producing prediction intervals that are (i) distribution-free with provable coverage guarantees, and (ii) spatially adaptive when the PDE residual correlates with prediction error -- tighter where physics is well-satisfied, wider where it is violated. Additionally, we prove that FNO's translation equivariance creates a fundamental approximation barrier for PDEs with Dirichlet boundary conditions, and show that coordinate channels resolve this with up to 63x error reduction. We validate PI-CP across six physics scenarios -- heat conduction (2D/3D), structural mechanics (2D/3D), Darcy flow, and Navier-Stokes -- demonstrating consistent 89-91% coverage for all four Conformal methods, while MC Dropout and Deep Ensembles are unstable (82-100%). FNO outperforms CNN and DeepONet by 10-12x.

</details>


### [74] [Fed-Equilibrium Framework for Topological Pareto Control in Robust and Fair Clinical Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11937)
*Ting Xu,Henry Leung*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Fed-Equilibrium: A federated learning framework that addresses knowledge dominance in multi-center clinical networks through two-stage gradient control, ensuring both security against adversarial attacks and fairness for minority cohorts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The deployment of Federated Learning in multi-center clinical networks faces "knowledge dominance" where high-volume hubs overwhelm minority community nodes, treating distinct clinical patterns of smaller cohorts as outliers. Existing geometric defenses provide security but leave the efficiency-fairness dilemma unresolved.

Method: Fed-Equilibrium implements a two-stage gradient control cascade: Stage I (geometric quality assurance) enforces directional consistency via cosine similarity funnel to filter malicious noise and create a stabilized manifold; Stage II (topological Pareto control) actively modulates verified contributions by identifying the optimal Pareto knee point to achieve topological equilibrium.

Result: Validated on a bi-national simulation integrating Canadian (CNODES) and U.S. (SyntheticMass) registries, the system simultaneously secures against adversarial divergence while accommodating underrepresented signals. The minority U.S. spoke (less than 3% data volume) achieved deep convergence comparable to the data-rich Canadian hub.

Conclusion: Fed-Equilibrium effectively counters "knowledge dominance" by establishing a true "knowledge commons" where global generalizability does not come at the cost of local clinical representation, advancing the paradigm from simple defense to topological equilibrium in federated learning for healthcare.

Abstract: The deployment of Federated Learning (FL) in multi-center clinical networks faces the challenge of "knowledge dominance," where high-volume hubs naturally overwhelm minority community nodes, implicitly treating the distinct clinical patterns of smaller cohorts as outliers. Existing geometric defenses provide a security baseline but leave this efficiency-fairness dilemma unresolved. To bridge this gap, we propose Fed-Equilibrium, a framework that advances the paradigm from simple defense to topological equilibrium. Unlike traditional aggregators, Fed-Equilibrium implements a sequential architectural synergy. It utilizes a two-stage gradient control cascade: Stage I (geometric quality assurance) enforces directional consistency via a cosine similarity funnel to filter malicious noise, creating a stabilized manifold; Stage II (topological Pareto control) then actively modulates verified contributions by identifying the optimal Pareto knee point. We validated this framework on a bi-national simulation integrating Canadian (CNODES) and U.S. (SyntheticMass) registries. Experimental results demonstrate that the system simultaneously secures the network against adversarial divergence while accommodating underrepresented signals. Notably, the minority U.S. spoke (representing less than 3% of data volume) achieved deep convergence comparable to the data-rich Canadian hub. This confirms that Fed-Equilibrium effectively counters "knowledge dominance," establishing a true "knowledge commons" where global generalizability does not come at the cost of local clinical representation.

</details>


### [75] [Efficient AI Model Deployment Using Quantization Analysis Tool](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11954)
*Dwith Chenna,Kanishka Macherla*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A Quantization Analysis Tool built on ONNX that streamlines quantization workflows, provides layer-wise sensitivity analysis and visualization, helping developers optimize models for edge deployment by balancing accuracy, size, and latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As deep learning models are increasingly deployed on resource-constrained edge devices and low-power platforms, there's growing demand for efficient model optimization techniques that reduce model size and computational cost while maintaining high accuracy.

Method: The paper presents the Quantization Analysis Tool built on the ONNX framework for broad interoperability. The tool provides detailed layer-wise sensitivity analysis, visualization of weight and activation distributions, and insights to guide precision selection by identifying layers that are resilient or sensitive to reduced precision.

Result: Experimental evaluations across multiple neural network architectures demonstrate that the tool effectively improves quantized accuracy, leading to improved efficiency in real-world deployment scenarios. The tool provides developers with valuable insights into quantization effects on model accuracy.

Conclusion: The work highlights the tool's capabilities, practical applications, and its role in enabling efficient AI model deployment through robust quantization analysis, helping developers make informed trade-offs between model size, latency, and accuracy.

Abstract: As deep learning models are increasingly deployed on resource constrained devices, the demand for efficient model optimization techniques continues to grow. Effective deployment of AI models on edge and low power platforms requires optimization methods that reduce model size and computational cost while maintaining high accuracy. This paper presents Quantization Analysis Tool, a practical system designed to streamline quantization workflows and support performance efficient model deployment. Built on the ONNX framework for broad interoperability, the tool provides detailed layer-wise sensitivity analysis, visualization of weight and activation distributions, and insights to guide precision selection. By identifying layers that are resilient or sensitive to reduced precision, the tool enables developers to make informed trade-offs between model size, latency, and accuracy. Experimental evaluations across multiple neural network architectures demonstrate that the tool effectively improves the quantized accuracy, leading to improved efficiency in real-world deployment scenarios. The tool also provides developers valuable insights into the effects on quantization on the model and its accuracy. This work highlights the tools capabilities, practical applications, and its role in enabling efficient AI model deployment through robust quantization analysis

</details>


### [76] [Performance, Efficiency and Collapse -- Advantages and Challenges in Offline Post-training of Code LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11956)
*Abhinav Anand,Sanjana Reddy Pachika,Shweta Verma,Mira Mezini*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Offline RL for code LLMs improves zero-shot performance without online sampling, using existing datasets instead of generating new samples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional RL post-training for code LLMs is computationally expensive, requiring intensive sample generation and GPU-CPU communication. There's a need to reduce these costs while maintaining or improving performance.

Method: Uses entirely offline reinforcement learning by leveraging existing datasets instead of generating new samples, avoiding the computational overhead of online sampling during post-training.

Result: Substantial improvement in zero-shot code generation performance with only a few hours of training, with gains observed across models from 0.5B to 7B parameters (though improvement varies by model family).

Conclusion: Offline RL is a viable and efficient alternative to traditional online RL for post-training code-generating LLMs, offering significant performance improvements while reducing computational costs.

Abstract: Post-training with reinforcement learning (RL) is a critical phase in the development of code-generating large language models (LLMs), as it ensures adherence to instructions and the production of functionally correct code. This process typically requires computationally intensive code sample generation from Transformer-based LLMs and substantial GPU-CPU communication for sequence verification. To address these computational challenges, this work examines whether RL-based post-training can be performed entirely offline by leveraging existing datasets rather than generating new samples. The findings indicate that, with only a few hours of training, zero-shot code generation performance of LLMs can be substantially improved without online sampling. Additionally, offline RL produces performance gains across models ranging from 0.5B to 7B parameters, although the extent of improvement varies among model families.

</details>


### [77] [Decoding Mixture Perception through Computational Modeling of Component Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11958)
*Fei Wang,Xiaoya Xie,Junfei Liu,Huihao Wang,Yixiao Wang,Yintao Wang,Yi Li,Hao Dong,Xing Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A bio-inspired deep learning framework accurately recognizes olfactory perception of complex molecular mixtures by modeling neural responses and transferring knowledge from molecular associations to guide mixture perception.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Olfaction remains crucial for danger discrimination, emotional experience, and memory formation, but recognizing olfactory characteristics of multi-molecule mixtures is challenging due to mixture complexity, concentration-dependent saturation effects, and receptor-specific activation thresholds.

Method: Proposed a bio-inspired deep learning framework that constructs neural response curves for molecule-receptor interactions, develops a fusion strategy integrating attention-weighted multi-receptor curves with concentration-dependent multi-molecule curves, and transfers knowledge from molecular associations to guide mixture perception recognition.
