Result: TD3 achieved higher average tumor reduction across the heterogeneous patient cohort, while DQN yielded tighter inter-patient dosing consistency. This reveals an efficacy-consistency trade-off: better tumor control (TD3) vs. more uniform dosing strategies (DQN). DRL policies showed complementary benefits to open-loop optimal control.

Conclusion: Simulation-trained DRL can learn effective state-dependent feedback dosing policies that complement open-loop optimal control, though real-world clinical translation will require addressing partial observability and noisy measurements through estimation techniques.

Abstract: Designing effective chemotherapy regimens is hindered by tumor heterogeneity and drug resistance, which complicate the deployment of patient-specific model-based optimal control across diverse populations. We develop and compare closed-loop deep reinforcement learning (DRL) dosing policies with continuous (TD3) and discrete (DQN) action spaces trained on a high-dimensional heterogeneous tumor model. The DRL policies are benchmarked against a Pontryagin's Maximum Principle (PMP)-derived open-loop benchmark. We assess generalization under parametric heterogeneity using a 100-patient virtual cohort with plus or minus 10 percent uniform perturbations in growth and drug-sensitivity parameters. Across this cohort, TD3 achieves higher average tumor reduction, while DQN yields tighter inter-patient dosing consistency, revealing a clear efficacy-consistency trade-off in this study. Our simulations assume full observation of all tumor subpopulations; translation to sparse and noisy clinical measurements will require partial-observability formulations and/or state estimation. Overall, the results show that simulation-trained DRL can learn state-dependent feedback dosing policies that complement open-loop optimal control benchmarks.

</details>


### [104] [Reinforcement Learning over Patient Trajectories for Clinical Reasoning in EHR Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12277)
*Yuxin Xiao,Sheng Zhang,Chandan Singh,Tristan Naumann,Hoifung Poon,Jianfeng Gao,Xiaodong Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RL fine-tuning enhances EHR foundation models' clinical reasoning by treating them as generative policies, improving prediction accuracy, enabling smaller models to outperform larger ones in data-limited settings, and generating more realistic patient trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current EHR foundation models have limited clinical reasoning capabilities due to being constrained by next-token prediction on incomplete EHR data, which restricts their effectiveness in complex clinical prediction tasks.

Method: Proposes a reinforcement learning fine-tuning framework that treats EHR foundation models as generative policies over patient trajectories. Formulates clinical prediction tasks as event-conditioned, time-windowed reasoning problems and designs time-aware, rollout-sensitive rewards to handle finite rollout lengths and temporally inconclusive outcomes.

Result: RL fine-tuning consistently improves performance over pre-trained backbones and strong baselines. Enables smaller models to surpass larger pre-trained models in data-limited regimes and induces positive transfer across tasks. RL-fine-tuned models generate trajectories with stronger structural/semantic alignment to ground truth and greater downstream utility.

Conclusion: RL fine-tuning is an effective approach to enhance the clinical reasoning capabilities of EHR foundation models, overcoming limitations of next-token prediction and enabling more robust performance across clinical prediction tasks, especially in resource-constrained scenarios.

Abstract: Electronic health record (EHR) foundation models trained on longitudinal patient trajectories have demonstrated strong performance across diverse clinical prediction tasks. However, their clinical reasoning capabilities remain constrained by next-token prediction on limited and incomplete EHR data. To address this, we propose a reinforcement learning (RL) fine-tuning framework that treats EHR foundation models as generative policies over patient trajectories. We formulate common clinical prediction problems (e.g., hospital readmission) as event-conditioned, time-windowed reasoning tasks. We then design time-aware, rollout-sensitive rewards to account for finite rollout lengths and temporally inconclusive outcomes. We find that RL fine-tuning consistently improves over pre-trained backbones and strong baselines. Notably, it enables smaller models to surpass larger pre-trained models in data-limited regimes and induces positive transfer across tasks. Further analysis shows that RL fine-tuned models generate trajectories with stronger structural and semantic alignment to ground truth and greater downstream utility.

</details>


### [105] [Amortized Low-Rank Adaptation for Model-Based Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12278)
*Fernando Palafox,David Fridovich-Keil*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CLAW: A method for efficient test-time adaptation of world models using hypernetwork-generated LoRA adapters, achieving better performance than gradient-based adaptation and in-context learning with minimal data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World models become inaccurate when environments change, and existing adaptation methods face trade-offs: in-context learning is cheap but limited, while gradient-based adaptation is expressive but computationally expensive. Need a method that balances expressivity and efficiency for adapting to unknown test-time environments with minimal data.

Method: CLAW uses a hypernetwork to generate low-rank (LoRA) adapters for world models at test time. During pretraining, the method simulates adaptation to diverse environments and jointly trains both the hypernetwork and base world model. At test time, the base model is frozen, and a forward pass of the hypernetwork generates adapters from a small batch of test-time transitions.

Result: CLAW outperforms both gradient-based adaptation and in-context learning during online adaptation using only seconds of test-time data. It avoids overfitting in data-scarce regimes, its advantage comes from expressive adapters rather than just context conditioning, and joint pretraining of hypernetwork with base model outperforms post-hoc training.

Conclusion: CLAW successfully addresses the expressivity-efficiency trade-off in world model adaptation by combining hypernetwork-generated LoRA adapters with joint pretraining, enabling effective adaptation to unknown environments with minimal test-time data.

Abstract: World models let agents plan by predicting the consequences of their actions, but changes in the environment can make them inaccurate. We study the problem of adapting a world model to an unknown test-time environment, drawn from a known environment family, using only a few episodes of interaction. Existing approaches trade off computational cost against expressivity, i.e., the range of models a method can produce. For example, in-context learning is computationally cheap but limited in expressivity, and gradient-based adaptation is expressive but computationally expensive. We present CLAW (Context-conditioned Low-rank Adaptation of World models), which addresses this tradeoff by using a hypernetwork to generate low-rank (LoRA) adapters at test time. During pretraining, we simulate adaptation to a variety of environments and jointly train the hypernetwork and base world model. At test time, we freeze the base model and use a forward pass of the hypernetwork to generate adapters from a small batch of test-time transitions. We evaluate CLAW in locomotion and manipulation environment families that vary in dynamics, embodiment, and reward. We show that, using only seconds of test-time data, CLAW outperforms gradient-based adaptation and in-context learning during online adaptation. We also show that CLAW avoids overfitting in data-scarce regimes, that its advantage comes from the expressive adapters rather than context conditioning, and that pretraining the hypernetwork jointly with the base model outperforms training it post hoc.

</details>


### [106] [FRIST: FMRI Representation Informed Shared-space Training Improves EEG-only Individual-Finger BCI Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12298)
*Jintao Zhang,Yidan Ding,Joshua Kosnoff,Maxim Karrenbach,Hanwen Wang,Bin He*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FRIST: fMRI-guided EEG decoding framework for finger-level BCI that improves EEG-only decoding performance by leveraging fMRI spatial information through a two-stage shared-space training approach.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Individual finger decoding from scalp EEG is challenging due to spatially close finger representations in sensorimotor cortex and volume conduction effects that blur EEG signals. High-resolution fMRI could provide useful spatial constraints but isn't suitable for real-time BCI applications.

Method: Two-stage framework: 1) learns fMRI-informed spectral projections from simultaneous EEG-fMRI recordings, 2) uses fMRI-derived class geometry to guide residual refinement of EEG predictions. Transfers information through shared finger labels without requiring paired trials, uses only EEG at inference.

Result: Significant accuracy improvements over EEG-only baselines across multiple conditions: two-class ME from 66.93% to 74.53%, three-class ME from 44.83% to 56.58%, two-class MI from 80.78% to 85.63%, three-class MI from 60.93% to 69.90%. Generalizes across EEG backbones and works even without target participant's own fMRI data.

Conclusion: fMRI provides valuable spatial constraints for EEG representation learning. FRIST offers effective multimodal strategy integrating fMRI's spatial specificity with EEG's real-time applicability, improving noninvasive EEG-based finger-level BCI decoding.

Abstract: Finger-level motor decoding is important for naturalistic brain-computer interface (BCI) control, yet individual-finger decoding from scalp electroencephalography (EEG) remains challenging because finger representations are spatially close in the sensorimotor cortex and blurred by volume conduction. Leveraging the high spatial resolution of functional MRI (fMRI), we introduce fMRI Representation-Informed Shared-Space Training (FRIST), a two-stage EEG decoding framework that first learns fMRI-informed spectral projections from simultaneous EEG-fMRI recordings and then uses fMRI-derived class geometry to guide residual refinement of EEG predictions. FRIST transfers information across recordings through shared finger labels without requiring paired trials and uses only EEG at inference. We evaluated 12 able-bodied participants during movement execution (ME) and motor imagery (MI) under two-class and three-class chronological session-held-out decoding simulating the online scenario. Using EEGNet as the EEG feature extractor, FRIST increased group average accuracy from 66.93% to 74.53% for two-class ME, from 44.83% to 56.58% for three-class ME, from 80.78% to 85.63% for two-class MI, and from 60.93% to 69.90% for three-class MI compared with the EEG-only EEGNet baseline. FRIST is also shown to improve EEG-only decoding when the target participant's own fMRI data were unavailable. FRIST also generalized across multiple EEG decoding backbones, reaching 87.40% in two-class MI and 72.54% in three-class MI with EEG Conformer as the EEG feature extractor. These findings indicate that fMRI provide useful spatial constraints for EEG representation learning. FRIST improves noninvasive EEG-based finger-level BCI decoding, offering a multimodal strategy for integrating the spatial specificity of fMRI with real-time applicability of EEG.

</details>


### [107] [Sampling via Decision-Flow: Training-Free Extraction of Improved Latent Reasoning Paths in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12317)
*Zhendong Mi,Shaoyi Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DF-Sample is a training-free inference method that discovers high-quality latent reasoning paths in pretrained LLMs by building hierarchical reasoning trees with global trajectory evaluation, outperforming both conventional sampling and trained RL methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether RL fine-tuning creates genuinely new reasoning capabilities or merely reshapes expression of latent knowledge, and to unlock high-reward reasoning trajectories that exist in base models without costly RL training.

Method: Decision-Flow Sampling (DF-Sample): training-free, data-free inference framework that constructs hierarchical reasoning trees, scores terminal nodes for quality, and back-propagates utilities to inform intermediate branching decisions through explicit global trajectory evaluation.

Result: DF-Sample achieves 45.6% accuracy on GPQA (vs 38.9% for power sampling, 39.9% for GRPO), consistently outperforms baselines across 3 models and 4 benchmarks, demonstrating that training-free methods can surpass trained RL approaches.

Conclusion: Substantial latent reasoning potential exists in pretrained base models, and explicit global trajectory evaluation during inference can recover high-quality but low-probability reasoning chains that standard decoding overlooks, without requiring RL fine-tuning.

Abstract: A central question in LLM reasoning is whether reinforcement learning (RL) instills genuinely new capabilities or merely reshapes how existing knowledge is expressed during inference. Building on the distribution-sharpening hypothesis, which holds that RL reallocates probability mass toward high-reward trajectories already latent in base models, we ask: can we unlock those latent paths without costly RL fine-tuning? We present Decision-Flow Sampling (DF-Sample), a training-free, data-free inference-time framework that constructs a hierarchical reasoning tree, scores terminal nodes for quality, and back-propagates utilities to inform each intermediate branching decision. Unlike conventional sampling strategies that make purely local step-wise choices, DF-Sample performs explicit global trajectory evaluation before committing to a path, recovering high-quality but low-probability reasoning chains that standard decoding overlooks. On GPQA, DF-Sample achieves 45.6% accuracy, surpassing power sampling (38.9%) and GRPO (39.9%), showing that a training-free method can outperform a trained one. Across three models and four benchmarks, DF-Sample consistently outperforms baselines, indicating substantial latent reasoning potential in pretrained base models.

</details>


### [108] [Simulating Disengaged Students to Evaluate LLM-based Tutors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12331)
*Xianghui Meng,Jionghao Lin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DAS2 is a reproducible protocol for simulating student disengagement states to evaluate AI tutor performance before deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Simulated students are useful for evaluating tutoring strategies, but must account for disengaged behaviors (gaming, wheel-spinning, off-task) since tutors need different responses for different learner states.

Method: Developed Disengagement-Aware Student Simulators (DAS2) modeling five learner states. Used ASSISTments09 data with two coders labeling 100 sessions, achieving high agreement. Created rule-based labeling system and evaluated using both prompt-only GPT-4o and fine-tuned Qwen2.5-7B models. Tested on five AI tutors from major families.

Result: Conditioning simulations on intended learner states reduced correctness-rate gaps significantly (gaming: 0.54→0.20; wheel-spinning: 0.51→0.18). Qwen2.5 matched authentic response-time distributions better, GPT-4o generated more distinguishable states. Tutor rankings remained stable across states, but absolute performance varied. Automated evaluation didn't fully align with human judgment.

Conclusion: DAS2 provides a practical pre-deployment framework for evaluating AI tutor responses to diverse engagement states, revealing state-specific tutor performance differences and the need for human validation alongside automated evaluation.

Abstract: Simulated students generated by computational models provide a practical way to evaluate tutoring strategies and pedagogical approaches used by human and AI tutors. However, such simulations should account for disengaged behaviors, including gaming the system, wheel-spinning, and off-task behavior, because tutors may need different responses for different learner states. We present Disengagement-Aware Student Simulators (DAS2), a reproducible pre-deployment protocol that models five learner-engagement states: engaged, gaming, wheel-spinning, off-task, and mixed, and evaluates AI tutor performance across these states. Using ASSISTments09, two coders independently labeled 100 sampled tutoring sessions based on anonymized interaction-log summaries. They achieved 84% agreement (Cohen's kappa = 0.78), and among agreed cases, human consensus labels matched DAS2 rule-based labels in 81% of cases (kappa = 0.75). Conditioning simulations on intended learner states reduced the correctness-rate gap between simulated and authentic sessions from 0.54 to 0.20 for gaming and from 0.51 to 0.18 for wheel-spinning. Fine-tuned Qwen2.5-7B better matched authentic response-time distributions, while prompt-only GPT-4o generated more distinguishable learner states. Evaluation of five AI tutors from the Claude, Llama, Gemini, Qwen, and GPT families showed that relative rankings remained stable across learner states and interaction lengths, while absolute performance varied, revealing state-specific differences in tutor support. Human validation further showed that automated tutor evaluation does not fully align with human judgment. DAS2 provides a pre-deployment framework for evaluating how AI tutors respond to diverse learner-engagement states before deployment.

</details>


### [109] [Theoretical Guarantees for One-Shot Magnitude Pruning and Compute-Adaptive Early Exit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12337)
*Erdem Koyuncu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents a unified framework for compute reduction in neural networks through pruning (static) and early exit (adaptive), with theoretical analysis showing compute-accuracy tradeoffs and scaling laws.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to study compute reduction in neural networks, recognizing the need for efficient computation in large models. It unifies partial vs. full computation approaches through two regimes: static (pruning) and adaptive (early exit).

Method: The authors take a theoretical approach: (1) analyze one-shot magnitude pruning in a single-neuron model with concentration theorems; (2) introduce conditional perceptron for early exit; (3) extend analysis to deep networks studying distortion accumulation with depth; (4) analyze compute-accuracy tradeoffs for frozen-backbone early exit using neural network Gaussian process models.

Result: Theoretical results show: (1) concentration theorems for one-shot pruning with explicit rates; (2) excess generalization error for early exit decays as a power of compute gap; (3) analysis of how pruning distortions accumulate with depth; (4) compute-accuracy tradeoff characterization for early exit. Numerical simulations corroborate the predicted scaling laws.

Conclusion: The paper provides a unified theoretical framework for understanding compute reduction techniques, offering formal guarantees and scaling laws for both pruning and early exit methods, with implications for efficient neural network design.

Abstract: We study compute reduction in neural networks through a unified partial versus full computation view, captured by one-shot magnitude pruning in the static regime and early exit in the adaptive regime. In an asymptotic single-neuron model, we prove a concentration theorem for one-shot magnitude pruning with explicit rates. We also introduce the conditional perceptron for early exit and show that its excess generalization error decays as a power of the compute gap, with an exponent that grows to infinity as the alignment between partial and full computations tends to one. We then extend the analysis to deep networks, characterizing how pruning-induced distortions accumulate with depth and deriving a corresponding compute-accuracy tradeoff for frozen-backbone early exit under a neural network Gaussian process model. Numerical simulations corroborate the predicted scaling laws.

</details>


### [110] [ParaRecover: A Process-Level Benchmark for Error Localization and Recovery in Parallel Tool-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12345)
*Bowen Guan,Zhentao Yin,Yanming Shen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ParaRecover: A benchmark for evaluating error localization and recovery in multi-turn parallel tool-use agents, with a taxonomy of 14 error types and SDE evaluation rubric.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agent benchmarks focus on final task success or tool-call correctness but provide limited insight into agents' ability to diagnose and recover from intermediate execution failures, especially critical in multi-turn parallel tool-use scenarios where errors can propagate across dependent branches and cause cascading failures.

Method: Introduces ParaRecover benchmark with: 1) Fine-grained taxonomy of 14 error types covering planning dependencies, tool selection, and argument matching, 2) 10,626 instances across two difficulty levels, 3) SDE rubric (Structural integrity, Diagnostic reasoning, Evolutionary strategy) for process-oriented evaluation, 4) Experiments across more than ten mainstream LLMs.

Result: Experiments reveal that even state-of-the-art models struggle with multi-turn error propagation, implicit tool-use failures, and precise replanning. The SDE rubric provides effective supervision signals for improving agents' reflective recovery capabilities.

Conclusion: ParaRecover addresses critical gaps in agent evaluation by focusing on error recovery capabilities, with the SDE rubric offering valuable process-level assessment that can guide improvements in agents' ability to handle complex parallel tool-use scenarios with cascading failures.

Abstract: Existing agent benchmarks mainly evaluate final task success or tool-call correctness, providing limited insight into whether agents can reliably diagnose and recover from intermediate execution failures. This limitation becomes particularly critical in multi-turn parallel tool-use scenarios, where errors may propagate across dependent branches and trigger cascading failures. We introduce ParaRecover, a process-level benchmark for evaluating error localization and recovery in multi-turn parallel tool-use agents. Built upon a fine-grained taxonomy of 14 error types covering planning dependencies, tool selection, and argument matching, the benchmark comprises 10,626 instances spanning two difficulty levels. To enable finegrained, process-oriented evaluation, we further propose the SDE rubric, which measures structural integrity, diagnostic reasoning, and evolutionary strategy during agent execution.Experiments across more than ten mainstream LLMs reveal that even state-of-the-art models still struggle with multi-turn error propagation,implicit tool-use failures, and precise replanning. Moreover, we demonstrate that the SDE rubric provides effective supervision signals for improving agents' reflective recovery capabilities. Our data and code are available at https://github.com/gbw206/ParaRecover.

</details>


### [111] [When Connected Does Not Mean Similar: Charting the Homophily Boundary of SNAP-KG for Streaming Entity Integration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12356)
*Jui-Chien Lin,Oshani Seneviratne*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SNAP-KG's effectiveness depends on having at least one homophilous relation (where connected nodes share class labels); performance drops sharply in fully heterophilous graphs, revealing a shared limitation across the method family rather than SNAP-KG-specific issue.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the limitations of SNAP-KG framework when applied outside its original evaluation setting, particularly examining what happens when no homophilous relations are available in multi-view knowledge graphs.

Method: Extended evaluation of SNAP-KG to three heterophilous graphs (Texas, Wisconsin, Chameleon), measured edge homophily of every view, compared performance when no homophilous views are available with transductive baselines from original evaluation.

Result: Clustering quality drops sharply for both SNAP-KG and baselines when no homophilous view is available. Performance is determined by relation homophily, not number of relations. Multi-view fusion only helps when at least one homophilous relation provides reliable foundation.

Conclusion: The homophily assumption is shared by the entire SNAP-KG method family, not specific to SNAP-KG. Heterophilous multi-view clustering is a separate research problem requiring different approaches, with future work proposing heterophily-aware teacher distillation into SNAP-KG's projector.

Abstract: SNAP-KG is a framework for assigning newly arriving entities to semantic communities in a growing knowledge graph (KG) using only their raw features, with no graph access and no retraining at inference time. It was evaluated on five multi-view benchmarks and a 2.4M-node OGB-WikiKG2 KG. In each of these datasets, at least one graph view is homophilous, meaning that connected nodes usually belong to the same class, and SNAP-KG performs well on all of them. This paper asks what happens outside that setting. We extend the evaluation to three heterophilous graphs (Texas, Wisconsin, Chameleon) and measure the edge homophily of every view. When no homophilous view is available, clustering quality drops sharply for both SNAP-KG and the transductive baselines used in its original evaluation. What decides this is the homophily of the relation, not the number of relations. Multi-view fusion still helps, but only when at least one homophilous relation provides a reliable foundation. The homophily assumption is therefore shared by the whole method family, not specific to SNAP-KG. We argue that heterophilous multi-view clustering is a separate research problem, outside the scope of this work. As future work, we outline how a heterophily-aware teacher could be distilled into SNAP-KG's projector to serve both homophilous and heterophilous KGs.

</details>


### [112] [LatentVerse: A Framework for Understanding Shared and Modality-Specific Information in Multimodal Latent Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12364)
*Majd Alafrange,Samuel Friedman,John Kitonyo,Sana Tonekaboni,Mahnaz Maddah*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LatentVerse is a unified representation analysis platform combining web-based visualization and CLI tools for evaluating latent embeddings in biomedical foundation models, with multimodal analysis capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As latent embeddings become central to biomedical AI with foundation models encoding multimodal data (clinical text, medical images, omics, signals), there's a need to understand their quality, structure, and information content. Existing evaluation workflows are fragmented across custom scripts and isolated metrics, lacking multimodal analysis, accessibility, and reproducibility.

Method: Developed LatentVerse - a representation analysis resource combining: 1) web-based visual analytics platform for accessible, report-driven exploration, and 2) command-line interface for scalable technical workflows. It unifies diagnostics for various representation quality metrics and extends to multimodal settings by decomposing embeddings into shared and modality-specific components.

Result: Evaluated LatentVerse through: 1) controlled unimodal and multimodal simulations, 2) discovery-oriented analyses on real biomedical embeddings, and 3) user study across diverse use cases. The platform successfully supports thorough and interpretable evaluation of latent spaces.

Conclusion: LatentVerse makes foundation model representations more understandable in biomedical and data science applications by providing a unified, accessible platform for comprehensive latent space analysis that bridges web-based visualization with technical command-line workflows.

Abstract: Latent embeddings have become a central data abstraction in modern machine learning, especially in biomedicine, where foundation models are increasingly used to encode multimodal data like clinical text, medical images, omics, and physiological signals. However, the utility and value of these representations depends on understanding their quality, structure, and the information they encode. Existing analysis workflows for evaluating representations remain fragmented across custom scripts, isolated metrics, and most importantly lack multimodal analysis, limiting accessibility and reproducibility. We present LatentVerse, a representation analysis resource that combines a web-based visual analytics platform for accessible, report-driven exploration with a command-line interface for scalable technical workflows. LatentVerse unifies diagnostics for various representation quality metrics and extends to multimodal settings by decomposing embeddings into shared and modality-specific components. We evaluate LatentVerse through controlled unimodal and multimodal simulations, discovery-oriented analyses on real biomedical embeddings, and a user study across diverse use cases. By supporting thorough and interpretable evaluation of latent spaces, LatentVerse makes foundation model representations more understandable in biomedical and data science applications.

</details>


### [113] [Certified AI Triage of ICU Alarms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12365)
*Mohammed Sameer Syed,Rozhin Yasaei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents a three-way triage approach for ventricular-tachycardia alarm reduction with statistical certification guarantees, achieving strong performance while bounding the risk of suppressing genuine alarms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ventricular-tachycardia alarms have a 71% false alarm rate, but suppressing genuine alarms can delay recognition of dangerous arrhythmias. There's a need for alarm reduction methods that provide statistical guarantees about their safety.

Method: Proposes reframing alarm reduction as three-way triage (retain, suppress, or defer) with statistical certification. Uses i.i.d. event sampling assumptions and provides clustered analysis as sensitivity check. Implements statistical bounds to ensure suppressed alarms contain no more than a user-set budget of genuine alarms with 95% confidence.

Result: With 5% budget, method suppresses 74.8% of false alarms while silencing only 1.5% of genuine ones, achieving AUROC 0.953 and Challenge Score 83.33, comparable to the strongest published systems. Shows multiplicity correction costs limit certification, and demonstrates calibration volume requirements for different budgets.

Conclusion: The three-way triage approach with statistical certification provides a principled method for alarm reduction that balances false alarm suppression with safety guarantees. The multiplicity correction imposes practical limits on what can be certified, making budget selection a key design parameter.

Abstract: In the VTaC benchmark 71% of ventricular-tachycardia alarms are false, but silencing a real one can delay recognition of a dangerous arrhythmia. We reframe alarm reduction as three-way triage (retain, suppress, or defer) and bound the decision this analysis treats as harmful: among suppressed alarms, the fraction that were genuine stays below a user-set budget with 95% confidence, under i.i.d. event sampling. Alarms sharing a waveform record are dependent, so the clustered analysis is a sensitivity check. On the official split a 5% budget certifies in all three seeds, suppressing 74.8% of false alarms while silencing 1.5% of genuine ones, at AUROC 0.953 and Challenge Score 83.33, numerically comparable to the strongest of the eleven published systems. Our central finding measures what multiplicity costs: the correction charges for every candidate, so a finer grid can certify strictly less. Under held-out calibration the 885-cell grid we declared certifies 1 of 15 fold-runs, while choosing the grid on a separate selection partition certifies 8. We project the calibration volume each budget needs, making an uncertifiable budget a design parameter. Finally, adding a learned reliability dimension to the policy grid did not sharpen the certified frontier.

</details>


### [114] [Split Conformal Prediction with Label-Shift-Adjusted Bayesian Scores](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12386)
*Hyeonsu Lee,Juyeon Kim,Erkhembayar Jadamba,Seungjin Choi,Hyunjin Shin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LSA score is a new conformal prediction method that maintains target domain coverage under label shift by correcting Bayesian predictive distributions using importance weighting, producing shorter intervals than existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard conformal prediction assumes exchangeability, which is violated under label shift (changing marginal label distribution while conditional distribution remains stable). Existing importance-weighted approaches use residual-based scores that ignore predictive uncertainty, resulting in uniform-width intervals. Bayesian methods produce adaptive intervals but their predictive distributions are misaligned with target domain under label shift.

Method: Proposes Label-Shift-Adjusted Bayesian Score (LSA score) derived from a posterior predictive tilting identity showing that target predictive is an importance-weighted transformation of source predictive. Uses this to derive direct correction to Bayesian score. Instantiated with Bayesian Ridge Regression where correction admits closed form. Works with any source predictive having tractable log-density.

Result: LSA score consistently yields shorter intervals than residual-based and source-based Bayesian scores on molecular property prediction under controlled label shift. Coverage in target domain remains comparable to baselines. Under stronger shift, all methods show some coverage loss due to density-ratio estimation issues with pseudo-labels.

Conclusion: LSA score effectively addresses label shift in conformal prediction by properly adjusting Bayesian predictive distributions, providing practical method for uncertainty quantification that maintains coverage while producing more efficient (shorter) prediction intervals than existing approaches.

Abstract: Conformal prediction provides distribution-free uncertainty quantification under exchangeability. However, this assumption is violated by label shift, where the marginal distribution of labels changes while the conditional distribution of inputs given labels remains stable. Under such shifts, standard conformal procedures no longer maintain their intended coverage behavior. Existing approaches address this via importance weighting. They pair the reweighting with residual-based nonconformity scores that ignore predictive uncertainty. The resulting intervals have uniform width. Bayesian conformal methods produce adaptive intervals by leveraging predictive distributions. They evaluate conformity under the source predictive, which is misaligned with the target domain under label shift. We propose the \emph{Label-Shift-Adjusted Bayesian Score} (LSA score), a nonconformity score derived from a posterior predictive tilting identity. This identity shows that the target predictive is an importance-weighted transformation of the source predictive. We use it to derive a direct correction to the Bayesian score. We evaluate the method on molecular property prediction under controlled label shift. The LSA score consistently yields shorter intervals than residual-based and source-based Bayesian scores. Coverage in the target domain remains comparable. Under stronger shift, all methods incur some coverage loss due to pseudo-label-based density-ratio estimation. The LSA score is defined for any source predictive with a tractable log-density. We instantiate it with Bayesian Ridge Regression, where the correction admits a closed form.

</details>


### [115] [RiPPLE: Cross-Space Performance Prediction from Early Training for Neural Architecture Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12418)
*Yifan Yang,Zhaoyan Wang,Zheng Gao,Xiaoyu Li,Jiaojiao Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RiPPLE is a neural architecture search method that uses early-training learning curve extrapolation from a small set of anchor architectures to create surrogate labels, then propagates these labels to rank all architectures in a search space without requiring full training for each candidate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural architecture search is expensive because fully training many candidate architectures is computationally prohibitive. Existing zero-cost proxies have inconsistent ranking quality across search spaces, while learned predictors typically require full training or partial-training features for each candidate, which is still costly.

Method: RiPPLE trains a small set of "anchor" architectures to an early stage, extrapolates their learning curves to create surrogate labels, and propagates these labels over label-free architecture features to rank the entire search space. The method uses early-training signals as labels only for anchors, not as per-candidate features.

Result: Evaluated on twelve benchmark cells from four search-space families and the larger DARTS space, RiPPLE achieves whole-space ranking from a fractional anchor-training budget. The results examine ranking quality, label efficiency, architecture selection, and the roles of key components like readout, coverage, and propagation.

Conclusion: RiPPLE provides an efficient alternative to full training and traditional zero-cost proxies by leveraging early-training learning curve extrapolation from a small anchor set, enabling effective architecture ranking across diverse search spaces with significantly reduced computational cost.

Abstract: Neural architecture search (NAS) evaluates candidate networks, but fully training enough architectures to rank an entire space is expensive. Zero-cost proxies score architectures at initialization, yet their ranking quality varies across search spaces. Learned predictors reduce evaluation cost but typically require fully trained labels or partial-training features for individual candidates. We introduce $\textbf{RiPPLE}$, $\underline{\textbf{R}}$anking v$\underline{\textbf{i}}$a $\underline{\textbf{P}}$refix-$\underline{\textbf{P}}$ropagated $\underline{\textbf{L}}$abel $\underline{\textbf{E}}$xtrapolation, which treats partial training as a source of labels for a small coverage set of anchors. RiPPLE trains these anchors to an early prefix, extrapolates their learning curves to surrogate labels, and propagates the labels over label-free architecture features. The early-training signal remains a label on the anchors rather than a per-candidate feature. Feature, readout, and encoding rules are selected without held-out accuracy and reused across search spaces. We evaluate the method on twelve benchmark cells from four search-space families and on the larger DARTS space. The results examine ranking quality, label efficiency, architecture selection, and the roles of readout, coverage, and propagation. RiPPLE provides a whole-space ranking from a fractional anchor-training budget, with comparisons interpreted under their respective evaluation and cost protocols.

</details>


### [116] [MInTRL: Off-policy Intervention can boost On-policy RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12419)
*Mingyu Chen,Yefan Tao,Gerald Friedland,Xuezhou Zhang,Chris Kong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MInTRL (Minimal Intervention Reinforcement Learning) introduces a method that expands exploration in RL through sparse, local interventions during on-policy rollouts, outperforming standard on-policy and off-policy approaches on math and code benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses the fundamental trade-off in RL: on-policy methods keep training data close to current policy but limit exploration, while off-policy methods can leverage external knowledge but suffer from distribution shift. The key challenge is expanding exploration without sacrificing learnability.

Method: MInTRL uses sparse, local interventions during on-policy rollouts where a judge-intervention policy periodically reviews outputs, replaces erroneous suffixes with short corrections, and returns control to the policy. Training uses a sequence-level advantage-regression objective that eliminates importance sampling.

Result: The method consistently outperforms standard on-policy and off-policy baselines across math and code benchmarks. Sparse interventions improve coverage beyond finite-budget on-policy sampling while preserving overall on-policy nature. Ablations show effectiveness with self-intervention and performance peaks at moderate intervention intensity.

Conclusion: Minimal intervention is established as an effective paradigm for enhancing on-policy RL, successfully balancing exploration expansion with learnability preservation through judicious, sparse interventions.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards is typically performed on-policy, keeping training data close to the current policy but limiting learning to trajectories that the policy can discover itself. Off-policy methods such as supervised fine-tuning, on the other hand, can leverage external knowledge beyond the base model's capabilities, but may suffer from large distribution shift. The key challenge is thus to expand exploration without sacrificing learnability. In this work, we introduce Minimal Intervention Reinforcement Learning (MInTRL), which expands the exploration frontier through sparse, local interventions in otherwise on-policy rollouts. During generation, a judge-intervention policy periodically reviews the current policy's output, replaces erroneous suffixes with short corrections, and immediately returns control to the policy. During training, MInTRL adopts a sequence-level advantage-regression objective that eliminates the need for importance sampling. We show that sparse, local interventions can substantially improve coverage beyond finite-budget on-policy sampling while preserving the overall on-policy nature of the resulting trajectories. Across math and code benchmarks, MInTRL consistently outperforms standard on-policy and off-policy baselines. Ablations show that MInTRL remains effective with self-intervention and across different judge policies, while performance peaks at moderate intervention intensity, highlighting the importance of intervening minimally. These results establish minimal intervention as an effective paradigm for enhancing on-policy RL.

</details>


### [117] [Granularity-Adaptive Credit Assignment for Long-Horizon LLM Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12424)
*Taoran Liang,Yang Liu,Shang Luo,Yingguang Yang,Rongrong Zhang,Yingzong Min,Yulin Huang,Jianshen Zhang,Yongzhi Qi,Kefu Xu,Congjing Ran,Bin Chong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GACA is a critic-free reinforcement learning method for language model agents that adaptively blends step-level and episode-level reward signals based on state-dependent uncertainty, outperforming prior methods on long-horizon tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current critic-free RL methods for language model agents (like GRPO and GiGPO) have limitations in providing appropriate credit assignment. GRPO only uses episode-level signals that can't identify which specific decisions drove outcomes, while GiGPO uses fixed weighting between step-level and episode-level signals, treating all decisions equally regardless of their importance.

Method: GACA introduces a state-dependent adaptive mixing approach that scores each step using negative log-likelihood (NLL) from its own rollout. It blends step-level and episode-level advantage estimates with per-step weights that increase with NLL scores, placing more emphasis on fine-grained signals for uncertain/pivotal decisions and on episode-level signals for routine decisions.

Result: On ALFWorld and WebShop benchmarks, GACA improves task success rates over both GRPO and GiGPO at both 1.5B and 7B model scales, demonstrating better performance in long-horizon language model agent tasks.

Conclusion: The right resolution for credit assignment in critic-free RL for language model agents is state-dependent, and GACA's adaptive uncertainty-based mixing provides superior performance by dynamically adjusting signal granularity based on decision criticality.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning is now the standard way to train large language model agents on long-horizon tasks, where dozens of interdependent actions precede a single sparse reward. Critic-free, group-relative methods such as GRPO suit this regime, but they broadcast one trajectory-level scalar to every step and cannot say which decision drove the outcome. GiGPO recovers a step-level signal by grouping time steps that share an anchor state, yet it merges the step- and episode-level estimates under one fixed weight, spending the same resolution on a pivotal branching decision as on a routine, near-deterministic transition. We argue that the right resolution is state-dependent, and propose GACA, a critic-free estimator whose granularity follows an uncertainty-based criticality proxy. GACA scores every step by the negative log-likelihood its own rollout already records, then blends the two advantages with a per-step weight that grows with that score, so the gradient places more weight on the fine-grained signal at above-average NLL and on the episode-level signal below it. We derive an exact risk decomposition for the implemented mixture and show that sufficiently small modulation improves on fixed mixing under positive directional alignment. A separate conditional result bounds local action-value variation using expected NLL, while an error-projection analysis characterizes when mixing adds value beyond scalar uncertainty reweighting. On ALFWorld and WebShop, GACA improves task success over GRPO and GiGPO at both 1.5B and 7B scales.

</details>


### [118] [Observation-Anchored Selective Assimilation for Longitudinal Tumor-State Proxy Forecasting in Post-Treatment Glioma](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12435)
*Yeonjae Jung,Minwoo Shin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A digital-twin framework for glioma forecasting using MRI, featuring observation-aware updates with selective assimilation that maintains forecasting accuracy while incorporating intermediate observations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-treatment MRI in glioma patients provides serial observations for tumor-state monitoring, but variable appearances and trajectories make accurate forecasting challenging. Current approaches need to better incorporate intermediate observations to update patient-specific tumor states.

Method: Formulated forecasting as observation-aware digital-twin updates where intermediate observations anchor patient-specific states. Used SegMamba-based single-step forecaster to predict updates from multimodal source-state tensors, with Observation-Anchored Selective Assimilation (OASA) that retains observed intermediate proxies as state anchors and selectively applies updates using validation-selected tiered case-level rules and voxel-wise soft gates.

Result: OASA maintained Dice at τ=0.2 comparable to persistence (0.6071 vs 0.6070) while yielding higher Dice at τ=0.5 (0.4269 vs 0.3981). Calibration further increased Dice at τ=0.2 to 0.6178, increasing false-positive support and reducing false-negative support, reflecting near-threshold support calibration rather than improved biological predictive capability.

Conclusion: The proposed observation-aware digital-twin framework with selective assimilation enables effective glioma forecasting that incorporates intermediate MRI observations while maintaining or improving accuracy compared to baseline methods, with potential for clinical application in longitudinal monitoring.

Abstract: Post-treatment MRI in patients with glioma provides serial observations for updating patient-specific tumor-state proxy estimates, but variable appearances and trajectories complicate forecasting. We formulate forecasting as an observation-aware digital-twin update in which an intermediate observation anchors the patient-specific state. Among 203 patients and 594 follow-up time points, a predefined no-new-treatment criterion retained 120 of 236 candidate triplets, split into 81/24/15 training/validation/test triplets at the patient level. Each time point was represented by a continuous voxel-wise tumor-state proxy map in [0,1] derived from MRI lesion labels. A SegMamba-based single-step forecaster predicted update proposals from multimodal source-state tensors. Observation-Anchored Selective Assimilation (OASA) retained the observed intermediate proxy as the state anchor and selectively applied updates through a validation-selected tiered case-level rule and voxel-wise soft gate. We compared initial-scan forecasting, rollout without assimilation, latest-observation persistence, direct prediction, OASA, OASA + calibration, and morphological dilation. Checkpoints, OASA rules, and calibration thresholds were selected using validation data only. Across three seeds on 15 held-out test triplets, OASA maintained Dice at $τ$ = 0.2 comparable to persistence (0.6071 $\pm$ 0.0025 vs. 0.6070) while yielding numerically higher Dice at $τ$ = 0.5 (0.4269 $\pm$ 0.0079 vs. 0.3981), with a small RMSE increase. Calibration increased Dice at $τ$ = 0.2 to 0.6178 $\pm$ 0.0025, increased false-positive (FP) support (11,836$\rightarrow$18,663), and reduced false-negative (FN) support (22,107$\rightarrow$17,536). This reflects near-threshold support calibration rather than improved biological predictive capability. Code is publicly available at https://github.com/jsudg436/longitudinal-proxy-forecasting.

</details>


### [119] [Beyond the Query: Do Retrieval Signals Improve Adaptive Multimodal RAG Routing?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12437)
*Qiaomu Li,Qiuyuan Zhang,Nong Ming*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Adding retrieval signals to routers doesn't reliably improve routing decisions compared to query-only baselines; the incremental value of retrieval signals must be demonstrated rather than assumed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adaptive RAG systems use retrieval-time signals to decide whether to perform additional steps (retrieval, reranking, multimodal processing). The paper investigates whether these retrieval signals actually provide routing value beyond what the query itself already provides.

Method: Across document, audio, and video RAG systems, researchers compared matched query-only routers with query+retrieval routers while keeping constant: optional actions, router family, training procedure, and evaluation. They systematically tested whether retrieval signals improve routing decisions over query-only baselines.

Result: On held-out final evaluations, adding tested retrieval signals did not produce reliable routing improvements over query-only baselines. While some retrieval signals were associated with whether later steps would help, this predictability didn't consistently lead to better RUN/SKIP decisions.

Conclusion: The main methodological lesson is that retrieval-state features should not be credited with routing value unless they improve over a matched query-only control. Results show routing and retrieval state aren't generally useless, but the incremental value of retrieval signals must be demonstrated rather than assumed.

Abstract: Adaptive RAG often uses retrieval-time signals to decide whether another retrieval, reranking, or multimodal step should run. We ask whether these signals add routing value once the query itself is already known. Across document, audio, and video RAG, we compare matched query-only and query+retrieval routers while holding the optional actions, router family, training procedure, and evaluation fixed. On the held-out final evaluation, adding the tested retrieval signals does not produce a reliable routing improvement over the query-only baseline. Some retrieval signals are associated with whether a later step will help, but that predictability does not consistently lead to bet- ter RUN/SKIP decisions. The main lesson is therefore methodological: retrieval-state features should not be credited with routing value unless they improve over a matched query-only control. Our results do not show that routing or retrieval state is generally useless; they show that the incremental value of retrieval signals must be demonstrated rather than assumed.

</details>


### [120] [3D Digital Twin Visualization of Multiclass GRF-Based Gait Disorder Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12442)
*Nayoung Son,Minwoo Shin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An integrated framework for gait analysis that combines classification of healthy vs. musculoskeletal impaired gait, model explainability using ε-LRP, and 3D visualization in Blender for improved transparency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a need for automated gait analysis that provides both accurate classification and interpretable outputs, particularly for distinguishing healthy gait from various musculoskeletal impairment groups.

Method: The framework uses bilateral ground reaction force (GRF) and center-of-pressure (COP) signals normalized over stance phase. Signals are standardized using training-set statistics. The model employs ε-Layerwise Relevance Propagation (ε-LRP) for explainability and integrates processed GRF signals with model predictions in a Blender-based 3D visualization system.

Result: The model achieved high validation accuracy of 99.00% and test accuracy of 90.07% with session-level split. Class-specific ε-LRP identified contributions across both sides, multiple signal components, and different stance phases. The 3D visualization enables sample-level inspection of gait trials and classification results.

Conclusion: The proposed framework successfully integrates classification, explainability through ε-LRP, and 3D visualization to improve model transparency in automated gait analysis, with source code made publicly available.

Abstract: Automated gait analysis requires accurate classification and interpretable outputs. We propose an integrated framework for classifying healthy gait and multiple musculoskeletal impairment groups using bilateral ground reaction force (GRF) and center-of-pressure (COP) signals. The signals were normalized over the stance phase and standardized using training-set statistics. The model achieved a validation accuracy of 99.00\% and a test accuracy of 90.07\% under a session-level split. Class-specific $ε$-LRP identified positive and negative contributions across both sides, multiple signal components, and different stance phases. Separately, the processed GRF signals and model predictions were synchronized within a Blender-based 3D visualization, enabling sample-level inspection of gait trials and classification results. The proposed framework integrates classification, explainability, and 3D visualization to improve model transparency. The source code is available in the following repository: https://github.com/nyoico/grf-gait-3d-visualization.git

</details>


### [121] [SAGE-Loop: Reliable Closed-Loop LLM-Driven AutoML with Trial-and-Correction and Adaptive Ensembling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12455)
*Junquan Gu,Shibo Cui,Xiangfeng Luo,Hang Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SAGE-Loop is a closed-loop, self-adaptive LLM-driven AutoML framework that improves reliability through multi-round trial-and-repair generation and adaptive ensemble selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AutoML struggles with instant feedback and adaptive optimization during execution, lacks process-level correction mechanisms when pipelines drift into suboptimal/failed states, and has fixed paradigms that weaken execution reliability and structural diversity utilization.

Method: SAGE-Loop performs multi-round generation and validation for trial-and-repair, adaptively selects ensemble strategies for both supervised and unsupervised tasks, and unifies model generation with model usage through a closed-loop system.

Result: Across 20 public datasets, SAGE-Loop consistently improves performance and stability on classification, regression, and clustering tasks, demonstrating ability to recover from execution failures and maintain robust pipeline behavior.

Conclusion: SAGE-Loop addresses the reliability gap in LLM-driven AutoML by introducing closed-loop trial-correction-improvement capability with evidence-based use of model diversity, improving both automation efficiency and pipeline reliability.

Abstract: Automated machine learning (AutoML) is reshaping data-driven science and industrial practice, and as large language models are introduced into AutoML, pipeline reliability becomes as important as automation efficiency. However, existing AutoML still struggles to realize instant feedback and adaptive optimization during execution, so once a run drifts into a suboptimal or failed state, it lacks a process-level correction mechanism. The fundamental pathology lies in its one-way pipeline: intermediate failures are typically terminated or bypassed, while fixed paradigms often strengthen model generation but leave ensemble decisions static, weakening both execution reliability and the controlled use of structural diversity. This indicates that LLM-driven AutoML needs a closed-loop ability for trial-correction-improvement together with evidence-based use of model diversity. To this end, we propose SAGE-Loop, a reliable closed-loop, self-adaptive, LLM-driven AutoML framework that performs multi-round generation and validation for trial-and-repair, and adaptively selects ensemble strategies in both supervised and unsupervised tasks, thereby unifying how to generate with how to use models. Across 20 public datasets, SAGE-Loop consistently improves performance and stability on classification, regression, and clustering tasks. Additional results further show its ability to recover from execution failures and maintain robust pipeline behavior.

</details>


### [122] [A Differentially Private Federated Proximal Optimization Framework for Customer Churn Prediction in Heterogeneous Federated Telecom Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12470)
*Joydeb Kumar Sana,Subrata Chakraborty,M M Manjurul Islam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A differentially private FedProx framework for telecom churn prediction that addresses privacy concerns and non-IID data challenges while maintaining competitive performance with centralized approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Customer churn prediction in telecom faces two key challenges: privacy concerns from centralized data collection (violating regulations like GDPR), and data heterogeneity (non-IID distributions across operators) that degrades federated learning performance. Existing federated approaches struggle with both privacy protection and heterogeneous data.

Method: Proposed DP-FedProx framework combining differential privacy (DP) with Federated Proximal optimization (FedProx). Compared against FedAvg, DP-FedAvg, FedProx, and centralized/local baselines using two telecom churn datasets. Evaluated with seven metrics and used SHAP for explainability analysis.

Result: FedProx-based models consistently outperform FedAvg-based models. DP-FedProx achieves competitive performance with centralized models (small accuracy reduction) while providing privacy guarantees. SHAP analysis reveals DP-FedProx prioritizes revenue group features.

Conclusion: The DP-FedProx framework provides a practical balance between prediction performance and privacy protection, addressing both non-IID data challenges and privacy concerns in telecom churn prediction without requiring centralized data collection.

Abstract: Customer churn is one of the major issues in the telecommunication industry. To predict customer churn, conventional centralized machine learning approaches have been widely used. This centralized approach requires customer data to be stored in a central repository, which raises privacy concerns and may violate data protection regulations. Federated learning addresses this problem by allowing multiple telecom operators to collaboratively train a global model without transferring their raw customer data. However, real-world customer data are often heterogeneous (non-IID), which may negatively affect the performance of standard federated learning. Trained models can also suffer from privacy attacks. To address those issues, we propose a Differentially Private (DP) based Federated Proximal optimization (FedProx) framework. All experiments were performed on two publicly available telecom churn datasets. We trained Federated Averaging (FedAvg), DP-FedAvg, FedProx, and the proposed DP-FedProx framework. For baseline comparison, we also used several centralized and local models. To evaluate the models, we employed seven widely used evaluation metrics. The experimental results show that the FedProx based models consistently outperform the FedAvg based models. Compared with the best centralized model, the proposed DP-FedProx framework achieves competitive prediction performance with only a small reduction in accuracy while providing privacy guarantees. To explain our model, we conducted SHAP analysis which shows that DP-FedProx method priorities revenue group features. These results indicate that the proposed DP-FedProx framework provides a practical balance between prediction performance and data privacy protection.

</details>


### [123] [Temporal Recurrence Favors Fewer Layers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12531)
*Ivan Anokhin,Johan Obando-Ceron,Irina Rish,Sebastian Risi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Recurrent models can trade depth for temporal recurrence - allocating compute across time instead of within each step often yields comparable or better performance with fewer layers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how temporal recurrence affects the optimal compute allocation in neural networks, specifically whether deep within-step computation is still needed when models can carry computation across time steps.

Method: Frame the problem as a compute-allocation optimization, varying within-step depth, expert width, and number of parallel experts per layer across different compute budgets. Compare best observed recurrent and non-recurrent allocations under matched per-step computation on Sokoban and autoregressive FineWeb language modeling tasks.

Result: Temporal recurrence shifts optimal compute allocation toward substantially fewer layers while maintaining comparable or better performance compared to non-recurrent models with similar per-step computation.

Conclusion: Recurrence enables more efficient compute allocation by distributing computation across time rather than within each step, suggesting that deep sequential processing can be achieved through temporal dynamics rather than deep architectures per step.

Abstract: In streaming tasks, recurrent models can carry latent computation across time, allowing each update to build on representations produced earlier. This raises a basic question: once temporal recurrence provides sequential computation across steps, how much depth is still needed within each step? Prior work has shown that recurrence can make shallow models competitive. We instead study this question as a compute-allocation problem, varying within-step depth, expert width, and the number of parallel experts per layer across several compute budgets. For each budget, we compare the best observed recurrent and non-recurrent allocations and the performance they achieve under approximately matched per-step computation. Across Sokoban and autoregressive FineWeb language modeling, we find that temporal recurrence shifts the best observed compute allocation toward substantially fewer layers, with comparable or better performance.

</details>


### [124] [$\text{GSF-}χ$: Global Stereochemical Fields for Chiral Graph Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12532)
*Jiaqing Xie,Yuxin Wang,Xipeng Qiu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GSF-χ is a graph transformer that encodes molecular chirality through reflection-even phase fields and Chiral-RoPE, enabling accurate differentiation of enantiomers while respecting proper symmetries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enantiomers (mirror-image molecules) share identical atomic connectivity and distances but can behave differently in chiral environments. Existing molecular encoders struggle to properly handle stereochemistry—they must respect atom relabelings and proper rotations while remaining sensitive to reflection differences.

Method: GSF-χ introduces a graph transformer where stereogenic units modulate all pairwise interactions rather than treating one atom as special. Each stereogenic unit creates a reflection-even phase field over all atoms. A handedness pseudoscalar χ directs relative rotation on latent query-key blocks via "Chiral-RoPE" that reflection inverts. A C₂ projection separates mirror-even from mirror-odd properties.

Result: GSF-χ leads all central-ECD output metrics and improves axial Rotation and Symbol tasks by 12.6% and 7.9% over strongest baselines. Equal-budget controls show the Rotation advantage stems from global signed support rather than parameter count. The C₂ projection provides exact enantiomer-pair consistency with minimal accuracy cost under full supervision.

Conclusion: The proposed Chiral-RoPE operator with reflection-sensitive phase modulation provides an effective framework for stereochemical encoding in graph neural networks, demonstrating strong empirical performance while maintaining proper symmetry properties and theoretical guarantees.

Abstract: Enantiomers share atoms, bonds, and pairwise distances yet can behave differently in chiral environments, so molecular encoders must respect atom relabelings and proper rotations without becoming blind to reflection. We introduce GSF-$χ$, a graph transformer in which stereogenic units modulate all pairwise interactions rather than single out one atom as special. Each central or axial stereogenic unit creates a reflection-even phase field over all atoms, a handedness pseudoscalar $χ$ sets the direction of a relative rotation on latent query--key blocks, giving a \textbf{Chiral-RoPE} that reflection inverts rather than leaves fixed. A $C_2$ projection separates mirror-even ECD peak counts and positions from mirror-odd peak signs. We prove the operator's even--odd decomposition and its annotation-inversion, permutation, and unit-order identities under explicit canonical-role conditions; property tests and a coordinate-reflection audit verify the laws end to end. GSF-$χ$ leads every central-ECD output and improves axial Rotation and Symbol by $12.6\%$ and $7.9\%$ over the strongest baseline. Equal-budget controls attribute the Rotation advantage to global signed support rather than parameter or edge count; the $C_2$ projection yields exact enantiomer-pair consistency at a small raw-accuracy cost under complete supervision and becomes predictive when mirror supervision is scarce.

</details>


### [125] [Quality-Constrained Routing over a Fixed Pool of Quantized Mixture-of-Experts Instances](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12550)
*Zhenghong Huang,Hongfan Wu,Jiheng Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FWP (Fragility-Weighted Perplexity) is introduced as a routing method for Quantized Mixture-of-Experts services that predicts request-specific quantization degradation to allocate requests to appropriate quantized instances while respecting quality degradation budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantized MoE services can host multiple pre-materialized instances of a base model, but quantization damage varies significantly across different requests and bitwidths. Since instance materialization and replication consume memory and require slow reconfiguration, routing decisions need to be made within a fixed resident pool of instances to maximize throughput under quality constraints.

Method: The paper introduces FWP, computed from prompt tokens on a reference-instance prefill and calibrated to candidate-instance degradation. It's based on an exact two-expert affinity-fragility decomposition and conditional multi-layer top-k expansion. Using these calibrated risks, a window-level linear program produces a signed reduced-reward score that is KKT-consistent with the LP optimum under optimal prices and primal-feasible tie allocation.

Result: On 88 extended Qwen prompts with complete W2, W3, and W4 instances quantizing all 6,144 expert blocks, FWP allocation achieves 1.284× offline model-based multiplier versus 1.253× for request-agnostic mixing and 1.000× for static W4, showing an incremental 2.5% relative gain. Quantization caused mean ΔNLL of 0.9437, 0.1832, and 0.0513 for W2, W3, and W4 respectively.

Conclusion: FWP provides an effective method for routing requests in quantized MoE systems by predicting request-specific quantization risks, enabling better throughput-quality tradeoffs compared to request-agnostic approaches, with the routing approach offering incremental but meaningful improvements over simpler allocation strategies.

Abstract: Quantized Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) services can hold several pre-materialized instances of one base model, but quantization damage varies sharply across requests and bitwidths. Because instance materialization and replica counts consume memory and require slow reconfiguration, we treat them as upstream provisioning decisions and study routing within a fixed resident pool. Within this fixed-pool boundary, we route each request to maximize modeled throughput under a class-level expected quality-degradation budget and measured instance capacities. To predict this request-specific risk, we introduce FWP (Fragility-Weighted Perplexity), computed from prompt tokens on a reference-instance prefill and calibrated to candidate-instance degradation. Underlying FWP is an exact two-expert affinity--fragility decomposition and a conditional multi-layer top-$k$ expansion whose bias, interaction, route-change, separability, and higher-order terms remain explicit. Using these calibrated risks, a window-level linear program yields a signed reduced-reward score that is KKT-consistent with the LP optimum under optimal prices and primal-feasible tie allocation. On 88 extended Qwen prompts, complete W2, W3, and W4 instances quantizing all 6,144 expert blocks incur mean $Δ$NLL of $0.9437$, $0.1832$, and $0.0513$. Under the same population and $τ=0.1513$, FWP allocation reaches a $1.284\times$ offline model-based multiplier versus $1.253\times$ for request-agnostic mixing and $1.000\times$ for static W4, an incremental $2.5\%$ relative FWP gain.

</details>


### [126] [TokenMapper: A Step Toward Interoperable Speech Token Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12563)
*Tal Kozakov,Tal Rosenwein,Eliya Nachmani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TokenMapper enables direct token-to-token translation between different neural audio codecs, eliminating the need for waveform reconstruction and reducing latency while maintaining speech quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural audio codecs create incompatible token spaces with different vocabularies and structures, preventing direct communication between speech models. Current solutions require decoding to waveforms and re-encoding, which increases latency and can cause information loss, limiting applications like conversational agents and speech-to-speech translation.

Method: TokenMapper is a direction-aware framework for direct token translation between heterogeneous speech tokenizers in the discrete domain. It supports structurally mismatched token spaces, including mappings between single-codebook and multi-codebook representations, while maintaining a shared effective token rate.

Result: Experiments show translation WER approaching native reconstructions within 2.5-6.8% absolute WER. Human MOS ranges from 2.29 to 4.39, following UTMOS trends. End-to-end latency is reduced by 4.8-94.5% relative to waveform bridging, reaching up to 972ms per utterance reduction. Testing includes GLM-4-Voice, MiMi, and DualCodec models.

Conclusion: TokenMapper provides a practical solution for cross-model speech token interoperability without intermediate waveform reconstruction, enabling efficient communication between different speech systems while maintaining quality and significantly reducing latency.

Abstract: Neural audio codecs discretize speech into token sequences, but the resulting token spaces differ in vocabulary and codebook structure, preventing direct communication across models. This limitation affects applications such as conversational voice agents and speech to speech translation systems where multiple speech models must interact. As a result, transferring information between speech systems typically requires decoding to waveform audio and re-encoding with a second tokenizer, increasing latency and introducing potential information loss. To address these limitations, we present TokenMapper, a direction aware framework for direct token to token translation between heterogeneous speech tokenizers in the discrete domain. TokenMapper supports structurally mismatched token spaces, including mappings between single codebook and multi codebook representations, under a shared effective token rate. Experiments on GLM-4-Voice, MiMi and DualCodec show consistent cross model performance. Specifically, translation WER approaches native reconstructions within 2.5-6.8% absolute WER, human MOS for TokenMapper outputs ranges from 2.29 to 4.39, following the same direction level trends as UTMOS and end to end latency is reduced by 4.8-94.5% relative to waveform bridging, reaching up to 972 ms per utterance. These results provide a practical step toward cross model speech token interoperability without intermediate waveform reconstruction.

</details>


### [127] [SCOPE-OPSD: Fisher-Conditioned Privileged Subspaces for On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12579)
*Yunmeng Chen,Kunyu Wang,Peihan Li,Yi Wang,Shuyin Xia,Yi Liu,Xinyong Cheng,Dehui Wang,Xiangyong Zhai,Yanxing Liu,Song Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SCOPE-OPSD improves on-policy self-distillation by projecting teacher-student discrepancies onto a structured Fisher-sensitivity subspace, achieving consistent performance gains without additional computational overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current on-policy self-distillation (OPSD) only transfers supervision through next-token probabilities, missing potential alignment information in the final-layer discrepancy between teacher and student models.

Method: SCOPE-OPSD projects privileged teacher-student residuals onto a frozen rank-64 factor estimated from residual covariance and language-model-head Fisher sensitivity. It reuses existing forward passes without adding rollouts or inference-time modules, with a matched Random control for comparison.

Result: SCOPE-OPSD consistently outperforms Pure OPSD across 25/50/75/100-step trajectories for Qwen3-1.7B, 4B, and 8B models, with strict gains in 11 of 12 model-checkpoint combinations. It also exceeds matched Random in 10 of 12 combinations, showing 1.39 Macro Avg@12 points improvement and 4.40× greater held-out privileged-gap capture.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate that a compact, Fisher-conditioned privileged subspace can effectively enhance short-budget OPSD by providing a second supervision channel through aligned final-layer discrepancies without additional computational cost.

Abstract: On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) scores student-generated prefixes with a solution-conditioned self-teacher, yet transfers supervision only through next-token probabilities. We ask whether the aligned final-layer discrepancy offers a useful second channel, and how to test that channel without confusing its geometry with auxiliary strength. SCOPE-OPSD projects the privileged teacher-student residual onto a frozen rank-64 factor estimated from residual covariance and language-model-head Fisher sensitivity. It reuses the forwards already required by OPSD and adds neither rollouts nor inference-time modules. A matched Random control preserves the structured factor's rank and nonzero spectrum and uses per-arm gradient-RMS calibration, isolating the effect of the data-dependent orientation. Across the complete 25/50/75/100-step trajectories for Qwen3-1.7B, 4B, and 8B, Structured is never below Pure OPSD, with strict gains in 11 of the 12 model-checkpoint combinations and an exact tie at 4B step 25. Structured also exceeds matched Random in 10 of the 12 combinations. At step 75 on Qwen3-1.7B, Structured exceeds matched Random by 1.39 Macro Avg@12 points in each of two independent training reruns. A cross-fitted diagnostic also shows 4.40 times greater held-out privileged-gap capture than the matched random orientation. The results support a compact, Fisher-conditioned privileged subspace for short-budget OPSD.

</details>


### [128] [Clustering-Based Balanced Sampling and Allocation with Data Parallelism for High-Performance Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12584)
*Hyunjin Kim,Youngeun Nam,Jaemin Han,Wonhyeok Choi,Jae-Gil Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CluSTER is a cluster-aware balanced sampling framework for efficient data reduction in instruction tuning that reduces training time by up to 69.6% with minimal accuracy loss by eliminating redundant computations through gradient-space clustering and balanced data allocation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Instruction-tuning datasets for LLMs are often large, redundant, and imbalanced, limiting efficient adaptation. Naive large-batch fine-tuning repeatedly includes overrepresented sample groups while weakly covering underrepresented but informative ones, especially under data parallelism across multiple GPUs.

Method: CluSTER curates a representative reduced dataset through gradient-space clustering and data parallelism-aware balanced allocation. It ensures dual-level coverage across clusters and workers while preserving the original data distribution by weighted update.

Result: Across multiple instruction-tuning datasets, CluSTER reduces training time by up to 69.6% with almost no accuracy loss compared to prior sampling and data reduction methods.

Conclusion: CluSTER reduces redundant computation and improves training stability without compromising model quality, providing an efficient framework for instruction tuning with data parallelism.

Abstract: Instruction-tuning datasets for large language models (LLMs) are often large, redundant, and imbalanced, limiting efficient adaptation.
