Naive large-batch fine-tuning repeatedly includes overrepresented sample groups while weakly covering underrepresented but informative ones, especially under data parallelism (DP) across multiple GPUs. We propose CluSTER, a Cluster-aware balanced Sampling framework for Training Efficient data Reduction in DP instruction tuning. CluSTER curates a representative reduced dataset through gradient-space clustering and DP-aware balanced allocation, ensuring dual-level coverage across clusters and workers, while preserving the original data distribution by weighted update. As a result, CluSTER reduces redundant computation and improves training stability without compromising model quality. Across multiple instruction-tuning datasets, CluSTER reduces training time by up to 69.6% with almost no accuracy loss compared to prior sampling and data reduction methods. Code is available at https://github.com/kaist-dmlab/CluSTER.

</details>


### [129] [Where Decoder Cosine Similarity Fails for SAE Feature Flow Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12591)
*Hendrik Droste,Christian Medeiros Adriano,Kathrin Korte,Holger Giese*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Researchers develop a "transition atlas" method to understand how state features and update features interact in foundation models, finding that most transitions can't be explained by simple cosine similarity between individual features and targets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As foundation models are increasingly adapted through fine-tuning, model editing, and alignment procedures while retaining previously acquired capabilities, there's a growing need to understand the internal computations that support these adaptations. While sparse autoencoders provide interpretable feature dictionaries, it remains unclear how state features and update features interact to produce downstream residual features.

Method: The researchers focus on MLP updates as a first test case, constructing a transition atlas of triples (state feature + update feature → target feature). They validate candidate triples by ablating the decoded update feature. They apply this to Pythia-160M and Gemma-3-4B models, analyzing transitions between specific layers.

Result: In Pythia-160M, they found 38,125 strong ablation-effect transitions, but 88.0% had both state-target and update-target decoder cosine similarity below 0.7. In Gemma-3-4B, they validated only top-ranked candidate triples and found 53.6% of strong-effect triples had both state-target and update-target decoder cosine similarity below 0.7. The Gemma results were directionally consistent with Pythia but weaker.

Conclusion: Feature flow atlases can serve as diagnostics of representation-update mechanisms and inform tools for steering model updates. Most transitions can't be explained by simple similarity between individual features and targets, suggesting more complex interactions. Future work will validate more complex patterns across layers, models, and SAE families.

Abstract: Foundation models are increasingly adapted through fine-tuning, model editing, and alignment procedures while retaining previously acquired capabilities. Understanding the internal computations that support these adaptations is therefore becoming increasingly important for continual model evolution. Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) provide interpretable feature dictionaries for residual-stream activations and sublayer outputs, but it remains unclear how state features and update features interact to produce downstream residual features. In this work, we focus on MLP updates as a first test case. We construct a transition atlas of triples $s_k + u_j \rightarrow t_\ell$, where a residual-state feature and an MLP-update feature jointly predict a target residual feature, and validate candidate triples by ablating the decoded update feature. In a 20M-token Pythia-160M $L_7 \rightarrow L_8$ run, we find 38,125 strong ablation-effect transitions, but 88.0% have both state-target and update-target decoder cosine similarity below 0.7. As a preliminary cross-model check, a run of 20M-token Gemma-3-4B $L_{21} \rightarrow L_{22}$ causally validates only the top 30,000 ranked candidate triples by ablating the decoded update feature, and 53.6% of strong-effect triples have both state-target and update-target decoder cosine similarity below 0.7. The Gemma result is directionally consistent with Pythia, but weaker, since update-target cosine recovers many of the strongest Gemma effects and the run is not a full-atlas causal validation. Ultimately, our results suggest that feature flow atlases can serve as diagnostics of representation-update mechanisms and thereby inform tools for steering model updates. Future work will validate more complex patterns across layers, models, and SAE families.

</details>


### [130] [Poisson-Corrector Complexity Bounds for Moreau--Yosida Unadjusted Langevin Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12594)
*Yuchen Xin,Zhihua Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MYULA convergence rate analysis: O(h) + Õ(h^{3/4}) Wasserstein-2 distance to smoothed target with logarithmic dependence on smoothing parameter; Õ(ε^{-4/3}) iterations to achieve ε-accuracy to original target.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to provide precise convergence guarantees for the Moreau-Yosida unadjusted Langevin algorithm (MYULA), which handles non-smooth composite potentials f+g where g is non-differentiable but Lipschitz. Analyzing convergence rates for such algorithms with non-smooth components is challenging, and the authors seek to establish explicit rates with minimal dependence on smoothing parameters.

Method: The authors study MYULA for sampling from distributions of the form π(dx) ∝ exp(-f(x)-g(x))dx, where f is strongly convex with Lipschitz gradient and g is convex and Lipschitz. They analyze convergence to the Moreau-smoothed target π_λ and prove Wasserstein-2 distance bounds between the invariant distribution of MYULA (π̂_{λ,h}) and π_λ. The proof combines: discrete Poisson corrector techniques, active-trace estimates, and shared-noise bounds for exact-Euler two-point curvature analysis.

Result: The main result shows that under step size condition 0 < h(L_f + λ^{-1}) ≤ c, the Wasserstein-2 distance satisfies: √m W_2(π_λ, π̂_{λ,h}) = O(h) + Õ(h^{3/4}) with only logarithmic dependence on λ^{-1} in error coefficients. Combining this with Moreau approximation bias yields Õ(ε^{-4/3}) iterations to achieve √m W_2(μ_N, π) ≤ ε for fixed model parameters and initialization.

Conclusion: MYULA achieves near-optimal convergence rates for sampling from composite potentials with non-smooth components, with logarithmic dependence on the smoothing parameter and requiring Õ(ε^{-4/3}) iterations to reach ε-accuracy to the original target distribution. The analysis provides tight bounds using novel proof techniques that handle the non-smoothness effectively.

Abstract: We study the classical Moreau--Yosida unadjusted Langevin algorithm (MYULA) for $π(\,\mathrm{d} x)\propto e^{-f(x)-g(x)}\,\mathrm{d} x$, where $f\in C^2(\mathbb{R}^d)$ is $m$-strongly convex with $L_f$-Lipschitz gradient and $g:\mathbb{R}^d\to\mathbb{R}$ is convex and globally $G$-Lipschitz. For the Moreau-smoothed target $π_λ$ and the MYULA invariant law $\widehatπ_{λ,h}$, we prove \[
  \sqrt m\,W_2(π_λ,\widehatπ_{λ,h})
  =O(h)+\widetilde O(h^{3/4}) \] under $0<h(L_f+λ^{-1})\le c$, with only logarithmic dependence on $λ^{-1}$ in the error coefficients. Combining this estimate with the Moreau approximation bias yields $\widetilde O(\varepsilon^{-4/3})$ iterations to achieve $\sqrt m\,W_2(μ_N,π)\le\varepsilon$, for fixed model parameters and initialization. The proof combines a discrete Poisson corrector with active-trace estimates and a shared-noise bound for the exact--Euler two-point curvature.

</details>


### [131] [SIMS: Scale-Invariant Merit-Function-Based Scalarization for Multi-Task Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12599)
*Zebin Chen,Fei Xing,Yang Chen,Hua Liu,Andy HF Chow,Yuhua Qian,Yu Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SIMS is a scale-invariant merit-function-based scalarization method for multi-task learning that addresses sensitivity to objective scales by using logarithmic transformations, achieving state-of-the-art performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing merit-function-based scalarization approaches in multi-task learning are sensitive to the relative scales of different objectives, as task losses often differ by orders of magnitude. The optimization process unfairly favors objectives with larger scales, even though the Pareto optimal solutions should be invariant to rescaling.

Method: Proposes Scale-Invariant Merit-function-based Scalarization (SIMS), which adopts a transformation-induced merit function using logarithmic transformations to convert multi-objective optimization to a single objective that's invariant to loss magnitudes. The theoretical analysis shows logarithmic transformation uniquely achieves scale invariance.

Result: Extensive experiments on representative multi-task benchmarks demonstrate that SIMS consistently outperforms existing scalarization methods and achieves state-of-the-art performance.

Conclusion: SIMS effectively addresses the scale sensitivity issue in multi-task learning scalarization through logarithmic transformation, preserving weak Pareto optimality while enabling robust optimization across objectives with vastly different scales.

Abstract: Multi-task learning (MTL) requires navigating unavoidable trade-offs among competing objectives. This paradigm is frequently formulated as multi-objective optimization (MOO), where the scalarization is favored to reduce an MOO problem to a single objective. We empirically find that existing merit-function-based scalarization approaches are sensitive to the relative scales of different objectives in practical MTL, where task losses commonly differ by orders of magnitude. The optimization process often favors objectives with larger scales even though the underlying Pareto optimal solutions remains invariant to rescaling (i.e., multiplying an objective by a positive constant). To address this issue, we propose Scale-Invariant Merit-function-based Scalarization (SIMS) for MTL. Specifically, SIMS adopts a transformation-induced merit function to convert the MOO problem of MTL to a single objective that renders optimization invariant to the magnitudes of losses. Theoretically, we prove that the requirement for scale invariance uniquely determines this transformation to be logarithmic. We further show that this general transformation-induced merit function preserves weak Pareto optimality and admits a smooth surrogate with controllable approximation error. Extensive experiments on representative multi-task benchmarks demonstrate that SIMS consistently outperforms existing scalarization methods and achieves state-of-the-art performance.

</details>


### [132] [Correlation-Guided Fast Machine Unlearning via Hessian Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12620)
*Ayushi Thakur,Ruchir Gupta,Amit Kumar Jaiswal,Prayag Tiwari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A computationally efficient machine unlearning framework that identifies correlated data points and applies closed-form parameter updates, achieving 82× speedup over standard influence function unlearning while maintaining model utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Increasing adoption of ML in network/security systems creates need to remove influence of specific training data (e.g., compromised/adversarial points). Privacy regulations like GDPR's right to be forgotten also require such capabilities. Existing approximate unlearning techniques are computationally prohibitive for real-world security deployment due to expensive Hessian-inverse-vector computations for each data point removal.

Method: Introduces computationally efficient unlearning framework that: 1) Identifies correlated data points in training set, 2) Applies theoretically derived closed-form parameter update rule, 3) Uses Hessian damping for numerical stability, 4) Provides theoretical guarantees. The method avoids repeated expensive Hessian-inverse-vector computations.

Result: Achieves 82× wall-clock speedup over standard influence function unlearning while preserving model utility (10^{-2} improvement in accuracy over state-of-the-art baselines). Evaluation across 7 diverse dataset-architecture combinations (including CIFAR-100 with ResNet-50) shows superior forgetting effectiveness: membership inference attack success rates of 0.660 and tug-of-war scores of 0.950.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a practical, efficient solution for machine unlearning in security systems, addressing computational bottlenecks of existing methods while maintaining model utility and privacy compliance requirements.

Abstract: The increasing adoption of machine learning in network and distributed security systems has created an urgent need for mechanisms that can selectively and efficiently remove the influence of specific training data to eliminate compromised or adversarial data points from production models. Privacy regulations such as GDPR's \emph{right to be forgotten} also pose similar requirements. However, existing approximate unlearning techniques remain computationally prohibitive for deployment in real-world security systems, as they require repeated expensive Hessian-inverse-vector computations for each data point removal, creating a bottleneck when processing multiple related requests in scenarios such as intrusion detection systems, spam filters, and threat intelligence platforms. Thus, we introduce a computationally efficient unlearning framework that identifies correlated data points in the training set and applies a theoretically derived closed-form parameter update rule, achieving an $82\times$ wall-clock speedup over standard influence function unlearning while preserving model utility with a $10^{-2}$ improvement in accuracy over state-of-the-art baselines. Our method establishes theoretical guarantees and ensures numerical stability through Hessian damping. Our evaluation across seven diverse dataset architecture combinations, including large-scale CIFAR-100 with ResNet-50, demonstrates superior forgetting effectiveness, with membership inference attack success rates of 0.660 and tug-of-war scores of 0.950.

</details>


### [133] [Geometric-to-Semantic Spherical Transfer Learning for Cortical Sulci Labeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12627)
*Saeb Tounsi,Joël Chavas,Pietro Gori,Vincent Frouin,Denis Rivière,Jean-François Mangin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Transfer learning framework that pre-trains on unlabeled cortical surface data for geometric understanding, then injects semantic sulcal lines to improve annotation of rare sulci in data-scarce regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning for cortical surface analysis faces a dilemma: high model capacity is needed to capture complex sulcal topology, but extreme scarcity of expert annotations (N=62 subjects) causes overfitting. Supervised approaches fail to generalize in this data-scarce regime, especially for variable and small sulci with high topological ambiguity.

Method: Geometric-to-Semantic Spherical Transfer Learning framework: 1) Pre-train spherical encoder on massive unlabeled data (UK Biobank, ~30k subjects) using locally-optimized strategy with continuous surface features (curvature and depth), 2) Bridge dimensional gap from geometric to semantic by integrating extracted sulcal fundi (lines) into pre-trained backbone via soft-initialized Topological Prior Injector to prevent catastrophic forgetting.

Result: Outperforms fully supervised baselines trained from scratch with mean Dice of 0.77. Local analysis shows self-supervised geometric priors yield largest performance gains on variable and tertiary sulci (up to 14.8%), confirming learning cortex shape benefits identifying its rarest parts.

Conclusion: Transfer learning with geometric pre-training on unlabeled data followed by semantic injection effectively addresses data scarcity in cortical surface analysis, particularly improving performance on rare and variable sulci where topological ambiguity is highest.

Abstract: Deep learning on cortical surfaces faces a dilemma: capturing the complex topology of over 60 nomenclature-dependent sulci per hemisphere requires high-capacity models, yet the extreme scarcity of expert annotations ($N=62$ subjects) inevitably causes overfitting. Standard supervised approaches fail to generalize in this data-scarce regime, particularly for variable and small sulci where topological ambiguity is high. To overcome this limitation, we introduce a Geometric-to-Semantic Spherical Transfer Learning framework.
  First, we leverage massive unlabeled data (UK Biobank, $\approx$30,000 subjects) to pre-train a spherical encoder using a locally-optimized strategy. By relying solely on continuous surface features (curvature and depth), the relevance of this pre-training is confirmed by the model's ability to detect localized and rare topological traits, such as sulcal interruptions. The downstream labeling task, however, introduces extracted sulcal fundi (lines) as an explicit semantic input. To bridge this dimensional domain gap (from purely geometric to semantic) without causing catastrophic forgetting, these anatomical lines are integrated into the pre-trained backbone via a soft-initialized Topological Prior Injector.
  Our experiments demonstrate that this approach outperforms fully supervised baselines trained from scratch, achieving a mean Dice of 0.77. Crucially, a local analysis reveals that the self-supervised geometric priors yield the largest performance gains on variable and tertiary sulci (up to 14.8%), confirming that learning the cortex shape is highly beneficial for identifying its rarest parts.

</details>


### [134] [Explaining Time Series Forecasting with Horizon-Resolved Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12639)
*Seunghan Lee,Jun Seo,Jaehoon Lee,Junhyeok Kang,Sangjun Han,Sungdong Yoo,Minjae Kim,Tae Yoon Lim,Dongwan Kang,Hwanil Choi,Soonyoung Lee,Wonbin Ahn*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HRX is a novel time series explanation framework that adds a horizon axis to show that different forecast steps depend on different past values, unlike existing methods that assume uniform dependencies across all predictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current time series explanation methods incorrectly assume that every predicted step depends on the same past values, returning only a single importance vector. The authors demonstrate that this assumption doesn't hold in reality, as different forecast horizons actually depend on different historical values.

Method: HRX (Horizon-Resolved eXplanation) introduces a plug-in framework with three components: 1) an estimator that extracts horizon-specific importance maps from any differentiable forecaster without modifying the TS backbone, 2) an evaluation protocol that validates the horizon axis by measuring prediction changes when removing top-ranked inputs, and 3) a rank criterion that predicts whether horizon resolution is beneficial for a given time series.

Result: The framework reveals that step-wise dependence is low-dimensional, with explanations across all steps being constructed from a few shared maps whose number doesn't scale with forecast length. Extensive experiments across various backbones and datasets show improvements from the horizon axis, and the approach works with estimators from previous explanation methods.

Conclusion: HRX successfully addresses the limitation of existing time series explanation methods by introducing horizon-specific explanations, showing that different forecast steps have distinct temporal dependencies, and providing an effective, scalable framework that improves interpretability without modifying the underlying forecasting models.

Abstract: Recent advances in explaining time series (TS) models have produced methods that identify which past values a prediction depends on. However, most existing methods return a single importance vector, assuming that every predicted step depends on the same past values. In this paper, we show that this assumption does not hold, as different forecast steps depend on different past values. Motivated by this observation, we propose Horizon-Resolved eXplanation (HRX), which adds a horizon axis to the explanation, so that every forecast step receives its own importance map. HRX is a simple yet effective plug-in framework with three components: 1) an estimator that reads these maps out of any differentiable forecaster without modifying the TS backbone, 2) an evaluation protocol that validates the horizon axis by measuring how much a single forecast step changes when the inputs an importance map ranks highest are removed, and 3) a rank criterion that predicts in advance whether the axis is worth resolving on a given TS. We further show that this step-wise dependence is low-dimensional, as the explanations of all steps are built from a few shared maps whose number does not grow with the forecast length. Extensive experiments across various backbones and datasets show that the improvement comes from the horizon axis and holds for estimators of previous explanation methods. Code is available at https://github.com/seunghan96/HRX.

</details>


### [135] [Distortion of AI Alignment Revisited: RLHF is a Decent Utilitarian Aligner](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12651)
*Kazusato Oko,Annie Ulichney,Nika Haghtalab,Han Bao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RLHF distortion in pluralistic settings scales with distribution mismatch between preference data and reference policy, not fundamentally with temperature. With aligned distributions, RLHF achieves near-optimal performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent work by Gölz et al. (2025) demonstrated that RLHF (Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback) can suffer from exponential distortion scaling with the Bradley-Terry temperature parameter β when users have heterogeneous preferences. This calls into question RLHF's effectiveness in pluralistic settings where diverse user preferences exist. The authors aim to investigate whether this exponential degradation is a fundamental property of RLHF or a consequence of specific algorithmic conditions.

Method: The paper presents a fine-grained analysis of RLHF with reward clipping, establishing tight upper and lower bounds on distortion across multiple regimes of KL regularization strength. The analysis shows that under the Bradley-Terry model, in a representative regime, the distortion is Θ̃(βB + β), where B bounds the log density ratio between μ and π_ref. This analysis specifically examines the relationship between distortion, temperature parameter β, and distribution mismatch between preference data distribution (μ) and reference policy (π_ref).

Result: The exponential distortion observed in prior work is not a fundamental property of RLHF but rather stems from distribution mismatch between preference data distribution (μ) and the KL reference policy (π_ref). Tight bounds show that distortion scales as Θ̃(βB + β) under the Bradley-Terry model, where B bounds the log density ratio between μ and π_ref. When μ = π_ref (no distribution mismatch), RLHF achieves optimal distortion of O(β) up to a constant. This reveals that distribution alignment is key to RLHF performance.

Conclusion: The distortion in RLHF algorithms is fundamentally tied to the distribution mismatch between preference data distribution (μ) and the KL reference policy (π_ref). When these distributions are aligned (μ = π_ref), RLHF achieves near-optimal performance with O(β) distortion. To maximize average utility effectively with RLHF, practitioners should use on-policy sampled preference data or fine-tune models on data close to μ before RLHF training.

Abstract: While Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) is the standard paradigm for aligning large language models with human preferences, its effectiveness in pluralistic settings has been called into question. Notably, recent work by Gölz et al. (2025) demonstrated that the \textit{distortion} -- defined as the multiplicative gap between the average user utility of the RLHF policy and the optimal average utility -- can scale exponentially with the Bradley-Terry temperature parameter $β$ when users have heterogeneous preferences. In this work, we present a fine-grained analysis of the distortion of RLHF with reward clipping and demonstrate that such exponential degradation is not a fundamental property of the algorithm but rather a consequence of distribution mismatch between the distribution generating preference data ($μ$) and the KL reference policy ($π_{\mathrm{ref}}$). To this end, we establish tight upper and lower bounds on the distortion of RLHF across multiple regimes of the KL regularization strength. We show that in a representative regime, under the Bradley-Terry model, the distortion is $\tildeΘ(βB + β)$, where $B$ is an upper bound on the log density ratio between $μ$ and $π_{\mathrm{ref}}$. In particular, when there is no distribution mismatch (i.e., $μ= π_{\mathrm{ref}}$), RLHF achieves the optimal distortion of $O(β)$ up to a constant. Our results suggest that, to reasonably maximize average utility with RLHF, it is preferable to use on-policy sampled preference data or to fine-tune before RLHF on data from a source close to $μ$.

</details>


### [136] [ProactiveBench: Can Streaming Video Models Really Interact Like Humans?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12658)
*Kaixuan Du,Xin Wan,YuKun Wang,Hang Zhang,Meng Cao,Dai Guan,Ming Chen,Ni Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ProactiveBench is a new benchmark for evaluating models on proactive video understanding, focusing on when to respond to events in streaming video rather than just what to respond, revealing that current systems tend to respond prematurely.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current video understanding evaluations are predominantly reactive - they query models at selected timestamps and don't assess when models should respond. There's a need for proactive interaction that requires monitoring standing requests, responding within appropriate intervals after target events, and otherwise remaining silent.

Method: Introduced ProactiveBench which evaluates models at one-second stream intervals without explicit response cues. Includes six subtasks varying trigger ambiguity and timing tolerance: Event Sensitivity (combines response and silence rates), four window-based subtasks distinguishing early/in-window/missed responses, and Duplicate Counting penalizing omissions and repetitions.

Result: Evaluation of six systems revealed that premature responses outnumber missed responses for four systems, showing a substantial gap in temporal decision-making required for human-like interaction. Systems struggle with knowing when to respond appropriately in continuous video streams.

Conclusion: There's a significant need for models to develop better temporal decision-making capabilities for proactive video understanding. Current systems tend to be too eager to respond rather than missing events, indicating they lack appropriate response timing judgment for human-like interaction in streaming video scenarios.

Abstract: Streaming video understanding requires models to process continuous multimodal input while maintaining temporal context. Existing evaluations are predominantly reactive: they query a model at a selected timestamp and therefore do not assess when it should respond. Proactive interaction instead requires monitoring a standing request, responding within an appropriate interval after the target event, and otherwise remaining silent. We introduce ProactiveBench, which evaluates models at one-second stream intervals without an explicit response cue. Its six subtasks vary trigger ambiguity and timing tolerance. Event Sensitivity geometrically combines response and silence rates on the same recording; four window-based subtasks distinguish early, in-window, and missed responses; and Duplicate Counting penalizes omissions and repetitions. Premature responses outnumber missed responses for four of the six evaluated systems, revealing a substantial gap in the temporal decision-making required for human-like interaction.

</details>


### [137] [SIFPBPNet: A Dual-Path Network for Wearable and Cuffless Blood Pressure Estimation via Individualized Steady-state Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12690)
*Shuailong Tang,Xiaoyu Li,Donglin Xie,Wei Chen,Guangpu Zhu,Yelei Li,Yali Zheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SIFPBPNet is a dual-path neural network that separately extracts steady-state and instantaneous features from PPG signals to improve cuffless blood pressure monitoring by addressing population heterogeneity and the "one-to-many mapping" problem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional population-based models for continuous cuffless BP monitoring using PPG suffer from limited accuracy due to significant population heterogeneity and the "one-to-many mapping" problem, where similar waveforms across different individuals correspond to different BP levels.

Method: Proposes SIFPBPNet with dual-path architecture: 1) Steady-state Feature Path (SFP) uses Graph Attention Network to extract individual-specific long-term characteristics from multi-day historical PPG trajectories, and 2) Instantaneous Feature Path (IFP) captures short-term dynamics from current PPG segments while incorporating steady-state prior through cross-attention mechanism.

Result: Achieves MAE of 8.57 mmHg (systolic) and 5.97 mmHg (diastolic), outperforming state-of-the-art models. The SFP module yields 2.8-13.1% relative MAE reductions for systolic BP when integrated into various backbone architectures, demonstrating strong generalizability and plug-and-play transferability.

Conclusion: SIFPBPNet's dual-path approach effectively addresses population heterogeneity in cuffless BP monitoring, with the SFP module showing great potential for accurate, personalized cardiovascular health management due to its strong generalizability and transferability to different architectures.

Abstract: Continuous and cuffless blood pressure (BP) monitoring using photoplethysmography (PPG) is of great interest for low-cost and personalized cardiovascular health management. However, significant population heterogeneity and the "one-to-many mapping" problem, where similar waveforms across individuals correspond to different BP levels, limit the accuracy of conventional population-based models. To address this challenge, we propose a dual-path architecture termed SIFPBPNet, which separately represents steady-state and instantaneous features, through a Steady-state Feature Path (SFP) and an Instantaneous Feature Path (IFP). The SFP employs a Graph Attention Network (GAT) to extract individual-specific and long-term characteristics from multi-day historical PPG trajectories. In parallel, the IFP captures short-term dynamics from current PPG segments and incorporates the steady-state prior via a cross-attention mechanism. Experiments on a large-scale wearable dataset demonstrate that SIFPBPNet achieves a Mean Absolute Error (MAE) of 8.57 and 5.97 mmHg for systolic and diastolic BP, respectively, outperforming state-of-the-art models. Furthermore, the SFP module consistently improves performance when integrated into various backbone architectures, yielding 2.8-13.1% relative MAE reductions for systolic BP. These results highlight the strong generalizability and plug-and-play transferability of the SFP module, underscoring its great potential for accurate cuffless BP monitoring.

</details>


### [138] [Write on Paper and Get the Online Digital Trace:\newline A New Era for Handwriting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12702)
*Florent Imbert,Yann Soullard,Eric Anquetil,Tanja Harbaum,Alexey Serdyuk,Fabian Kress,Tim Hamann,Peter Kampf*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A system combining a digital pen with AI algorithms to digitize handwriting on regular paper without requiring special surfaces or external references.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current handwriting digitization requires specific hardware (stylus+touchscreen, EMR tablets, special paper) but lacks a low-cost, widely accepted solution for regular paper. Handwriting on regular paper offers better haptics, no latency, and improves information retention, but capturing digital traces remains challenging.

Method: Combines hardware (sensor-equipped digital pen) with software innovations using advanced AI algorithms and adaptive AI techniques. The approach integrates hardware development with real-time trajectory reconstruction and processing using embedded AI, enabling pen movement tracking without external reference systems.

Result: Proposes an innovative solution that reconstructs digital handwriting traces from regular paper, overcoming the challenge of accurate pen trajectory tracking without external references while allowing unrestricted pen movement.

Conclusion: This work advances state-of-the-art in automated handwriting trace reconstruction, creating a seamless connection between traditional handwriting on paper and digital trace capture, enabling the benefits of paper writing with digital convenience.

Abstract: Capturing the digital trace of handwriting usually requires a specific stylus and a compatible substrate, be it a capacitive touchscreen, an ElectroMagnetic Resonance (EMR) tablet as used in Wacom systems or special paper. While writing on regular paper offers rich haptics, no latency and is well known for improving information retention, no low-cost and widely accepted, effective solution exists to digitize such a pen trace. The challenge is to accurately track the pen's trajectory without an external reference system while allowing unrestricted freedom of pen movement across a surface. We propose an innovative solution that combines a digital pen, advanced artificial intelligence algorithms, and adaptive AI techniques to reconstruct the digital trace of handwriting. Our approach integrates hardware development, focusing on a sensor-equipped pen, with software innovations to optimize trajectory reconstruction and processing in real time using an embedded AI. This work aims to advance the state-of-the-art in automated trace reconstruction of handwriting, enabling a seamless connection between traditional handwriting on paper and capturing the trace digitally.

</details>


### [139] [InRTL: Effective Intra-Inter Interaction Learning for Relational Tables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12712)
*Weichen Li,Ken Zhong,Zheng Wang,Li Pan,Jianhua Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: InRTL proposes a unified framework for relational table learning that models both intra-table and inter-table dependencies using column-aware encoders and Transformer-based attention mechanisms with scalability enhancements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Relational table learning is important for modeling multiple tables connected through PK-FK relationships, but there is a lack of principled modeling frameworks specifically tailored to this task.

Method: Proposes InRTL framework with: 1) Column-aware table encoder for initial row representations, 2) Transformer-based self-attention for intra-table interactions, 3) Transformer-based cross-attention for inter-table interactions across PK-FK-linked tables, 4) Scalability improvements through linearized attention and heterogeneous graph neural networks.

Result: Extensive experiments on ten datasets covering 24 real-world tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach. Code is publicly available.

Conclusion: InRTL provides a principled, unified framework for relational table learning that effectively models both within-table and cross-table dependencies while maintaining scalability.

Abstract: Relational table learning has recently emerged as an important research direction for modeling multiple tables connected through primary key-foreign key (PK-FK) relationships. Despite recent advances, a principled modeling framework tailored to this task remains underexplored. In this paper, we propose Intra-Inter Relational Table Learning (InRTL), a unified framework that explicitly models dependencies both within and across relational tables. Specifically, InRTL formalizes two complementary interaction patterns: intra-table interactions, describing associations among rows within the same table, and inter-table interactions, describing dependencies between rows across PK-FK-linked tables. To model these dependencies, we develop a column-aware table encoder to generate initial row representations, followed by Transformer-based self-attention and cross-attention modules for intra-table and inter-table learning, respectively. To further improve scalability, InRTL incorporates linearized attention and heterogeneous graph neural networks to simplify the self-attention and cross-attention operations. Extensive experiments on ten datasets covering 24 real-world tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach. Code is available at https://github.com/W1nterFloW/InRTL.

</details>


### [140] [Physics-Guided Synthetic High-Frequency Ultrasound Generation for Skin Layer Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12735)
*Junkyung ju,Kyungho Yoon,Minwoo Shin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Physics-guided synthetic HFUS generation framework creates multilayer skin ultrasound images for pretraining segmentation models, improving real-domain performance when fine-tuned on real data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automated skin-layer analysis in HFUS is limited by scarcity of densely annotated data, especially for deeper structures (dermis, subcutaneous tissue, fascia, muscle). Existing datasets only provide superficial annotations like epidermis and SLEB.

Method: Propose a physics-guided synthetic HFUS generation framework: construct multilayer acoustic skin phantoms, assign layer-dependent acoustic properties, use k-Wave simulation to generate paired synthetic HFUS images, dense layer masks, and simulation metadata. Use synthetic data for pretraining segmentation models, then fine-tune on real Mendeley HFUS data.

Result: Synthetic pretraining followed by real fine-tuning achieved real-domain performance comparable to real-only training and improved mean Dice/IoU in three of four evaluated trainable architectures. Shows transferable anatomical and textural cues exist in synthetic data.

Conclusion: Physics-guided synthetic HFUS images contain transferable anatomical and textural cues for real-domain skin layer segmentation, though further reduction of the synthetic-real appearance gap is needed for greater gains. Framework provides valuable pretraining data for segmentation tasks.

Abstract: High-frequency ultrasound (HFUS) enables noninvasive visualization of superficial skin structures, but automated skin-layer analysis is limited by the scarcity of densely annotated data. Existing real HFUS datasets commonly provide annotations for superficial targets such as the epidermis and subepidermal low-echogenic band (SLEB), while dense labels for deeper structures such as dermis, subcutaneous tissue, fascia, and muscle are rarely available. We propose a physics-guided synthetic HFUS generation framework for skin layer segmentation. The framework constructs multilayer acoustic skin phantoms, assigns layer dependent acoustic properties, and uses k-Wave simulation to generate paired synthetic HFUS images, dense layer masks, and simulation metadata. To evaluate whether the generated data provide transferable supervision, we use it for downstream segmentation pretraining and fine-tune the models on real Mendeley HFUS data. Synthetic pretraining followed by real fine-tuning achieved real-domain performance comparable to real-only training and improved mean Dice/IoU in three of four evaluated trainable architectures. These results suggest that physics-guided synthetic HFUS images contain transferable anatomical and textural cues for real-domain skin layer segmentation, although further reduction of the synthetic-real appearance gap is needed to enable greater gains. The code and data are available at: https://github.com/Finn-02/synthetic-hfus-skin-layer-segmentation.

</details>


### [141] [Optimizing for the decision not the prediction: an exploration of Smooth Net Benefit as a training objective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12752)
*Koen M. F. Gorgels,Lasai Barreñada,Maarten van Smeden,Ben Van Calster,Ewout W. Steyerberg,Wouter A. C. van Amsterdam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: σNB is a differentiable approximation of Net Benefit for aligning model training with clinical utility, but shows mixed results: modest gains for logistic regression but little benefit for more flexible models like GAMs and XGBoost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current prediction models are trained using objectives like Bernoulli negative log-likelihood (NLL), but clinical decisions often depend on specific risk thresholds. There's a need to align model training with threshold-specific clinical utility.

Method: The authors introduce Smooth Net Benefit (σNB) as a differentiable approximation of Net Benefit. They evaluated σNB as a training objective for logistic regression, generalized additive models (GAMs), and XGBoost with three Hessian implementations. Experiments used the Framingham cardiovascular risk dataset and 44 TabZilla datasets (72 dataset-threshold combinations).

Result: σNB training didn't consistently improve Net Benefit in Framingham. Across TabZilla: logistic regression mean standardized Net Benefit increased from 0.5669 (NLL) to 0.5765 (σNB); GAMs decreased from 0.5921 to 0.5625; XGBoost achieved 0.6745 with NLL vs 0.6723-0.6735 with σNB. σNB gains in logistic regression were positively associated with XGBoost's performance advantage over NLL-trained logistic regression.

Conclusion: σNB's effect is context-dependent with modest gains concentrated in logistic regression and little benefit for more flexible models. Decision-focused optimization may be most useful when limited model flexibility leaves greater scope for improvement. The results don't support σNB as a general NLL replacement, but support further investigation of decision-focused objectives in settings where conventional likelihood-based training may not adequately capture decision-relevant structure.

Abstract: Objective Prediction models are commonly trained using objectives such as Bernoulli negative log-likelihood (NLL), although downstream clinical decisions may depend on specific risk thresholds. We introduce Smooth Net Benefit ($σ$NB), a differentiable approximation of Net Benefit designed to align model training with threshold-specific clinical utility.
  Materials and Methods We evaluated $σ$NB as a training objective for logistic regression, generalized additive models (GAMs), and XGBoost with three Hessian implementations. Experiments used the Framingham cardiovascular risk dataset and 44 TabZilla datasets comprising 72 dataset-threshold combinations.
  Results $σ$NB training did not consistently improve Net Benefit in Framingham. Across the TabZilla benchmark, mean standardized Net Benefit for logistic regression increased from 0.5669 with NLL to 0.5765 with $σ$NB (mean difference 0.0096, 95% CI -0.0001 to 0.0193). For GAMs, mean standardized Net Benefit decreased from 0.5921 to 0.5625 (mean difference -0.0296, 95% CI -0.0721 to 0.0129). For XGBoost, NLL achieved 0.6745 compared with 0.6723--0.6735 across $σ$NB implementations. In logistic regression, $σ$NB gains were positively associated with the performance advantage of XGBoost over NLL-trained logistic regression.
  Discussion The effect of $σ$NB was context dependent, with modest gains concentrated in logistic regression and little benefit for more flexible model classes. This suggests that decision-focused optimization may be most useful when limited model flexibility leaves greater scope for improvement.
  Conclusion Our results do not support $σ$NB as a general replacement for NLL training, but support further investigation of decision-focused objectives in settings where conventional likelihood-based training may not adequately capture decision-relevant structure.

</details>


### [142] [Curriculum-Based Adversarial Heterogeneous Agent Reinforcement Learning for Autonomous Quad-Copter Landing in Maritime Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12758)
*Allan Minh-Tam Nguyen,Sree Showrya Kotala,Stefan Banioi-Crijman,Kurt Driessens,Rico Möckel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Learning robust cooperative control policies for UAV shipboard recovery using adversarial reinforcement learning, achieving better generalization to severe sea states than domain randomization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recovering UAVs in maritime environments is challenging due to wind turbulence and ship-deck motion. Conventional landing approaches become unreliable, making this a valuable test case for alternative control and learning approaches.

Method: Simulated mid-air capture of quadrotor UAVs by ship-mounted robotic arm using Heterogeneous-Agent Proximal Policy Optimization (HAPPO) Reinforcement Learning. Trained with curriculum and adversarial wind agent (HARL-AC) in NVIDIA Isaac Lab, compared against curriculum-based domain randomization and single-sea-state benchmark.

Result: HARL-AC and domain randomization achieve up to 97.5% success on in-distribution sea states 0/4/5. On out-of-distribution sea states 7/8/10, HARL-AC generalizes better with up to 16% higher median success at sea state 10 and up to 14% lower crash rates. Adversarial training yields more cautious behavior with <3% timeout increase but safer recovery in severe conditions.

Conclusion: Adversarial reinforcement learning with heterogeneous agents (HARL-AC) produces more robust and generalizable cooperative control policies for UAV shipboard recovery compared to domain randomization, especially in severe, unseen sea states.

Abstract: Recovering unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) in maritime environments is challenging due to wind turbulence and ship-deck motion, making it a valuable test case for alternative control and learning approaches as conventional landing approaches often become unreliable. We study simulated mid-air capture of quadrotor UAVs by a ship-mounted robotic arm, learning robust cooperative control policies with Heterogeneous-Agent Proximal Policy Optimization (HAPPO) Reinforcement Learning. We train with HAPPO using a curriculum and an adversarial wind agent (HARL-AC) in NVIDIA Isaac Lab, and compare the obtained control policies against those generated through curriculum-based domain randomization and a benchmark trained on a single sea state. In-distribution evaluation on sea states $0/4/5$ shows comparable success for HARL-AC and domain randomization of up to $97.5\%$. On out-of-distribution sea states $7/8/10$, HARL-AC generalizes better, achieving up to $16\%$ higher median success rate at sea state 10, and substantially lower crash rates of up to $14\%$ compared to the domain randomization policy. Furthermore, we show that the adversarially trained policy shows more cautious behavior, slightly increasing timeouts by $<3\%$, but yields safer recovery behavior in severe, unseen conditions.

</details>


### [143] [Convergence of Stochastic Gradient Methods under Heavy-Tailed Noise and Hölder Smoothness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12785)
*Misbah Uz Zaman,Anirbit Mukherjee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Analysis of stochastic gradient methods under relaxed assumptions: Hölder smooth objectives and heavy-tailed gradient noise, establishing convergence guarantees for three variants (SGD, δ-GClip, and G-Clip) across different noise regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical convergence theory for stochastic gradient methods relies on restrictive assumptions - Lipschitz smoothness and finite-variance gradient noise - that are often violated in practical scenarios like training deep neural networks. The paper addresses these limitations by studying more realistic conditions.

Method: Three approaches are analyzed: 1) Standard SGD under Hölder continuity and α-th moment noise, 2) δ-regularized gradient clipping (δ-GClip) for provable training of wide/deep networks, 3) Standard gradient clipping (G-Clip). All are examined under (L,s)-Hölder continuous gradients and gradient noise with bounded α-th moment.

Result: 1) Standard SGD achieves O(T^{-s/(1+s)}) rate when α ≥ 1+s. 2) δ-GClip achieves O(T^{-2s(α-1)/[(1+s)(2α-1)]}) rate. 3) G-Clip achieves the same δ-GClip rate when α ≥ 1+s, and O(T^{-2s(α-1)/[(α-1)+s(2α-1)]}) in the very heavy-tailed regime (α < 1+s), which is the first convergence guarantee in this regime.

Conclusion: The paper provides rigorous convergence guarantees for stochastic gradient methods under significantly relaxed assumptions that better match practical scenarios. It establishes that gradient clipping can handle heavy-tailed noise regimes where standard SGD fails, with G-Clip particularly effective in the very heavy-tailed regime.

Abstract: Classical convergence guarantees for stochastic gradient methods typically assume Lipschitz-smooth objectives and finite-variance gradient noise, both frequently violated in practice. In contrast, we study nonconvex stochastic optimization under the joint relaxation of these assumptions: objectives with $(L,s)$-Hölder continuous gradients, $s\in(0,1]$, and gradient noise satisfying only a bounded $α$-th moment condition for $α\in(1,2]$. We establish three convergence results. Firstly, that standard SGD converges at rate $O(T^{-s/(1+s)})$ whenever $α\ge1+s$, extending the classical nonconvex SGD rate to heavy-tailed noise and Hölder smoothness simultaneously. Secondly, we analyze $δ$-regularized gradient clipping ($δ$-GClip), a provable trainer of wide and deep nets, and establish a stationarity rate of $O(T^{-2s(α-1)/[(1+s)(2α-1)]})$ under the same condition. Thirdly, we analyze standard gradient clipping (G-Clip) and show that it recovers the above rate for $α\ge1+s$ while in the very heavy-tailed regime $α<1+s$, it has a convergence rate $O(T^{-2s(α-1)/[(α-1)+s(2α-1)]})$ --- the first convergence guarantee in this regime for any stochastic gradient based method.

</details>


### [144] [VertiFuseX: Generalizable Financial Forecasting via Multi-Stream Temporal Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12793)
*Aashish Bohra,Vivek Vijay*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: VertiFuseX is a hybrid LSTM architecture with penultimate-layer vertical fusion that improves stock price prediction by preserving multi-scale temporal information, achieving significant accuracy gains with efficient computational performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stock price prediction is challenging due to noisy financial data. Existing deep learning models suffer from rigid decision-level fusion, ad hoc hyperparameter tuning, compressed final-layer outputs, causing information loss, overfitting, and poor cross-market generalization.

Method: VertiFuseX stacks and reweights penultimate features from LSTM, Bi-LSTM, and St-LSTM branches, integrates a parallel DNN stream, and jointly optimizes all components via backpropagation under fixed hyperparameters. This preserves richer intermediate temporal information across scales.

Result: On 15 years of global equity indices data (2010-2024) with strict out-of-sample testing: 30-54% MAPE reductions, over 40% improvements in MAE and RMSE vs LSTM baselines; outperforms 7 SOTA models in 33 metric-dataset comparisons; 675k parameters, 2.6 MB memory, 1.5 ms/sample inference.

Conclusion: VertiFuseX offers a lightweight, interpretable, deployment-ready framework for robust financial forecasting with significant accuracy improvements, computational efficiency, and validated economic performance in trading simulations.

Abstract: Stock price prediction remains challenging due to the non-stationary and noisy nature of financial time series. Existing deep learning models often rely on rigid decision-level fusion, ad hoc hyperparameter tuning, and compressed final-layer outputs, causing information loss, overfitting, and limited cross-market generalization. We propose VertiFuseX, a hybrid LSTM architecture using penultimate-layer vertical fusion of multi-scale temporal representations. VertiFuseX stacks and reweights penultimate features from LSTM, Bi-LSTM, and St-LSTM branches, integrates a parallel DNN stream, and jointly optimizes all components via backpropagation under a fixed hyperparameter configuration. This preserves richer intermediate temporal information across scales. Evaluated on 15 years (2010-2024) of closing prices from 10 global equity indices using strict chronological out-of-sample testing with the final 365 trading days held out, VertiFuseX achieves 30-54% MAPE reductions and over 40% improvements in MAE and RMSE versus LSTM-based baselines, and outperforms seven state-of-the-art models across 33 metric-dataset comparisons. Ablation studies confirm penultimate-layer fusion drives these gains over final-layer fusion and decision-level ensembling. Gradient-based saliency analysis shows consistent emphasis on mid-range dependencies at lags 9-15 days. Economic validation via algorithmic trading simulation under extreme market regimes shows reduced maximum drawdowns and superior risk-adjusted returns. With 675k parameters, a 2.6 MB memory footprint, and 1.5 ms/sample inference latency, VertiFuseX offers a lightweight, interpretable, deployment-ready framework for robust financial forecasting.

</details>


### [145] [RunningTensor: Generalizing Linear Attention to Higher-Order Recurrent States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12814)
*Luca Herranz-Celotti,Vincent Guigue*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RunningTensor generalizes linear attention's matrix memory to higher-order tensors, enabling $\mathcal{O}(W^o)$ working memory capacity while maintaining linear-time sequence processing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Linear attention and state-space models have matrix-valued recurrent states that limit the order of interactions representable in memory. The authors want to increase memory capacity beyond second-order tensor constraints to capture higher-order interactions.

Method: Introduces RunningTensor - an order-$o$ tensor memory that updates via rank-1 outer product and reads by contracting against $o-1$ vector queries. Order 2 recovers linear attention; order 3 is studied as proof of concept. Maintains both recurrent and parallel forms while staying linear in sequence length.

Result: Outperforms linear attention and SSM baselines on synthetic multi-query associative recall. After pretraining, improves performance on language-understanding and non-synthetic retrieval tasks.

Conclusion: Higher-order recurrent state (beyond matrix-valued state) provides useful additional memory capacity for sequence modeling tasks, enabling better capture of complex interactions while maintaining computational efficiency.

Abstract: Linear attention and state-space models provide linear-time sequence modeling, but their recurrent memory remains a second-order tensor (a matrix), limiting the order of interactions that can be represented in the state. We introduce the RunningTensor, which generalizes this memory to an order-$o$ tensor, updated by a rank-1 outer product and read by contracting against $o-1$ vector queries. Order $2$ recovers linear attention; we study order $3$ as a proof of concept, retaining both recurrent and parallel forms while remaining linear in sequence length $T$ and improving working memory capacity from $\mathcal{O}(W^2)$ to $\mathcal{O}(W^o)$. On synthetic multi-query associative recall, RunningTensor outperforms linear-attention and SSM baselines. After pretraining, it also improves performance on language-understanding and non-synthetic retrieval tasks, suggesting that higher-order recurrent state can provide useful additional memory capacity beyond matrix-valued state.

</details>


### [146] [GenOR-Twin: A Semantic Middleware for Integrating Operational Discourse with Mathematical Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12863)
*Rahimeh Neamatian Monemi,Shahin Gelareh,Lubin Cui,Nelson Maculan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GenOR-Twin is a neuro-symbolic framework that uses LLMs to translate operational logs into mathematical optimization constraints, creating a digital twin that can dynamically adapt optimization problems based on real-world disruptions while maintaining feasibility guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to bridge the translation gap between unstructured operational logs and rigorous mathematical optimization, enabling real-world systems to adapt to operational uncertainty and variability while maintaining the feasibility guarantees of exact combinatorial methods.

Method: The method combines Large Language Models as semantic translators to convert operational logs into formal constraints, a dynamic constraint injection mechanism for real-time modification of optimization problems, and an adaptive decision policy that selects between schedule repair or full re-optimization based on system slack analysis. The framework maintains bidirectional coupling between operational observations and virtual model states in a knowledge graph.

Result: The framework successfully turns static optimization models into resilient systems that adapt to operational uncertainty, demonstrated across six distinct optimization domains while maintaining the synchronization requirements of a proper digital twin.

Conclusion: GenOR-Twin represents a significant advancement in bridging neuro-symbolic AI and operations research, creating adaptable optimization systems that can handle real-world disruptions while preserving mathematical rigor and feasibility guarantees through LLM-powered semantic translation.

Abstract: We introduce GenOR-Twin, a neuro-symbolic framework that bridges the translation gap between unstructured operational logs and rigorous mathematical optimization. Our architecture uniquely positions Large Language Models as semantic translators rather than direct solvers, ensuring that the system retains the feasibility guarantees of exact combinatorial methods. { \color{red}We design a dynamic constraint injection mechanism (the runtime translation of qualitative disruption events into formal mathematical constraints) that allows the system to structurally modify the optimization problem's feasibility region in real-time based on qualitative human inputs. The resulting bidirectional coupling---where operational observations update the virtual model state and optimized decisions are reflected back into the Knowledge Graph---satisfies the synchronization requirement of a proper Digital Twin. The framework features an adaptive decision policy} that automatically selects between low-complexity schedule repair and full re-optimization by analyzing the available system slack. Finally, we demonstrate the generalization of this approach across six distinct optimization domains, {\color{red}turning static models into resilient systems that adapt to the operational uncertainty and variability of real-world environments.}

</details>


### [147] [What an odour descriptor corpus can and cannot measure: valence, attenuation, and the ceiling of the public record](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12875)
*Stylianos Kampakis,Fabio Rovai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper reveals significant limitations in machine olfaction datasets - descriptors don't measure the same thing across corpora, and valence (pleasantness) is the missing critical factor that explains more variance than all structural and descriptor information combined.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine olfaction relies on pooled public descriptor datasets, but it's unknown whether descriptor words measure the same thing across different corpora, what the measurement limits are, and what critical information might be missing from current approaches.

Method: Audited four Pyrfume corpora using McNemar's test to examine corpus effects, analyzed descriptor agreement statistics (tetrachoric vs. kappa), measured differential functioning of descriptors, and tested predictive performance of various approaches against human panel reliability.

Result: Corpora disagree heterogeneously across descriptors (I² = 80%); sources agree on which molecules deserve a descriptor but differ on application thresholds; valence (pleasantness) explains 54.6% of achievable variance vs. 33.9% from all descriptors; requires 15-20 human raters to saturate reliability.

Conclusion: Current pooled descriptor corpora have fundamental measurement inconsistencies, but the biggest limitation is missing valence information. Human pleasantness ratings are essential for capturing olfactory perception, and simply adding more descriptors or structural features won't bridge this gap.

Abstract: Machine olfaction trains on pooled public descriptor corpora, but whether a shared descriptor word measures the same thing across corpora has not been tested, nor has the ceiling of what any of them can measure.
  We audit four corpora from Pyrfume. Conditioning on the molecule makes McNemar's test the exact conditional test of the corpus effect. Corpora disagree heterogeneously across descriptors ($I^2 = 80\%$) and non-uniformly with labelling breadth ($z = 17.2$), so no single offset repairs pooling. Median tetrachoric agreement is 0.795 against median $κ$ of 0.413: sources largely concur on which molecules deserve a word and differ on how readily they apply it. Of 109 descriptors with an estimable effect, 36 show large differential functioning on the ETS scale.
  Against a human panel's reliability, Morgan fingerprints with the full RDKit descriptor block reach 32.9\% of achievable; adding every label from two merged corpora reaches 33.9\%. The gap does not close with model capacity, encoding choice, more molecules, or more words.
  The missing variance is valence. One pleasantness rating per molecule reaches 54.6\% of achievable (57.1\% on an independent older instrument). Valence recovered from descriptors ($ρ= 0.457$) yields only 16.6\%, so it must be measured. Five raters exceed structure plus the full descriptor record; fifteen to twenty saturate. We release a descriptor crosswalk and twenty machine-checked theorems.

</details>


### [148] [Large Distant Gradients Need Not Be Reliable: reliability-weighted credit assignment for long-horizon autoregressive forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12890)
*Junhao Zhao,David Michael Simberg,Jacob Kang,Colin Connor Kurniawan,Nan Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Internal-DW is a backward-only intervention for autoregressive forecasting that reliability-weights internal gradient routes, improving forecast accuracy by reducing the influence of unreliable distant gradients while preserving long-horizon supervision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In autoregressive forecasting, backpropagation through time (BPTT) causes distant gradients to dominate updates while amplifying both predictable signal and unpredictable noise together. Large distant gradients may not carry reliable learning signal despite their magnitude.

Method: Introduces Internal Dual-Wiener routing (Internal-DW), which preserves full forward rollouts and all horizon losses while reliability-weighting internal gradient routes. At each residual block, bounded Wiener gains are derived for identity and nonlinear routes to balance preserving predictable learning signal against suppressing unpredictable variation, estimated from route-level gradient statistics and an explicit noise model.

Result: On four history-dominated, weak-drive testbeds, Internal-DW reduces forecast error by 5.2%-13.8% relative to full BPTT, outperforms gradient clipping and Jacobian regularization on all four, and outperforms validation-selected truncated BPTT (TBPTT) on three. It extends or preserves the fitted optimal training-horizon range across testbeds.

Conclusion: Internal-DW shows that retaining long-horizon supervision doesn't require trusting every backward contribution equally. The method has clear applicability boundaries - benefits diminish when usable history is limited or when the sampler fails to represent dominant drive-dependent variation, but it provides principled gradient routing for autoregressive forecasting.

Abstract: In autoregressive forecasting, long prediction rollouts provide distant supervision, but backpropagation through time (BPTT) carries gradients from those losses through many autoregressive steps. Repeated Jacobian products can make distant gradients dominate the update while amplifying predictable signal and unpredictable noise together; a large distant gradient therefore need not carry reliable learning signal. Motivated by this observation, we introduce Internal Dual-Wiener routing (Internal-DW), a principled backward-only intervention that preserves the full forward rollout and all horizon losses while reliability-weighting internal gradient routes. At each residual block, we derive bounded Wiener gains for the identity and nonlinear routes that balance preserving predictable learning signal against suppressing unpredictable variation, and estimate them from route-level gradient statistics and an explicit noise model. In a controlled system with known gradient signal-to-noise ratio (SNR), we show that distant gradients can grow even as their SNR falls, and that Internal-DW reduces held-out error in recovering predictable gradient signals and improves forecasting. On four history-dominated, weak-drive testbeds, Internal-DW reduces forecast error by 5.2%-13.8% relative to full BPTT, outperforms gradient clipping and Jacobian regularization on all four, and outperforms validation-selected truncated BPTT (TBPTT) on three. It also extends or preserves the fitted optimal training-horizon range across these four testbeds. Across the full benchmark suite, the current Internal-DW estimator has a clear applicability boundary: its benefit diminishes or reverses when usable history is limited or when the selected sampler fails to represent dominant drive-dependent variation. The results show that retaining long-horizon supervision does not require trusting every backward contribution equally.

</details>


### [149] [Quantifying the Value of Privileged Information Using a PAC-Bayesian Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12891)
*Vasily Bokov,Sebastian Schmitt,Vedran Dunjko,Hao Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes an information-theoretic framework to quantify the potential value of privileged information in machine learning, using PAC-Bayes theory to measure maximum possible performance gains from auxiliary training data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Learning Using Privileged Information (LUPI) approaches lack a general framework explaining how and when privileged information transfers useful knowledge. Current theories are algorithm-specific and rely on setting-dependent proofs, creating a gap in understanding the fundamental mechanisms of PI transfer.

Method: An algorithm-agnostic, information-theoretic approach based on the PAC-Bayes framework that compares the tightest achievable risk bounds with and without PI to establish an upper limit on extractable gain. Introduces a metric that quantifies this potential directly from empirical training risk without requiring test-time data access.

Result: Demonstrates robust correspondence between the proposed training-time metric and true test-time performance gains in both supervised and unsupervised settings, validating that the framework accurately measures the potential value of privileged features.

Conclusion: The work provides a necessary step toward an information-theoretic understanding of LUPI, enabling quantification of privileged features' potential before model commitment, moving beyond algorithm-specific analyses to a more general framework.

Abstract: In practice, various learning scenarios provide access to auxiliary features exclusively during training. Incorporating such data to enhance model performance gave rise to a paradigm known as Learning Using Privileged Information (LUPI). While this extra information is intended to improve the resulting model, establishing a generalized, cohesive understanding of how privileged information (PI) transfers useful knowledge remains a challenge. Vapnik's original theory and subsequent works offer performance guarantees in certain cases, but these results are inherently per-algorithm and rely on setting-specific proof approaches. Consequently, a more general framework explaining how and when PI transfers useful knowledge is still missing. To bridge this gap, we introduce an algorithm-agnostic, information-theoretic approach based on the PAC-Bayes framework. Rather than asking whether a particular algorithm exploits PI, we ask how much value it could offer: comparing the tightest achievable risk bound with and without PI yields its potential - an upper limit on the extractable gain. We introduce a metric that quantifies this potential directly from empirical training risk, bypassing the need for test-time data access, and validate our findings in both supervised and unsupervised settings. The results demonstrate a robust correspondence between our training-time metric and true test-time performance gains. Ultimately, this work takes a necessary step toward an information-theoretic understanding of LUPI, and quantifying the potential of privileged features before committing to a model.

</details>


### [150] [Behavior Quotient Learning for Low-Rank Adaptation of LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12896)
*Pengyang Zhou,Xiaobin Tu,Zhengxi Liu,Rongkun Xue,Haochen Li,Miancan Liu,Ziyuan Chen,Yinggui Wang,Jinkui Ren,Xiantao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BQ-LoRA is a novel low-rank adaptation framework that addresses challenges in learning from diverse agent trajectories by organizing updates through a local behavior quotient manifold with two key components: behavior quotient balancing and decision preserving compression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based agent approaches face challenges: multi-LoRA approaches increase storage requirements and inference routing overhead, while single LoRA approaches struggle with learning from diverse agent trajectories under fixed rank budgets due to redundant behavioral changes and approximation distortion in weight space.

Method: BQ-LoRA uses a local behavior quotient manifold with two modules: 1) Behavior Quotient Balancing (BQB) constructs quotient manifold from decision distributions and reweights trajectory update directions based on local density in quotient tangent space; 2) Decision Preserving Compression (DPC) projects balanced gradient onto intrinsic fixed rank tangent space with joint control of effective weight error and decision distribution distortion.

Result: Experiments conducted on AppWorld and BrowseComp-Plus datasets comparing BQ-LoRA with standard LoRA and recent low-rank adaptation methods, with separate ablations evaluating complementary contributions of both components.

Conclusion: BQ-LoRA provides an effective framework for organizing trajectory updates in LLM-based agents through behavior quotient manifolds, addressing redundancy and compression challenges while maintaining decision quality under fixed rank constraints.

Abstract: LLM-based agents rely on heterogeneous interaction capabilities to accomplish complex tasks. Existing approaches often distribute these capabilities across multiple LoRA adapters, which increases adapter storage requirements and introduces routing overhead during inference. A single LoRA avoids this overhead, but learning from diverse agent trajectories under a fixed rank budget presents two challenges. First, trajectories with different interaction traces and parameter gradients can induce equivalent changes in decision distributions, causing repeated updates to overemphasize redundant behavioral changes. Second, an aggregated update may exceed the rank budget of the adapter, and approximating it in weight space can distort the decision changes that it is intended to produce. We propose BQ-LoRA, a low-rank adaptation framework that organizes trajectory updates through a local behavior quotient manifold. It contains two modules, i.e., behavior quotient balancing (BQB) and decision preserving compression (DPC). BQB constructs the quotient manifold from decision distributions and reweights trajectory update directions according to their local density in the quotient tangent space. DPC projects the balanced gradient onto the intrinsic fixed rank tangent space and refactorizes the resulting target by jointly controlling effective weight error and distortion of decision distributions. Experiments on AppWorld and BrowseComp-Plus compare BQ-LoRA with standard LoRA and recent low-rank adaptation methods, while separate ablations evaluate the complementary contributions of both components.

</details>


### [151] [Physical-State-Guided Diffusion Sampling for Full-Waveform Inversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12899)
*Chen Min,Haowen Jiang,Zheng Ma,Xiongbin Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PSG (Physical-State-Guided Diffusion Sampling) is a new method for full waveform inversion that combines diffusion models with physical constraints through a Gaussian bridge, improving geological structure recovery while maintaining FWI initialization and optimization history.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full waveform inversion (FWI) suffers from ill-posedness and nonlinearity, making accurate subsurface velocity reconstruction highly dependent on initialization and prior information. Existing diffusion posterior sampling methods can provide learned geological priors, but directly coupling their denoisers to nonlinear wave solvers often yields unreliable physical guidance.

Method: PSG couples a persistent physical velocity to the diffusion prior through a Gaussian bridge. The physical state is refined by waveform fitting regularized by the denoised velocity, and then guides the reverse diffusion process. This formulation separates wave-equation and denoiser gradients while preserving conventional FWI initialization and accumulated optimization history.

Result: On four OpenFWI families, PSG's terminal denoised estimates outperform classical and diffusion-based baselines under both clean and missing-trace acquisitions, and maintain strong structural recovery under measurement noise. Repeated stochastic runs preserve dominant geological structures, with ensemble variability concentrated near geological interfaces and positively associated with local inversion error. A frozen OpenFWI-trained prior also successfully supports inversion of larger Marmousi, Overthrust, and BP2004 Salt models without retraining.

Conclusion: PSG provides an effective framework for integrating learned geological priors with physical constraints in full waveform inversion, enabling robust recovery of complex geological structures while maintaining the advantages of conventional FWI initialization and optimization history.

Abstract: Full waveform inversion (FWI) estimates subsurface velocity from seismic recordings, but its ill-posedness and nonlinearity make accurate reconstruction strongly dependent on initialization and prior information. Diffusion posterior sampling provides a learned geological prior, yet directly coupling its denoiser to the nonlinear wave solver can yield unreliable physical guidance. We propose Physical-State-Guided Diffusion Sampling (PSG), which couples a persistent physical velocity to the diffusion prior through a Gaussian bridge. The physical state is refined by waveform fitting regularized by the denoised velocity, and in turn guides the reverse diffusion process. This formulation separates the wave-equation and denoiser gradients while preserving conventional FWI initialization and accumulated optimization history. On four OpenFWI families, PSG's terminal denoised estimates outperform classical and diffusion-based baselines under clean and missing-trace acquisitions and maintain strong structural recovery under measurement noise. Repeated stochastic runs preserve the dominant geological structures, with ensemble variability concentrated near geological interfaces and positively associated with local inversion error. A frozen OpenFWI-trained prior further supports inversion of the larger Marmousi, Overthrust, and BP2004 Salt models, recovering complex geological structures without retraining.

</details>


### [152] [Hidden in Rounds: Predicting the Time Cost of 802.11 Contention in Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12903)
*Satwat Bashir,Tasos Dagiuklas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A simulation study of federated learning over IEEE 802.11 wireless networks that evaluates communication performance metrics (frame-delivery ratio, throughput) and uses FedAvg with a communication time estimation model, finding consistent convergence but varying communication times across client densities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how federated learning performs over real wireless (IEEE 802.11) networks where communication constraints like frame delivery ratios and channel contention affect model update transmission, without actually simulating full model updates or end-to-end training time.

Method: Uses ns-3 network simulator to measure frame-delivery ratio and saturation throughput under varying client densities and offered loads. These metrics are used in a FedAvg trainer as proxies for update admission probability, with a separate equation to estimate communication time. The study evaluates 720 runs across multiple configurations (2 datasets, 2 data partitions, 6 client densities, 6 offered loads, 5 seeds).

Result: All 720 runs reached predefined target accuracy within round budget. Rounds-to-target showed little change with offered load, but communication time-to-target increased by about two orders of magnitude across client density range. A Bianchi-anchored estimator achieved 2.3% to 10.2% mean absolute percentage error on held-out configurations. No statistically distinguishable excluded-class accuracy gap was detected between uniform and persistent heterogeneous participation strategies.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that federated learning can converge under various wireless network conditions using estimated communication metrics rather than full simulation, but results are specific to evaluated configurations with no general convergence or fairness guarantees. Communication time scales significantly with client density despite consistent convergence behavior.

Abstract: Federated learning over IEEE~802.11 shares the wireless channel among clients that send model updates. We use ns-3 to measure the frame-delivery ratio and saturation throughput for different client densities and offered loads. A separate FedAvg trainer uses the frame-delivery ratio as a first-order proxy for the update-admission probability and uses an equation to estimate communication time.
