The method does not simulate the delivery of a complete model update or measure end-to-end training time. Across 720 evaluated runs with two datasets, two data partitions, six client densities, six offered loads, and five seeds, all runs reached their predefined target accuracy within the round budget. Rounds-to-target changed little with offered load, while communication time-to-target increased by about two orders of magnitude across the client-density range. A Bianchi-anchored estimator produced a mean absolute percentage error from $2.3\%$ to $10.2\%$ on held-out configurations. This error is measured against communication time constructed from the same round-duration equation, not against independently measured completion time. We also compare uniform participation with persistent heterogeneous participation. The study does not detect a statistically distinguishable excluded-class accuracy gap over five seeds, but the confidence intervals are wide. The results apply only to the evaluated configurations and do not provide a general convergence or fairness guarantee.

</details>


### [153] [Offline Reinforcement Learning for Wind Farm Control: A Wind Tunnel Study under Dynamic Wind Directions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12905)
*Yuhan Su,Hongyang Dong,Simone Tamaro,Filippo Campagnolo,Carlo L. Bottasso,Xiaowei Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An offline reinforcement learning algorithm called MTD3-BC is proposed for wind farm power maximization through yaw control under changing wind directions, achieving ~10% power gains over baseline with reduced computational costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wind farm power maximization is challenging due to wake effects and changing wind directions. Existing methods require extensive simulator interactions (online RL) or accurate wake models (model-based approaches), which are computationally expensive or require difficult-to-obtain models.

Method: Proposed Modified Twin Delayed Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient with Behavior Cloning (MTD3-BC), an offline RL algorithm that learns from precollected data without online simulator interactions. Introduced an action consistency term for smooth yaw adjustments and avoids wake model requirements.

Result: Experimental validation shows MTD3-BC achieves ~10% farm-level power gains over greedy baseline, matches performance of model-based wake-steering benchmark, while using no wake model and requiring only a small fraction of online RL's training cost.

Conclusion: MTD3-BC provides an efficient, model-free solution for wind farm control under variable wind directions, demonstrating the feasibility of offline RL for experimental wind farm optimization with significant performance improvements and reduced computational requirements.

Abstract: This paper addresses the wind farm power maximization problem in the presence of wind direction changes. Specifically, a model-free Modified Twin Delayed Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient with Behavior Cloning (MTD3-BC) algorithm is proposed to tackle this task through yaw control under varying wind direction conditions. MTD3-BC is an offline reinforcement learning (RL) algorithm that aims to infer good behavior from only a precollected offline dataset. Additionally, to ensure smooth and moderate yaw adjustments, a new action consistency term is introduced into the policy optimization objective. Unlike online RL methods, MTD3-BC does not require extensive interactions with a wind farm simulator during training, significantly reducing computational costs and training time. A wind tunnel experiment is conducted to validate the effectiveness of the algorithm under varying wind directions. The results demonstrate that MTD3-BC successfully mitigates wake effects, delivering farm-level power gains of approximately 10\% over the baseline greedy strategy and performance on par with a data-calibrated model-based wake-steering benchmark, while requiring no wake model and only a small fraction of the training cost of online RL. To our knowledge, this is the first time an offline RL wind farm control policy has been validated and demonstrated experimentally.

</details>


### [154] [A Large-Scale AIS Dataset from Finnish Water](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12938)
*Debayan Bhattacharya,Ikram Ul Haq,Carlos Pichardo Vicencio,Sebastien Lafond*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Researchers introduce a comprehensive AIS dataset from Finnish waters in the Baltic Sea region, containing 229 million data points including unique data from Finnish lakes, with applications for studying maritime activities and vessel behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AIS data has evolved beyond collision prevention to become a versatile tool for maritime research. The Baltic Sea region is strategically important with intercontinental cargo routes, military activities, and frozen water expanses, yet comprehensive AIS datasets for this specific area are limited. The inclusion of Finnish lakes data addresses a unique gap in maritime research.

Method: The authors curate and categorize existing AIS datasets, collect new AIS data specifically from the Baltic Sea area (including Finnish lakes), compile 229 million data points, analyze ship types and relevant features, and create visualizations of maritime traffic patterns to aid comprehension and analysis.

Result: A comprehensive AIS dataset from Finnish waters and Baltic Sea region containing 229 million data points with detailed ship type categorization and feature analysis. The dataset uniquely includes data from Finnish lakes, providing researchers with a valuable resource for studying maritime activities in this dynamic region.

Conclusion: This new AIS dataset serves as a catalyst for innovation and collaboration in maritime research, offering researchers a gateway to deeper insights into maritime activities in the Baltic Sea region and Finnish lakes, with potential applications across diverse maritime domains.

Abstract: This research paper contributes to the maritime research community by introducing a comprehensive AIS dataset from Finnish waters, specifically the Baltic Sea region. AIS data, initially designed for collision prevention, have evolved into a versatile tool with applications across diverse maritime domains. Our paper not only curates and categorises existing AIS datasets but also introduces a collected AIS dataset from the Baltic Sea area, renowned for its intercontinental cargo routes, military activities, and frozen water expanses. This dataset includes 229 millions data points and provides researchers with a resource for studying maritime activities and vessel behaviour in this dynamic region, notably distinguished by the inclusion of data from Finnish lakes. Our analysis includes a detailed listing of ship types and relevant features, empowering researchers to explore various maritime domains. To enhance comprehension and analysis, we provide visualisations of maritime traffic patterns. By publishing this AIS dataset, we aim to catalyse innovation and collaboration in maritime research, offering a gateway to deeper insights into maritime activities in the Baltic Sea and Finnish lakes.

</details>


### [155] [Information-Induced Training Geometry: Exact Reduction, Canonical Completion, and Structured Expressivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12991)
*Zavier Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper develops a mathematical framework for understanding how training data constraints determine optimizer geometry through partial information channels, providing closed-form solutions for positive-definite completion and exact variational reduction in affine-invariant Riemannian settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Training data constrains optimizer geometry through partial information channels, but it's unclear how this partial information determines the complete positive cometric or what degrees of freedom remain unidentified.

Method: The paper uses affine-invariant Riemannian geometry to resolve full-column-rank positive-definite compression problems. It develops a framework of split-Hadamard metric submetry, explicit completion formulas, gauge-invariant rank stratification, and closed-form pullback pair metrics to analyze visible-metric motion versus subspace rotation.

Result: The central result shows that compression maps are split-Hadamard metric submetries with explicit unique completions that are affine-invariant nearest full geometries. The framework yields exact full-to-visible variational reduction, closed-form prior-data shrinkage, and quantitative bounds on geometry recovery.

Conclusion: The work provides a complete characterization of exact reduction, reference-dependent completion, and structured expressivity for finite-dimensional affine-invariant optimization models, explaining how partial information determines optimizer geometry through mathematically precise mechanisms.

Abstract: Training data constrains optimizer geometry through the covectors visible to a declared information channel. We study how such partial information determines a full positive cometric relative to a reference and which degrees of freedom remain unidentified. Our central result resolves full-column-rank positive-definite compression under affine-invariant Riemannian geometry. The compression map is a split-Hadamard metric submetry and admits an explicit unique completion that is the affine-invariant nearest full geometry realizing a visible target and yields exact full-to-visible variational reduction. When the channel moves, the completions form a gauge-invariant rank stratification of the positive-definite cone. Its closed-form pullback pair metric separates visible-metric motion from subspace rotation through a reference-mismatch weight, yields an explicit positive-semidefinite multi-direction Gram matrix, and exposes the precise singularity of reference-valued modes. The mechanism is explained by a metric theorem equating ball submetry, attained fiber distance, and lossless reduction of every monotone radial visible decision problem. A smooth split-Hadamard theorem supplies coherent information sheets, proximal commutation, and solution-wise gradient-flow lifting. The positive-definite realization also gives closed-form prior-data shrinkage. Diagonal and block optimizer families reduce to relative-interior conic image tests with valid facial certificates, while deterministic and finite-sample bounds quantify recovery of the visible geometry and its subspace. Together these results characterize exact reduction, reference-dependent completion, and structured expressivity for the stated finite-dimensional affine-invariant model.

</details>


### [156] [Dimension-Corrected Hitting Times for Heavy-Tailed Spectral Emergence in Neural Optimizer Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12994)
*Zongmin Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The study analyzes when heavy-tailed spectral distributions emerge in neural network weights, formulating it as a censored hitting-time problem and finding a dimension-corrected spectral-gap law that better explains onset timing than previous explanations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Heavy-tailed spectral densities in neural network weights are widely used as diagnostics of implicit self-regularization, but there's poor understanding of when these heavy tails emerge during training, particularly the step complexity of their formation.

Method: Formulated spectral heavy-tail formation as a right-censored hitting-time problem. Used controlled full-batch teacher-student dynamics experiments, finite-onset regression analysis, right-censored lognormal accelerated-failure-time models, theoretical proofs about linear networks and Adam recurrences, and empirical analysis of projected-kernel profiles and pretrained transformer weights.

Result: Found dimension-corrected spectral-gap law (τ_HT ≈ CΔ₁^(-γ)d^ρ) with R²=0.683, γ=0.626, ρ=0.772 across 330 runs. Right-censored models favored dimension-corrected model over gap-only (AIC improved from 706.62 to 628.70). Theoretical results showed early loss dynamics don't determine spectral tails, Adam recurrences don't imply spectral redistribution, and projected singular-basis spreading implies hitting-time bound.

Conclusion: The work provides a reproducible spectral hitting-time law with rigorous conditional theory, demonstrating that dimension-corrected spectral gaps better explain heavy-tail onset than first-step spike-bulk gap alone, but does not claim Adam necessarily generates heavy tails from first principles.

Abstract: Heavy-tailed empirical spectral densities of neural-network weight matrices are widely used as diagnostics of implicit self-regularization, but the step complexity of heavy-tail emergence remains poorly understood. We formulate spectral heavy-tail formation as a right-censored hitting-time problem: a run that does not reach a heavy-tail diagnostic within the observation horizon is treated as censored rather than discarded. In controlled full-batch teacher--student dynamics, we find that the first-step spike--bulk gap alone does not explain onset time. Instead, finite-onset regression supports a dimension-corrected spectral-gap law, (τ_{\mathrm{HT}}\approx CΔ_1^{-γ}d^ρ), with (R^2=0.683), (γ=0.626), and (ρ=0.772) across 330 completed runs. Right-censored lognormal accelerated-failure-time models further favor the dimension-corrected model over a gap-only model, improving AIC from 706.62 to 628.70. Theoretically, we prove that exact early loss dynamics in linear networks do not determine factor spectral tails, that Adam recurrences alone do not imply spectral redistribution, and that projected singular-basis spreading implies contraction of a spectral-tail potential and hence a dimension-corrected hitting-time bound. Empirically, projected-kernel profiles support the sufficient spreading mechanism, Adam and AdamW agree under tested grids, GD and signGD do not reach onset in the same regimes, and real pretrained Qwen2.5-0.5B and Pythia-70M transformer weights show non-Gaussian spectral-tail structure relative to matched Gaussian nulls. The result is a reproducible spectral hitting-time law with rigorous conditional theory, not a claim that Adam necessarily generates heavy tails from first principles.

</details>


### [157] [A Full Adam Theorem for Spectral Heavy-Tail Onset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12996)
*Zongmin Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proves a comprehensive "Adam theorem" characterizing spectral heavy-tail onset in Gaussian Stein-Hermite teacher-student models with closed-form state evolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish a complete theoretical understanding of Adam optimization dynamics in neural networks, specifically how spectral properties (heavy-tail onset) emerge from the algorithm's momentum and denominator mechanisms, and to determine what can and cannot be theoretically captured about Adam's behavior.

Method: The authors use a closed Gaussian Stein-Hermite teacher-student state-evolution model. They start from full-batch Adam recurrences, apply Stein-Hermite calculus for population gradients, prove finite-width covariance concentration, transform multi-step Adam momentum into a non-centered Gaussian sign kernel, control the Adam denominator via basis-homogenization theorem, derive regularly varying projected updates using Hermite edge-transfer theorem, push responses through exact Gram updates, and prove approximate-target KL contraction with matching hitting bounds.

Result: The main result is a hitting time law: τ_ε = Θ(Δ_1^{-γ}d^ρlog(Ψ_0/ε)), where Δ_1 is the first spike-bulk spectral gap. The theorem is "full" because every step from Adam's momentum and denominator to the spectral hitting law is formalized. Additional results show: 1) a stronger arbitrary-gradient Adam theorem is impossible, and 2) exact two-step linear-network loss dynamics cannot identify factor spectra or heavy-tail hitting times.

Conclusion: This work provides a complete theoretical characterization of Adam's spectral behavior in specific teacher-student settings, establishing fundamental limits on what can be proven about Adam optimization while offering precise hitting time bounds for heavy-tail onset phenomena.

Abstract: We prove a full Adam theorem for spectral heavy-tail onset in a closed Gaussian Stein-Hermite teacher-student state-evolution model. The theorem begins with the actual full-batch Adam recurrences, derives the population gradient by Stein-Hermite calculus, proves finite-width covariance concentration, converts multi-step Adam momentum into an exact non-centered Gaussian sign kernel, controls the diagonal Adam denominator by a basis-homogenization theorem, derives a regularly varying projected update response from a Hermite edge-transfer theorem, pushes the response through the exact Gram update, and proves approximate-target KL contraction with matching upper and lower hitting bounds. The final law is (τ_\varepsilon=Θ(Δ_1^{-γ}d^ρ\log(Ψ_0/\varepsilon))), where (Δ_1) is the first spike-bulk spectral gap. The result is full in the following precise sense: every step from Adam's momentum and denominator to the spectral hitting law is formalized inside the closed state-evolution model. We also prove that a stronger arbitrary-gradient Adam theorem is impossible, and that exact two-step linear-network loss dynamics do not identify factor spectra or heavy-tail hitting times.

</details>


### [158] [Dual-guided Hierarchical Edge Localization for Large-scale Optimal Transport Across Dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13010)
*Wenzhou Xia,Qiaoqiao Ding,Jingwei Liang,Xiaoqun Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HELLO is a hierarchical solver for large-scale optimal transport that uses dual potentials to guide coarse-to-fine initialization and refinement, achieving linear memory complexity and scaling to millions of points with GPU acceleration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard discrete optimal transport requires solving linear programs with quadratically many transport variables, which is computationally prohibitive at large scales (millions of points). Current methods struggle with both runtime and memory efficiency when dealing with massive datasets.

Method: HELLO uses a hierarchical approach: 1) Edge localization via coarse-to-fine initialization using dual potentials across recursive subsampling hierarchy, 2) Refinement that iteratively inserts largest dual violators per row/column while maintaining KKT tolerance, and 3) Budgeted pruning for linear memory complexity. For exact arithmetic, it uses a symbolic lexicographic rule guaranteeing finite termination at global optimum.

Result: HELLO achieves lower transport objectives with order-of-magnitude runtime improvements over baselines, scales to 1.28 million samples per marginal in 8192 dimensions on a single H100 GPU using 41.6 GiB peak memory, and maintains full relative KKT residual below 10^(-6). It also generalizes to various OT variants.

Conclusion: HELLO provides an efficient hierarchical solver for large-scale optimal transport that overcomes computational bottlenecks while maintaining theoretical guarantees, enabling practical applications of OT at unprecedented scales across diverse machine learning tasks.

Abstract: Optimal transport (OT) compares distributions and aligns datasets in machine learning, yet unregularized discrete OT requires a linear program with quadratically many transport variables. We propose HELLO, a hierarchical solver that casts large-scale discrete OT as edge localization and uses dual potentials to guide both coarse-to-fine initialization and within-level refinement. Initialization propagates coarse dual potentials across a recursive subsampling hierarchy to assign candidate edges. Refinement then iteratively inserts the largest dual violators in each row and column until the relative KKT residual meets a prescribed tolerance, while budgeted pruning ensures linear memory complexity. For exact-arithmetic refinement, we prove finite termination at a global optimum under a symbolic lexicographic rule. At the million-point scale, HELLO attains lower transport objectives with order-of-magnitude runtime improvements over strong baselines across feature dimensions from single digits to thousands. It further scales to 1.28 million samples per marginal in 8192 dimensions on a single H100, using 41.6 GiB peak GPU memory while satisfying a full relative KKT residual below $10^{-6}$. Beyond standard discrete OT, the framework supports general pairwise costs and serves as a scalable balanced-OT oracle for semi-discrete OT, Gromov--Wasserstein, unbalanced OT, and OT-based Flow Matching.

</details>


### [159] [Attention Quantization for Tabular Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13031)
*Jonas M. Kübler,Benjamin Jäger,Klemens Flöge,Noah Hollmann,Frank Hutter*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Tabular foundation models face different efficiency challenges than LLMs; FP8 quantization of queries/keys/values in attention calculation provides 1.7x speedup without accuracy loss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With rising adoption of tabular foundation models, their inference performance optimization becomes crucial. Unlike LLMs, these models have different size and serving patterns, requiring specialized efficiency approaches.

Method: Developed FP8 quantization strategy for queries, keys, and values in attention calculation, using explicit FP8 matrix multiplication instructions. Key insight: must align quantization error in test rows with training rows to prevent accuracy drops.

Result: Triton kernel achieves up to 1.7x speedup over regular 16-bit kernels. On TabPFN-v3 and TabICLv2 models, no relevant accuracy loss observed across TabArena and BeyondArena benchmarks.

Conclusion: For tabular foundation models, attention calculation optimization via FP8 quantization is more impactful than weight or KV cache quantization (common in LLMs), providing significant speedup without sacrificing accuracy.

Abstract: With the recent rise and adoption of tabular foundation models, optimizing their inference performance becomes an emerging field for efficiency research. While the models are architecturally similar to transformer-based large language models (LLMs), the size and serving patterns differ significantly. We show that the focus should be on the attention calculation and less on weight or KV cache quantization, which are more popular in LLMs. We develop a quantization strategy for queries, keys, and values to FP8 and use explicit FP8 matrix multiplication instructions to speed up the attention calculation. We find that it is crucial to align the quantization error in the test rows with the quantization error in the training rows, as otherwise the accuracy drops drastically. Our Triton kernel achieves a speedup up to 1.7x over regular 16-bit kernels, and we show that on TabPFN-v3 and TabICLv2 there is no relevant accuracy loss across TabArena and BeyondArena.

</details>


### [160] [Groupoid-Based Internal State Representations for Reinforcement Learning with Local Symmetries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13035)
*Ben Opperman,Eduardo Alonso,Esther Mondragón*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Groupoid-based RL framework for dynamically discovering and exploiting local, state-dependent symmetries to improve sample efficiency in complex environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL methods rely on fixed group actions or predefined abstractions, assuming globally structured MDPs, which limits their ability to exploit modularity and local, context-dependent regularities found in realistic environments.

Method: Proposes a reinforcement learning framework using groupoids to capture local, state-dependent symmetries. The agent maintains orbit representatives with transporters that map raw states to canonical forms, enabling learning in symmetry-reduced space while preserving local distinctions.

Result: Empirical results show improved sample efficiency and convergence in dense and large-scale environments with strong partial symmetries, yielding substantial performance gains over standard Q-learning.

Conclusion: Dynamically exploiting local symmetry provides a practical and mathematically principled route to scalable and generalizable reinforcement learning.

Abstract: Symmetries play a central role in reducing the complexity of reinforcement learning problems, yet most existing approaches rely on fixed group actions or predefined state abstractions. Classical reinforcement learning algorithms typically assume a globally structured Markov decision process with uniformly applicable actions and transitions, an assumption that limits their ability to exploit modularity and local, context-dependent regularities present in many realistic environments. We propose a reinforcement learning framework using groupoids to capture local, state-dependent symmetries and support the dy- namic discovery of equivalence structures during interaction. The agent maintains orbit representatives together with transporters that map raw states to canonical forms, enabling learning and decision-making to be performed in a symmetry-reduced space while preserving local distinctions. Empirical results demonstrate that the proposed groupoid-based approach improves sample efficiency and convergence in dense and large-scale environments exhibiting strong partial symmetries, yielding substantial performance gains over standard Q-learning. These findings show that dynamically exploiting local symmetry provides a practical and mathematically principled route to scalable and generalisable reinforcement learning.

</details>


### [161] [DynSHAP: Towards Explainable Dynamic Survival Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13042)
*Nastasya Anokhina,Jonas Jürß,Pietro Liò*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DynSHAP is a SHAP framework specifically designed for dynamic survival analysis that handles longitudinal, irregular inputs and functional survival outputs, providing interpretable feature attributions over time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning models for dynamic survival analysis achieve strong performance but are black boxes that limit clinical trust. Existing explainability methods cannot handle the unique challenges of DSA: longitudinal, irregular inputs and functional survival outputs simultaneously.

Method: Extends SHAP framework to DSA by treating time-feature pairs as players in Shapley game. Introduces Temporal DynSHAP which learns linear dependencies in features over time and uses conditional sampling to address temporal dependencies in explanations.

Result: Temporal DynSHAP recovers temporally dependent features more accurately than marginal estimators on synthetic data. Applied to real-world clinical datasets and DSA architectures, DynSHAP produces faithful attributions showing which patient information drove predictions and when.

Conclusion: DynSHAP provides a practical interpretability solution for dynamic survival analysis models, enabling clinical trust by revealing the temporal dynamics of feature importance in survival predictions.

Abstract: Deep learning models for dynamic survival analysis (DSA) achieve strong predictive performance by incorporating longitudinal patient data, but their black box nature limits clinical trust and adoption. Existing explainability methods cannot handle longitudinal, irregular inputs and functional survival outputs simultaneously, which limits their usability in DSA. We propose DynSHAP, a SHAP framework suited specifically for dynamic survival analysis. It extends common marginal SHAP estimators to this setting by treating time--feature pairs as players in the Shapley game. We further introduce Temporal DynSHAP, which learns linear dependencies in features over time and uses conditional sampling to address them in explanations. When applied to synthetic data with known ground-truth attributions, Temporal DynSHAP recovers temporally dependent features more accurately than marginal estimators for a given state-of-the-art model. Applied to two real-world clinical datasets and two DSA architectures, DynSHAP produces attributions faithful to model learning, allowing medical experts to see which patient information drove the prediction and when.

</details>


### [162] [Transfer Learning for Evolving Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13039)
*Ricardo Ribeiro Pereira,Jacopo Bono,Hugo Ferreira,Pedro Ribeiro,Pedro Saleiro,Pedro Bizarro,Carlos Soares*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Transfer Learning for Evolving Domains (TrED) formalizes a continuous trajectory of domain evolution as a unified transfer learning problem, where classical settings represent different snapshots rather than isolated problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world applications involve evolving data availability over time, but current transfer learning research is segmented into isolated sub-areas (domain generalization, adaptation, multi-domain learning) that only represent static snapshots of this evolution.

Method: Formalizes TrED with three components: data availability process (environment-fixed), learning protocol (method-chosen), and evaluation criterion (scores entire trajectory of models). Examines existing literature to identify building blocks for solutions.

Result: Most existing methods are tailored to single regimes; even strongest candidates don't optimize the whole trajectory. Classical transfer learning settings are recovered as regimes within the TrED framework.

Conclusion: TrED is a well-posed but unsolved problem that better represents real-world domain evolution, representing an important direction for future transfer learning research.

Abstract: Transfer learning explores how to leverage knowledge from various tasks or domains (sources) to enhance predictive performance in related tasks or domains (targets). Typically, transfer learning research is segmented into several isolated sub-areas (such as domain generalisation, domain adaptation, or multi-domain learning), each making distinct assumptions about target data availability, namely how much data and how many labels are available at training time. However, in many real-world applications, data availability is not fixed but evolves over time, as instances and labels are progressively collected from a new domain. Each of the classical settings then describes only a snapshot of a trajectory that a deployed system must traverse in full. We formalise this trajectory as a transfer learning problem in its own right, Transfer Learning for Evolving Domains (TrED), specified by a data availability process fixed by the environment, a learning protocol that the method is free to choose, and an evaluation criterion that scores the whole trajectory of models rather than a single one. Within this formalism, the classical settings are recovered as regimes that a learner may pass through, rather than as separate problems that TrED concatenates. We then examine the transfer learning literature to identify mechanisms that are promising building blocks for a solution, and find that most methods are tailored to a single regime and that even the strongest existing candidates do not yet optimise the whole trajectory. We argue that TrED is a well-posed and unsolved problem, and an important direction for future research.

</details>


### [163] [MAxBench: A Multinomial Concept Recovery Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13072)
*Divya Appapogu,Freya Behrens,Yonatan Belinkov,Aaron Mueller*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MAxBench is a new evaluation framework for comparing different geometry types in multinomial concept representations, finding affine subspaces outperform rank-one/linear subspaces for steering, but no method consistently beats prompting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current interpretability research focuses on binary concepts with simple steering directions, but many real-world concepts are multinomial (like animals, countries) with more complex geometries. There's uncertainty about appropriate representation geometries and effective recovery methods for such concepts.

Method: Introduced MAxBench, a geometry-agnostic evaluation framework that compares concept representations through sampling. Evaluated 10 localization methods covering 5 geometry types across 6 concepts and 4 models.

Result: Affine subspaces steer more reliably with greater recall than rank-one or linear subspaces; much of this advantage comes from better non-zero offsets; manifold steering is competitive when applicable; no method consistently outperforms prompting, aligning with prior binary concept findings.

Conclusion: Interpretability research should expand beyond binary concepts to include more varied concept structures, as different geometry types have different performance characteristics for multinomial concepts.

Abstract: Fine-grained control of language model behaviors (e.g., steering) is among the more actionable outcomes of interpretability research. For binary concepts such as refusal, a single direction in activation space often suffices for steering. However, many concepts are not binary: Animals and Countries contain many subcategories, each with multiple instances. For these concepts, the search space over possible representation geometries is far larger than for binary concepts; it is thus not clear what geometries are most appropriate, nor what methods are most effective at recovering them. In this work, we introduce MAxBench, a geometry-agnostic evaluation framework for multinomial concept representations based on sampling from the recovered concept representation. We use MAxBench to compare 10 localization methods (covering 5 geometry types) across 6 concepts and 4 models. Using this framework, we find that (i) affine subspaces steer more reliably and have greater recall than rank-one or linear subspaces; (ii) much of this advantage is due to better non-zero offsets rather than the choice of bases; (iii) manifold steering is competitive with the best methods when applicable; and (iv) no method consistently outperforms prompting, in alignment with prior findings on binary concepts. These findings underscore the importance of expanding the scope of interpretability research and meta-evaluation to concepts with more varied structure.

</details>


### [164] [Quantile-based Loss Filtering for Outlier-Robust Stochastic Gradient Descent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13040)
*Jamie Haddock,Anna Ma,Elizaveta Rebrova*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Quantile-k-Loss SGD (QkL-SGD) - a robust optimization method that samples k component losses each iteration and updates using uniformly random selection from the lower empirical q-quantile, outperforming standard SGD and min-k-loss SGD in corrupted finite-sum settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need robust optimization methods for finite-sum problems with corrupted component functions where gradients may be highly unreliable. Existing methods like min-k-loss SGD can stall by repeatedly selecting nearly solved components, while standard SGD is vulnerable to corrupted gradients.

Method: Quantile-k-Loss SGD (QkL-SGD): at each iteration, sample k component losses, compute their empirical distribution, and update using an index chosen uniformly from the lower empirical q-quantile. This general framework includes min-k-loss (q=1/k) and median-based methods as special cases.

Result: 1) Prove linear convergence under standard convexity assumptions when sample size scales with number of corruptions and subset strong-convexity threshold. 2) Small-sample probabilistic analysis covering any sample size k, where convergence depends on probability of selecting outlier and curvature of selected good step. 3) Experiments on polynomial regression, regularized logistic regression, and hinge loss show intermediate quantiles outperform both standard SGD and min-k-loss SGD.

Conclusion: Quantile-k-Loss SGD provides a flexible and robust optimization framework for corrupted finite-sum problems, with intermediate quantiles offering better performance than extreme methods by balancing robustness with informative updates and avoiding the stagnation issues of min-k-loss approaches.

Abstract: We study loss-based filtering for finite-sum optimization with a subset of corrupted component functions whose gradients may be highly unreliable. Motivated by minimum-loss-based SGD (min-$k$-loss) and quantile-based methods for corrupted linear systems, we propose and analyze a general loss-filtering framework -- Quantile-\(k\)-Loss SGD (Q\(k\)L-SGD) -- that samples \(k\) component losses at each iteration and updates using an index chosen uniformly from the lower empirical \(q\)-quantile. We prove linear convergence of this family of methods under standard convexity assumptions, requiring the sample size to scale with the number of corruptions and a subset strong-convexity threshold. For the cases when large enough sampling is impossible or undesirable, we give a complementary small-sample probabilistic analysis that covers any sample size $k$ and the convergence behavior depends on the probability of selecting an outlier and on the curvature of the selected good step. Experiments on polynomial regression, regularized logistic regression, and regularized hinge loss show that intermediate quantiles often outperform both standard SGD and min-\(k\)-loss SGD. In particular, min-\(k\) often stalls by repeatedly selecting nearly solved components, while intermediate quantiles retain robustness and produce more informative updates.

</details>


### [165] [Robust Policy Optimization via Adversarial Importance Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13044)
*Amine Andam,Jamal Bentahar,Mustapha Hedabou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ADVIS introduces an importance sampling method for robust DRL that requires no extra environment interactions or auxiliary networks, provides a modular library for reproducible evaluations, and shows that proper evaluation requires diverse attacker configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current robust DRL methods have limitations across three stages: algorithm design often requires additional environment interactions or auxiliary networks; implementation lacks standardized tools for reproducibility; and evaluation can overestimate robustness by using limited attacker configurations.

Method: 1. ADVIS (Adversarial Importance Sampling): uses importance sampling over trajectories from standard training to estimate verifiable worst-case returns without extra environment interactions or auxiliary networks. 2. advrl library: modular PyTorch library with clean single-file implementations of robustness methods and attacks. 3. Enhanced evaluation: tests against 6-14x more attacker configurations than prior work to avoid overestimation.

Result: Demonstrated effectiveness on continuous control environments relative to existing baselines. Showed that optimal adversarial hyperparameters don't transfer across agents, revealing prior evaluation approaches could overestimate robustness. The advrl library enables reproducible and traceable evaluations.

Conclusion: The paper presents a comprehensive approach to robust DRL addressing algorithm design, implementation, and evaluation stages. ADVIS provides a practical method for optimizing worst-case returns, advrl enables standardized benchmarking, and the evaluation methodology reveals the importance of diverse attacker configurations for accurate robustness assessment.

Abstract: Significant progress has been made in safeguarding deep reinforcement learning (DRL) policies against input perturbations. Developing robust DRL involves three main stages: algorithm design, implementation, and evaluation. In this work, we identify and address a key limitation at each stage. First, we introduce Adversarial Importance Sampling (Advis), a method that uses importance sampling over trajectories from standard training to estimate and optimize verifiable worst-case returns. Advis satisfies three desirable criteria not jointly achieved by prior work: it requires no additional environment interactions, no auxiliary networks, and captures long-term robustness. Second, we introduce advrl, a modular PyTorch library that provides clean, single-file implementations of existing robustness methods and adversarial attacks, facilitating rapid prototyping and enabling reproducible and traceable evaluations. Third, we revisit evaluation under learned adversaries and show that optimal adversarial hyperparameters do not transfer across agents, which can lead to an overestimation of robustness when using a limited set of attacker configurations. Accordingly, we evaluate policies against a large and diverse set of attackers, using 6-14x more configurations than prior work. Finally, we evaluate our approach on continuous control environments, demonstrating its effectiveness relative to existing baselines. The code is available at: https://github.com/AmineAndam04/advrl

</details>


### [166] [MCRL2: Multi-resource Cross-attention-based Representation Learning-augmented Reinforcement Learning for Cloud Microservice Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13048)
*Tiangang Li,Shi Ying,Xiangbo Tian,Chuan Shi,Ding Xiao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MCRL2: A reinforcement learning approach with multi-resource cross-attention representation learning for microservice scheduling that outperforms existing methods in load balancing and performance metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current microservice scheduling faces challenges with dynamic resource imbalance under fluctuating workloads, nonlinear coupling across multiple resource dimensions, and heterogeneity of microservice demands. Existing RL approaches struggle to capture complex interdependencies among heterogeneous resources and neglect learning informative system representations.

Method: Proposes MCRL2 with two key components: 1) MCRL representation learning using multi-resource cross-attention mechanism to capture interactions among nodes, resources, and microservices, and 2) Augments reinforcement learning with MCRL-enhanced actor-critic architecture combined with maximum entropy objective for better state representation.

Result: Extensive experiments on real production cluster traces show MCRL2 significantly outperforms existing baselines in load balancing, scheduling success rate, and average completion time across diverse workload patterns.

Conclusion: MCRL2 addresses limitations of current RL approaches for microservice scheduling by incorporating representation learning with cross-attention mechanisms, leading to more stable and effective scheduling decisions in dynamic, heterogeneous resource environments.

Abstract: Efficient microservice scheduling is crucial for maintaining load balance across nodes in data centers and ensuring high quality of service. However, achieving this in practice remains challenging due to dynamic resource imbalance under fluctuating workloads, nonlinear coupling across multiple resource dimensions, and the heterogeneity of microservice resource demands. While reinforcement learning-based approaches have shown promise, they struggle to capture the complex interdependencies among heterogeneous resources and neglect the importance of learning informative system representations. To address these limitations, we propose MCRL2, a novel reinforcement learning approach augmented with multi-resource cross-attention-based representation learning for microservice scheduling. Specifically, we first propose MCRL, a novel representation learning approach that captures structured and informative interactions among nodes, resources, and microservices via a multi-resource cross-attention mechanism. Then, MCRL2 augments reinforcement learning through MCRL-enhanced actor-critic architecture combined with a maximum entropy objective, improving system state expressiveness and leading to more stable and effective scheduling decisions. Extensive experiments on real production cluster traces demonstrate that MCRL2 significantly outperforms existing baselines in load balancing, scheduling success rate and average completion time across diverse workload patterns.

</details>


### [167] [A Unified and Constrained View of Regularization-Based Robust Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13050)
*Amine Andam,Jamal Bentahar,Mustapha Hedabou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper unifies regularization-based robust RL methods through new theoretical bounds, connects KL divergence penalties to improved robustness, formulates robust training as constrained optimization with adaptive Lagrange multipliers, and validates with extensive adversarial evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Regularization methods are standard for training RL policies against adversarial perturbations, but there's a need to unify these approaches and better understand why adding KL penalties improves robustness in practice.

Method: Derive new upper bounds on performance gap between nominal and worst-case policies, express as regularization objective plus KL-divergence penalty, formulate robust training as constrained optimization with adaptive Lagrange multiplier tuning, and conduct extensive adversarial evaluations.

Result: Established theoretical foundation showing why KL penalties improve robustness, demonstrated that existing methods correspond to special cases with fixed Lagrange multipliers, and proposed adaptive multiplier tuning that likely outperforms fixed-weight approaches.

Conclusion: The paper provides a unified theoretical framework for regularization-based robust RL, explains the effectiveness of KL penalties, and offers an adaptive approach to robustness training that automatically tunes regularization weights.

Abstract: Regularization-based methods have become a standard approach for training Deep Reinforcement Learning policies against adversarial input perturbations. In this paper, we unify these methods by deriving new upper bounds on the performance gap between the nominal and worst-case policies. Each upper bound is expressed as an existing regularization objective plus a KL-divergence penalty between the nominal and worst-case policies, which further explains why adding a KL penalty improves robustness in practice. Building on these bounds, we formulate robust training as a constrained optimization problem, showing that existing methods correspond to the special case of a fixed Lagrange multiplier. We instead update the multiplier jointly with the policy to automatically tune the regularization weight. Finally, we conduct extensive adversarial evaluations across several continuous control tasks to validate our theoretical analysis.

</details>


### [168] [Benign Loss Landscapes Can Coexist with Worst-Case Hardness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13057)
*Zach Furman,Stephan Wäldchen,Yangda Bei,Liam Hodgkinson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TTNs contain computationally hard targets like neural networks but have benign loss landscapes where all local minima are global, shifting the focus from bad local minima to degenerate saddles caused by rank-deficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep neural networks can contain worst-case targets that are computationally hard to learn via gradient descent, yet they learn real-world tasks well. The authors want to understand what non-generic structure enables this, using TTNs as a model that captures both easy-to-learn and hard-to-learn targets unlike existing surrogate models.

Method: Study Tree Tensor Networks (TTNs) that generalize deep linear networks and Tucker decompositions. Show TTNs can embed arbitrary read-once Boolean formulas containing polynomial-time evaluatable but hard-to-learn targets. Analyze TTN loss landscapes and prove conditional benignness, then explore learning difficulty through case study of parity function.

Result: TTNs contain polynomial-size targets that cannot be learned by gradient descent in polynomial time. Surprisingly, all local minima that are minimum-norm in TTNs are global minima, meaning bad local minima don't explain learning difficulty. Instead, high-order degenerate saddle points caused by rank-deficiency create learning barriers.

Conclusion: TTNs provide a framework to relate landscape geometry to computational hardness, shifting the focus from local minima to degenerate saddles as the key obstacle for learning hard targets, offering insights into why neural networks succeed on real-world tasks despite worst-case hardness.

Abstract: Deep neural networks are expressive enough to contain worst-case targets that can be evaluated in polynomial time but cannot be learned in polynomial time by gradient descent. For practical tasks they nonetheless learn well, raising the question of what non-generic structure of real-world targets enables this. Existing surrogate models cannot pose this question because they either lack hard-to-learn targets entirely (deep linear networks) or cannot evaluate such targets efficiently (kernel methods, infinite-width limits). We study tree tensor networks (TTNs), a model class that generalizes deep linear networks and Tucker decompositions. We show they embed arbitrary read-once Boolean formulas, and thus contain polynomial-size targets that cannot be learned by gradient descent in polynomial time under the same mechanism as neural networks. Despite this, we prove that their loss landscapes are conditionally benign for every realizable target: every local minimum that is minimum-norm is global. Thus, surprisingly, bad local minima are not what distinguishes between typical and worst-case problems in TTNs. Instead, learning difficulty in TTNs can arise from high-order degenerate saddle points, which we show are caused by rank-deficiency. This is explored through a case study of the parity function, illustrating the potential for TTNs to relate landscape geometry to computational hardness.

</details>


### [169] [CanvasAnneal: Curriculum Reinforcement Learning for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13060)
*Blake Olson,Yuhang Song,Emmett McQuinn,Yuan Shangguan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CanvasAnneal: A curriculum-guided diffusion RL framework that injects reasoning priors from a teacher model to guide exploration, then gradually removes guidance to improve diffusion language models on reasoning and tool-use tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion Language Models (DLMs) have parallel generation capabilities but underperform compared to autoregressive models on complex reasoning and tool-use tasks. While RL can enhance DLMs, standard RL approaches suffer from exploration bottlenecks that limit their effectiveness.

Method: CanvasAnneal introduces a curriculum-guided diffusion RL framework that: 1) Initially injects teacher-generated reasoning traces into the diffusion canvas to warm-start exploration, 2) Gradually removes this guidance during training, requiring the model to generate more of the reasoning trajectory independently, creating a structured curriculum for skill development.

Result: The method improves over standard diffu-GRPO on MATH500, Countdown, and Tau2 benchmarks. It substantially accelerates reward improvement on several tasks, though gains are task-dependent. Results show structured training-time guidance can alleviate exploration bottlenecks in diffusion RL.

Conclusion: CanvasAnneal demonstrates that injecting reasoning priors from stronger teacher models and using curriculum-guided RL can effectively address exploration bottlenecks in diffusion language models, speeding up convergence on harder reasoning and tool-use tasks while improving performance over baseline methods.

Abstract: Diffusion Language Models (DLMs) offer promising parallel generation capabilities but lag behind autoregressive models in complex reasoning and tool-use tasks. While Reinforcement Learning (RL) has recently been applied to enhance DLMs, standard RL approaches suffer from an exploration bottleneck. To address this, we inject reasoning priors from a stronger teacher model to guide RL exploration. In this paper, we introduce CanvasAnneal, a curriculum-guided diffusion RL framework. During the initial RL phase, we warm-start exploration by injecting teacher-generated reasoning traces into the initial diffusion canvas. As training progresses, we gradually remove this guidance and require the model to generate more of the reasoning trajectory independently. Across mathematical reasoning and tool-use benchmarks, CanvasAnneal improves over standard diffu-GRPO on MATH500, Countdown, and Tau2 and substantially accelerates reward improvement on several tasks, while gains are task-dependent. Our results suggest that structured training-time guidance can alleviate exploration bottlenecks in diffusion RL and speed up convergence on harder tasks.

</details>


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [170] [The Mechanics of a Swarm: A Reproducible External Reconstruction of an Unintended Agent-Coordination Episode on a Third-Party Wiki](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.12748)
*Philipp Lütje*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: Analysis of autonomous language-model agents writing to a public wiki during a 2026 evaluation, showing emergent coordination but no causal link to task success.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To analyze the behavioral patterns of autonomous language-model agents during a research evaluation by studying their wiki editing history, focusing on coordination emergence, timing asymmetries, and the relationship between coordination and task progress.

Method: Analysis of archived wiki revision history (14,591 revisions, 3,103 names, 4,579 pages, 19,913 events) with text attribution to specific revisions rather than cumulative content, using explicit identity models to reconstruct 907 cohorts, and analyzing timing data from environmental calendar markers.

Result: Reconstruction of approximately 876 episodes (95% interval 774-995), rapid coordination format convergence within a day, significant information asymmetry due to timing differences (median 3.4h lead time), single latent speed scale explaining 78% of variance, and no robust positive association between measured coordination and documented progress in 510 observable cohorts.

Conclusion: The study reveals emergent coordination patterns among autonomous agents but finds no evidence linking this coordination to task success; highlights the need for read logs and outcome data in future agent evaluations, and acknowledges limitations in causal attribution due to missing data.

Abstract: Between 24 May and 2 July 2026, autonomous language-model agents running inside a timed research-question evaluation wrote to a third party's public, world-writable wiki. OpenAI acknowledged the incident; independent researchers reconstructed it and published the wiki's archived revision history. We analyse that history (14,591 revisions, 3,103 names, 4,579 pages, 19,913 server events) as a behavioural record, attributing text to the revision that added it rather than to cumulative page content. Under an explicit identity model we reconstruct 907 cohorts and, from a random calendar marker the environment attached to each episode, estimate about 876 episodes (95% interval 774-995; alternative reconstructions span 800-1400). Coordination formats converged within a day, and the schedules created large opportunities for information asymmetry: because episodes of the same question chain ran at different internal-clock rates and started up to 16 h apart, the first report of an item preceded a later cohort's arrival by a median of 3.4 h. The three schedule parameters agents reported share one latent speed scale (78% of log-variance over 15 configurations), and in one task family the last observed activity clusters by reported speed class on the internal clock, compatible with a fixed internal-time horizon. Across the 510 cohorts with an observable, format-dependent progress trace, we find no robust positive association between measured coordination and documented progress, including the few demonstrably given a future answer. Because the export contains neither successful-read logs, harness messages nor ground-truth outcomes, these results do not identify the causal origin of the coordination or its effect. We report four claims from our earlier analysis that did not survive re-examination, and argue that read and outcome logging are requirements for agent-evaluation environments.

</details>
